                Architecture During the Delhi Sultanate (1206-1526 AD)
                    During the Delhi Sultanate, architecture evolved under two broad styles:
                        - Imperial Style of Architecture
                            The Imperial Style flourished under different dynasties, each contributing unique features while maintaining a consistent architectural approach.
                                - Slave Dynasty (Ilbari Dynasty) (1206-1290 AD) – Mameluke Style
                                    •	Early constructions were remodeled Hindu structures.
                                    •	Qutub Minar was a significant architectural achievement.    
                                    Examples:
                                        Qutub Minar:
                                            •	Initiated by Qutb-ud-din Aibak (constructed only the ground floor).
                                            •	Completed by Iltutmish (added three storeys).
                                            •	Fifth storey added by Feroze Shah Tughlaq.
                                        •	Quwwat-ul-Islam Mosque (converted from a Jain temple).
                                        •	Arhai-din-ka-Jhopra (Ajmer).
                                - Khilji Dynasty (1290-1320 AD) – Seljuk Style
                                    •	Extensive use of red sandstone.
                                    •	Arcuate style became prominent.
                                    •	Mortar used as a cementing agent.
                                    Examples:
                                        •	Alai Darwaza (by Ala-ud-din Khilji).
                                        •	Siri Fort.
                                - Tughlaq Dynasty (1320-1414 AD) – Strength Over Decoration
                                    •	Use of grey sandstone.
                                    •	Focus on structural strength rather than decoration.
                                    •	Combination of arch and lintel entrance designs.
                                    •	Batter technique introduced (sloping walls for extra strength).
                                    Examples:
                                        •	Cities of Tughlaqabad, Jahanpanah, and Ferozabad.
                                - Lodi Dynasty (1451-1526 AD) – Introduction of Double Domes
                                    •	Only tombs were built.
                                    •	Double domes introduced to:
                                    •	Increase structural strength.
                                    •	Reduce the inner height of the dome.
                                    •	Tombs were octagonal, plain, and supported by sloping verandahs.
                                    Examples:
                                        •	Lodi Gardens.
                                        •	City of Agra (established by Sikandar Lodi).
                        - Provincial Style of Architecture
                            During the Delhi Sultanate, Indo-Islamic influences blended with local traditions, leading to distinct regional architectural styles in Bengal, Jaunpur, Malwa, and Bijapur.
                            Provinicial Schools of Architecture
                                - Bengal School of Architecture (1203–1573 AD)
                                    •	Use of bricks and black marble.
                                    •	Retained traditional Bangla-style sloping roofs (previously used in temples).
                                    Examples:
                                        •	Qadam Rasul Mosque (Gour).
                                        •	Adina Mosque (Pandua).
                                - Jaunpur School of Architecture (1394–1479 AD) – Sharqi Style
                                    •	Patronized by the Sharqi rulers.
                                    •	No minarets, following Pathan-style preferences.
                                    •	Bold inscriptions on huge screens in prayer halls.
                                    Example:
                                        •	Atala Mosque (Jaunpur).
                                - Malwa School of Architecture (1405-1569 AD) – Pathan School
                                    •	Dhar and Mandu became major architectural hubs.
                                    •	Extensive use of colored stones and marbles.
                                    •	European influence seen in large windows.
                                    •	Stylized arches and pillars for decoration.
                                    •	Minarets were not used.
                                    Environmental Adaptations:
                                        •	Large windows ensured ventilation.
                                        •	Lightly arched pavilions kept interiors cool.
                                        •	Artificial reservoirs (‘baulis’) for water storage.
                                        •	Locally available materials were used.
                                        •	Batter system (introduced by Tughlaqs) strengthened buildings.
                                    Examples:
                                        •	Rani Roopmati Pavilion.
                                        •	Jahaz Mahal.
                                        •	Ashrafi Mahal.
                                - Bijapur School of Architecture (1490-1656 AD) – Deccan Style
                                        •	Developed under the Adil Shah rulers.
                                        •	Introduced 3-arched facades, bulbous domes, and narrow-neck domes.
                                        •	Cornices used for embellishment.
                                        •	Ceilings had no apparent support (held by iron clamps and mortar plaster).
                                        •	Walls richly decorated with carvings.
                                        Example:
                                            •	Gol Gumbaz (mausoleum of Adil Shah, Bijapur).
                                        Whispering Gallery
                                            •	Built to amplify even whispers across the gallery.
                                            •	Examples:
                                                - Gol Gumbaz.
                                                - Victoria Memorial.
                                                - Golghar Granary (Bandipore).

            Mughal Architecture
                The Mughals were great patrons of art and architecture. Under their rule, architecture regained prominence, marked by grand visions and artistic inspiration.
                - Early Mughal Architecture
                    Babur (1526–1530)
                        •	Babur constructed mosques in Panipat and Rohilkhand in 1526 AD.
                        •	His reign was too short-lived to establish a distinct architectural style.
                    Humayun (1530–1540, 1555–1556)
                        •	His reign was marked by conflicts, preventing much architectural development.
                        •	Initiated the construction of Dinpanah, but it remained incomplete.
                        •	Persian influence became prominent during this period.
                    Sher Shah Suri (1540–1545) (Interregnum period)
                        •	His reign marked a transition from Lodi to Mughal architecture.
                        •	Key constructions:
                            •	Qila-e-Kuhna Mosque (Old Fort, Delhi).
                            •	Rohtas Fort (Pakistan).
                            •	Sher Shah Suri Masjid (Patna, Afghan style).
                        •	Grand Trunk Road (Sadak-e-Azam): Extended from Bengal to Punjab with sarais and tree-lined roads for travelers.
                        •	Sher Shah Suri’s Tomb (Sasaram):
                            •	Built with red sandstone, situated in a lake.
                            •	Continued Delhi Sultanate architectural traditions.
                    With Akbar’s accession in 1556, the golden period of Mughal architecture began.
                - Akbar’s Architecture (1556–1605)
                    Akbar took a keen interest in architecture, favoring red sandstone and introducing the Tudor arch (four-centered arch).
                    Key Constructions
                        - Agra Fort
                            •	One of the first constructions under Akbar.
                            •	Most existing structures were later built by Shah Jahan.
                            •	Prominent buildings:
                                •	Moti Masjid (Shah Jahan).
                                •	Diwan-i-Aam & Diwan-i-Khas (Shah Jahan).
                                •	Jahangiri Mahal.
                                •	Sheesh Mahal (Turkish Bath).
                            •	Charbagh-style gardens were introduced.
                            •	Akbar’s harem housed over 5000 women inside the fort.
                        - Fatehpur Sikri (Akbar’s capital city, a blend of Hindu and Persian styles)
                            •	Notable structures:
                                •	Buland Darwaza (1576 AD): 40m high, commemorating victory over Gujarat.
                                •	Salim Chishti’s Tomb (1581 AD): White marble, intricate jaali work, Quranic inscriptions.
                                •	Panch Mahal: Five-storied wind-catcher structure (Persian badgir concept).
                                •	Jodha Bai’s Palace (Mariam-uz-Zamani’s Palace): Hindu motifs of bell and flowers.
                                •	Ibadat Khana: Religious debates among scholars.
                                •	Pachisi Court: Akbar played chess here.
                                •	Hiran Minar: Built for Akbar’s favorite elephant, doubled as a lighthouse.
                        •	Akbar also built Govind Dev Temple in Vrindavan.
                - Jahangir’s Architecture (1605–1627)
                    Jahangir focused more on paintings than architecture, but notable constructions include:
                        •	Akbar’s Tomb (Sikandra).
                        •	Shalimar Bagh (Kashmir).
                        •	Moti Masjid (Lahore).
                        •	Tomb of Itmad-ud-Daulah (Father of Noor Jahan):
                            •	First Mughal structure built entirely in white marble.
                            •	Finest pietra dura (inlay work).
                        •	Tomb of Jahangir (built by Shah Jahan in Lahore).
                - Shah Jahan’s Architecture (1628–1658)
                    Mughal architecture reached its climax under Shah Jahan.
                    Key Constructions
                        - Taj Mahal (1632–1653, Agra)
                            •	Built in memory of Mumtaz Mahal (Arjumand Banu Begum).
                            •	Features:
                                •	Calligraphy, pietra dura inlay work, foreshortening technique, Charbagh gardens, water features.
                                •	Jaali work: Extremely fine, lace-like.
                                •	Low relief carvings on marble.
                        - Other Constructions
                            •	Red Fort (Delhi).
                            •	Jama Masjid (Delhi).
                            •	Shalimar Bagh (Lahore).
                            •	City of Shahjahanabad (Delhi).
                            •	Peacock Throne: Finest example of Mughal metalwork.
                - Aurangzeb’s Architecture (1658–1707)
                    Mughal architecture declined under Aurangzeb due to his orthodox policies.
                        •	Commissioned Bibi-ka-Maqbara (Aurangabad) in memory of Begum Rabia-ud-Daurani.
                            •	Often called a poor imitation of the Taj Mahal.
                - Regional Architectural Styles (Developed during the Mughal Period)
                    - Rajput Architecture (Influenced by Mughal style, but distinct in scale and grandeur)
                        •	Features:
                            •	Jharokha (hanging balconies).
                            •	Arched cornices creating bow-shaped shadows.
                    - Sikh Architecture (Developed in Punjab, combining Mughal and Rajput styles)
                        •	Features:
                            •	Multiple chhatris (kiosks) on rooftops.
                            •	Onion domes, shallow cornices, fluted domes covered with brass/copper gilding.
                            •	Decorated arches with multiple foliations.
                        •	Example: Harmandir Sahib (Golden Temple, 16th–17th century).
                    - Awadh Architecture (18th–19th Century, Lucknow)
                        •	Built by Nawabs of Awadh to preserve Mughal traditions.
                        •	Experimented with new materials (mortar mix of brick dust, urad dal, rice husk, tree gum).
                        •	Prominent structures:
                            - Bara Imambara (1784): Includes Asfi mosque, Bhul-bhulaiya (labyrinth), and step well.
                            - Chota Imambara (1838): Mausoleum of Nawab Muhammad Ali Shah & mother.
                            - Rumi Darwaza (Turkish Gate, 1784): Named after Sufi saint Jalaluddin Rumi.
                Conclusion
                    Mughal architecture evolved from early Indo-Islamic influences to a distinct style blending Persian, Central Asian, and Indian elements. 
                    It reached its peak under Shah Jahan but declined under Aurangzeb, giving rise to regional styles in Rajasthan, Punjab, and Awadh.

            Architecture in Kashmir
                Development of Kashmiri architecture can be broadly divided into two important phases of its political rule – early medieval Hindu phase and 14th century onwards Muslim rule. 
                    No major monuments that were made prior to 600 AD exist, except for a few Buddhist monuments like monasteries and stupas (now in ruins) discovered at Harwan and Ushkar.
                - Temples in Kashmir
                    Kashmiri temple architecture has unique features suited to the local geography and is renowned for its exquisite stone carvings. 
                    Due to its location on important trade routes, the architectural style is influenced by various foreign sources. 
                    Temple-making reached great heights under the rulers of the Karkota Dynasty and Utpala Dynasty.
                    - Main Features of Kashmiri Temple Architecture
                        •	Trefoil arches (influenced by Gandhara style)
                        •	Cellular layout and enclosed courtyard
                        •	Straight-edged pyramidal roofs
                        •	Column walls (Greek influence)
                        •	Triangular pediments (Greek influence)
                        •	Relatively more number of steps
                    - Important Temples
                        - Martand Sun Temple
                            •	Location: Anantnag, Kashmir
                            •	Built: 8th century AD under Karkota Dynasty ruler Lalitaditya Muktapida
                            •	Features:
                                •	A synthesis of Gandhara, Chinese, and Gupta architectural influences
                                •	Constructed in a courtyard layout surrounded by columns
                                •	Main temple has a pyramidal roof
                                •	Carvings of Vishnu, River Goddesses (Ganga & Yamuna), and Sun God
                        - Temples at Awantipora
                            •	Built by: King Avantivarman (first ruler of Utpala Dynasty)
                            •	Built: 9th century AD
                            •	Features:
                                •	Two temples:
                                    •	Avantiswami Temple (dedicated to Lord Vishnu)
                                    •	Avantisvara Temple (dedicated to Lord Shiva)
                                •	Situated inside a paved courtyard with four shrines in its corners
                                •	Roman and Gandharan influences clearly visible
                                •	Gateway has two chambers with intricate carvings
                        - Pandrethan Temple
                            •	Also known as Meruvardhana-swami
                            •	Dedicated to Vishnu, but also has Shiva images
                            •	Built: Early 10th century AD (near Srinagar)
                            •	Features:
                                •	Carved from a single block of stone
                                •	Exquisite wall carvings
                                •	Domed roof and arches
                        - Mamleshwar Shiva Temple
                            •	Location: Pahalgam
                            •	Built: Around 400 AD
                            •	Mentioned in Kalhana’s Rajatarangini
                        - Monuments in Paraspore
                            •	Location: Modern-day Paraspore
                            •	Built by: Lalitaditya Muktapida (who made Parihaspora his capital)
                            •	Features:
                                •	Temples dedicated to Vishnu and Parihaskesana
                                •	Also had Buddhist structures

                - Architectural Development under Islamic Rule
                    With the establishment of Muslim rule, Kashmiri architecture saw a fusion of existing Hindu-Kashmiri styles with Islamic influences. 
                        The monuments were mostly square in shape, made of wood, brick, and stone masonry. 
                    Key architectural elements included:
                        •	Absence of cloisters and domes
                        •	Multi-levelled sloping roofs
                        •	Tall spires
                    Important Islamic Monuments
                        - Jamia Masjid, Srinagar
                            •	Built: 14th–15th century AD
                            •	Features:
                                •	Large courtyard
                                •	370 wooden pillars
                                •	Absence of curved dome, following Kashmiri architectural traditions
                        - Aali Masjid, Srinagar
                            •	Built by: Shahmiri king Sultan Hassan Shah (15th century AD)
                            •	Second-largest mosque in Kashmir Valley
                            •	Features:
                                •	Absence of dome, using timber and stone construction
                                •	Pyramidal roof with arch-shaped windows
                        - Pari Mahal
                            •	Built by: Mughal prince Dara Shikoh (mid-17th century AD)
                            •	Features:
                                •	Originally an observatory
                                •	Terraced structure on a hill overlooking Dal Lake
                                •	Located near Chashme Shahi Garden
                - Gardens in Kashmir
                    The Mughal rulers built numerous gardens inspired by Persian designs, using the Charbagh style. These gardens feature:
                        •	Water streams, fountains, and trees as essential elements
                        •	Terraced layouts with distinct sections
                    Prominent Mughal Gardens in Kashmir
                        - Chashme Shahi
                            •	Built around a fresh spring in Persian style
                        - Shalimar Bagh
                            •	Built by: Jahangir (1619 AD)
                            •	Served as Jahangir’s royal summer residence
                            •	Three terraces:
                            •	Diwan-e-Aam (Public Audience Hall)
                            •	Diwan-e-Khas (Private Audience Hall)
                            •	Zenana Garden (for royal women)
                        - Nishat Bagh
                            •	Largest Mughal garden in Kashmir
                            •	Located near Dal Lake
                            •	Has 12 terraces
                        - Other Important Gardens
                            •	Badamwari Garden (Srinagar)
                            •	Verinag, Kokernag, and Achabal Gardens (Anantnag)

        - Additional important information
            FAMOUS UNIVERSITIES OF ANCIENT INDIA
                - Nalanda (Bihar)
                    •	Most renowned university in South Asia.
                    •	Existed during the Gupta period, flourished under Harshavardhana and Pala kings.
                    •	Major centre for Mahayana Buddhist teachings, but all three Buddhist doctrines were taught.
                    •	Subjects: Vedas, fine arts, grammar, philosophy, logic, medicine, etc.
                    •	Attracted scholars from Central Asia, South-East Asia, and beyond.
                    •	Famous scholars: Nagarjuna (Madhyamika Shunyavad), Aryabhatta (astronomer).
                    •	Chinese scholars: Hsuan Tsang (spent 2 years), I-Tsing (spent 10 years).
                    •	Had eight separate compounds and dormitories for students.
                    •	Deeply influenced Tibetan Buddhism.
                - Vikramshila (Bihar - Bhagalpur district)
                    •	Established by King Dharampala of the Pala Dynasty.
                    •	Primarily a Buddhist learning centre; Vajrayana sect flourished here.
                    •	Tantric teachings, logic, Vedas, astronomy, law, grammar, philosophy, etc. were taught.
                    •	Scholars were invited by foreign rulers to spread Buddhist teachings.
                - Odantapuri (Bihar)
                    •	Built by Pala King Gopala I as a Buddhist Mahavihara.
                    •	Destroyed by Bakhtiyar Khilji.
                - Jaggadala (Bengal)
                    •	Centre for Vajrayana Buddhism, established by King Ramapala (Pala dynasty).
                    •	Provided refuge to scholars after the decline of Nalanda and Vikramshila.
                - Takshashila (Modern-day Pakistan)
                    •	Existed since 5th century BC.
                    •	Both Buddhist and Hindu theologies were taught.
                    •	Subjects: Political science, hunting, medicine, law, military tactics, etc.
                    •	Noted scholars: Chanakya (Arthashastra), Charak, Panini, Jivaka, Prasenajit.
                - Valabhi (Saurashtra, Gujarat)
                    •	Major centre for Hinayana Buddhism.
                    •	Subjects: Administration, statecraft, laws, philosophy, etc.
                    •	Chinese scholar Hsuan Tsang visited.
                    •	Supported by Maitraka Dynasty rulers.
                - Kancheepuram (Tamil Nadu)
                    •	A centre of Hinduism, Jainism, and Buddhism since 1st century AD.
                    •	Gained prominence under Pallava rule.
                - Manyakheta (Karnataka - Malkhed)
                    •	Rose to prominence under Rashtrakuta rule.
                    •	Scholars of Jainism, Buddhism, and Hinduism studied here.
                    •	Home to a Dvaita school of thought Matha.
                - Pushpagiri Vihara & Lalitagiri (Odisha)
                    •	Established by Kalinga kings around 3rd century AD.
                    •	Primarily a Buddhist learning centre.
                - Sharada Peeth (Pakistan-occupied Kashmir)
                    •	Important centre for Sanskrit scholars.
                    •	Many significant texts were written here.
                    •	Also has a Sharda Devi temple.
                - Nagarjunakonda (Andhra Pradesh - 160 km from Amaravathi)
                    •	Major Buddhist centre, attracted scholars from Sri Lanka, China, etc.
                    •	Had many Viharas and Stupas.
                    •	Named after Nagarjuna, a scholar of Mahayana Buddhism.

            Prominent Temples Outside India
                - Angkor Wat (Cambodia)
                    •	World’s largest Hindu temple, located in Cambodia.
                    •	Built between 800-1300 AD by Khmer rulers, mainly Suryavarman II.
                    •	Closely related to Chola architecture, but with local influences.
                    •	Hinduism spread to South-East Asia due to Chola and Kalinga Empire’s sea-faring activities, leading to Hindu dynasties like Champa (Vietnam), Khmer (Cambodia), and Majapahit (Java).
                - Prambanan Temple (Java, Indonesia)
                    •	Largest Hindu temple in Indonesia, a UNESCO World Heritage Site.
                    •	Dedicated to Trimurti – Brahma, Vishnu, and Shiva.
                    •	Built in the 9th century AD by Sanjaya kings of the Mataram/Medang Kingdom.
                    •	Features 240 small temples and shrines within its complex.
                - Pashupatinath Temple (Kathmandu, Nepal)
                    •	Built in Nepalese Pagoda style, located on the banks of River Bagmati.
                    •	Dedicated to Lord Pashupatinath (Shiva).
                    •	Cubical structure with two-tiered roofs and two Garbhagrihas (Inner & Outer).
                    •	Inner sanctum houses a four-faced Shiva Linga.
                    •	Includes Vasukinath Temple and sacred Arya Ghat.
                    •	Exact builder is unknown.
                - Preah Vihear Temple (Cambodia)
                    •	Dedicated to Sikharesvara and Bhadresvara (forms of Lord Shiva).
                    •	Symbolically represents Mount Meru, the abode of gods.
                    •	Built mainly by Khmer Kings Suryavarman I & II (11th-12th century AD).
                    •	UNESCO World Heritage Site.
                    •	Subject of a border dispute between Thailand and Cambodia, resolved by the ICJ in favor of Cambodia (1962).
                - Katas Raj Temple (Pakistan)
                    •	Ancient Shiva temple, made of soft sandstone with trefoil arches, dentils, and pointed roofs.
                    •	Believed to be visited by Pandavas during exile.
                    •	Mentioned in the travelogues of Hiuen Tsang (7th century).
                    •	Complex includes Satgraha (seven temples), a sacred lake, and ruins of a Buddhist stupa.
                - Aditya Sun Temple (Multan, Pakistan)
                    •	One of the oldest and most revered Sun temples of South Asia.
                    •	Mentioned by Herodotus (5th century), Hiuen Tsang (7th century), and Al-Istakhri (10th century).
                    •	Famous for its gold idol of the Sun god with ruby eyes.
                    •	Frequently raided, notably by Mahmud of Ghazni.
                - Munneswaram Temple (Sri Lanka)
                    •	Over 1000 years old, reconstructed in the 16th century by Sri Lankan kings.
                    •	Destroyed by Portuguese Jesuits in early 16th century and later rebuilt.
                    •	Mainly dedicated to Lord Shiva, with shrines of Ganesha, Kali, and Ayyanayake (Sinhalese Buddhist deity).

            Major Buddhist Pilgrimage Sites in India
                - Bihar
                    •	Mahabodhi Temple, Bodh Gaya
                    •	Mahaviharas: Nalanda, Vikramshila, Somapura, Odantapuri, Pushpagiri, Jagaddala
                - Uttar Pradesh
                    •	Sarnath (near Varanasi) – Site of Buddha’s first sermon
                    •	Kushinagar
                    •	Piprahwa (on the Nepal border)
                - Madhya Pradesh
                    •	Sanchi
                    •	Bharhut
                - Odisha
                    •	Lalitagiri, Vajragiri, and Ratnagiri
                    •	Rock-cut elephant and Ashokan edicts at Dhauli (Mauryan period)
                - Chhattisgarh
                    •	Sirpur
                - Tamil Nadu
                    •	Nagapattinam
                - Maharashtra
                    •	Bhaja and Karla Caves (Pune)
                    •	Ajanta, Ellora, and Pithalkora Caves (Aurangabad)
                    •	Kanheri Caves (Mumbai)
                    •	Pandavleni Caves (Nashik)
                - West Bengal
                    •	Ghum Monastery
                - Sikkim
                    •	Rumtek Monastery
                    •	Pemayangtse Monastery
                    •	Enchay Monastery
                - Ladakh
                    •	Alchi Monastery
                    •	Spituk Monastery
                    •	Shey Monastery
                - Himachal Pradesh
                    •	Dhankar Monastery
                    •	Nako Monastery (Kinnaur)
                    •	Kye Monastery
                    •	Tabo Monastery (Spiti Valley)
                - Arunachal Pradesh
                    •	Tawang Monastery (Largest monastery in India)
                    •	Bomdila Monastery
                - Karnataka
                    •	Namdroling Monastery, Bylakuppe

            Major Jain Pilgrimage Sites in India
                - Gujarat
                    •	Palitana Temples, Shatrunjay Hills (Kathiawar) – Holiest site for the Śvētāmbara sect, dedicated to Rishabha (1st Tirthankara), with over 800 marble temples.
                    •	Girnar Temples, Junagadh – Houses 16 temples, the largest dedicated to Neminath (22nd Tirthankara).
                    •	Akota (Vadodara) – Site of discovered Jain bronze statues.
                    •	Hathee Singh Jain Temple, Ahmedabad.
                    •	Terminology: Jain temples in Gujarat and southern Rajasthan are called Derasar.
                - Rajasthan
                    •	Dilwara Temples, Mount Abu – Five intricately carved marble temples:
                    •	Vimal Vasahi (built by Vimal Shah, 11th century AD)
                    •	Luna Vasahi, Pittalhar, Parshvanath, and Mahavir Swami (13th–17th century).
                    •	Ranakpur Temples, Pali District – Built in the 15th century, with over 1,400 uniquely carved pillars, showing Hoysala architectural influence rather than Nagara style.
                    •	Nasiyan Temple (Soniji Ki Nasiyan), Ajmer – Built in the 19th century, dedicated to Rishabhdev (1st Tirthankara).
                - Bihar
                    •	Pawapuri, Nalanda – Site of Mahavira’s (24th Tirthankara) cremation.
                    •	Chausa – Site of discovered Jain bronze statues.
                - Jharkhand
                    •	Shikharji (Parasnath Hills) – Holiest Jain pilgrimage site where 20 Tirthankaras attained Moksha.
                - Madhya Pradesh
                    •	Bawangaja, Barwani District – Home to an 84 ft statue of Lord Adinath, carved from a single rock.
                    •	Various Jain temples at Gwalior, Chanderi, and Khajuraho.
                - Karnataka
                    •	Shravanabelagola – Houses the Gomateshwara statue of Bahubali, built in the 10th century AD by Chamundaraya (minister of the Ganga dynasty). Contains several Basadis (Jain temples).
                    •	Namdroling Monastery, Bylakuppe.
                - Uttar Pradesh
                    •	Shantinath Temple Complex, Deogarh (Lalitpur, Bundelkhand) – Houses 31 temples with exquisite iconography.
                    •	Kankali Tila, near Mathura – Discovery of Ayagapata votive tablets (1st century BCE–3rd century CE) used in Jain worship, featuring symbols like stupa, dharmachakra, and triratna.
                - Odisha
                    •	Udayagiri and Khandagiri Caves – Dating back to 2nd–1st century BC, carved during the reign of King Kharavela.
                    •	Famous caves: Hathi Gumpha, Rani Gumpha, Ganesh Gumpha.
                    •	Rich carvings of Jain Tirthankaras and inscriptions, including the Hathigumpha inscription, which details King Kharavela’s conquests.
                - Haryana
                    •	Hansi (Hisar) – Site of discovered Jain bronze statues.
                - Tamil Nadu
                    •	Sittanavasal Caves – Ancient Jain rock-cut temple.
                - Maharashtra
                    •	Mangi-Tungi (near Tahrabad) – A twin-pinnacled peak dedicated to Jainism, considered a gateway to enlightenment.
                        •	Contains 6th-century images of Tirthankaras in Padmasana and Kayotsarga postures.
                        •	In 2016, the 108 ft “Statue of Ahimsa” was consecrated, making it the tallest Jain idol in the world (Guinness World Record).
                    •	Terminology: Jain temples in Karnataka are called Basadis.

            Temples of the Parsi Community in India
                The Parsi faith has three major types of fire temples:
                    - Atash Behram (“Fire of Victory”) – the highest grade of fire temple.
                        •	The exterior is generally kept simple, as the focus is on holding the sacred fire and performing the Yasna ceremony (prayers) rather than glorification of the faith.
                        •	The temple has an inner sanctum where the fire is kept, with vents for smoke escape.
                        •	The ceremonies performed are of the highest order, requiring elaborate arrangements.
                        •	They are conducted by high priests called Dasturs.
                        •	Atash Behram fire is the highest grade of sacred fire, created by gathering 16 different types of fire.
                        Eight Atash Behrams (Fire Temples) in India
                            - Iranshah Atash Behram – Udvada, Gujarat (built in the 18th century).
                            - Bhagarsath Anjuman Atash Behram – Navsari, Gujarat (built in the 18th century).
                            - Dadiseth Atash Behram – Mumbai.
                            - Wadia Atash Behram – Mumbai.
                            - Banaji Atash Behram – Mumbai.
                            - Anjuman Atash Behram – Mumbai.
                            - Modi Atash Behram – Surat (built in the 19th century).
                            - Vakil Atash Behram – Surat (built in the 19th century).
                    - Atash Adaran – the second-highest grade.
                    - Atash Dadgah – the most common, with over 100 Dadgahs in India, mostly in Maharashtra and Gujarat.      
            
            Sun Temples in India
                - Basic information
                    •	The Sun has been worshipped since the Vedic age.
                    •	It is revered as Aditya or Surya in Hinduism.
                    •	Many rituals and temples are dedicated to the Sun.
                    •	Sun temples also exist in Japan, Egypt, and China.
                    •	Suryavanshi Rajputs claim descent from the Sun God.
                - Major Sun Temples in India
                    - Modhera Sun Temple, Gujarat
                        •	Built in the 11th century.
                    - Konark Sun Temple, Odisha
                        •	Built by Narasimhadeva I in the 13th century.
                        •	Shaped like a chariot (Rath) with a raised mandapa.
                    - Brahmanya Dev Temple, Unao (Madhya Pradesh)
                    - Suryanaar Kovil, Kumbakonam (Tamil Nadu)
                        •	Built in the 11th century in Dravidian style.
                        •	Dedicated to the Sun God and Navagrahas.
                        •	Features a five-layered Gopuram.
                    - Suryanarayana Swamy Temple, Arasavalli (Andhra Pradesh)
                        •	Built by a Kalinga king in the 7th century.
                        •	Granite idol holding a lotus.
                    - Dakshinaarka Temple, Gaya (Bihar)
                        •	Built by King Prataparudra of Warangal in the 13th century.
                        •	The deity wears Persian attire (waist girdle, boots, and jacket).
                        •	Has a Surya Kund (water reservoir) nearby.
                    - Navalakha Temple, Ghumli (Gujarat)
                        •	Built in the 11th century in Solanki and Maru-Gurjara styles.
                        •	Faces east, built on a large platform.
                    - Surya Pahar Temple, Goalpara (Assam)
                    - Martand Sun Temple, Kashmir

        Modern Architecture
            - European Influence on Indian Architecture
                •	The decline of the Mughal Empire coincided with the arrival of European colonists.
                •	A power struggle ensued among the Portuguese, French, Dutch, Danish, and British.
                •	Eventually, British rule was established until 1947.
                •	The Europeans introduced new architectural styles, which can be seen in their constructions.
            - Portuguese Influence
                •	Introduced the Iberian style of architecture.
                •	Initially built trading posts and warehouses, later fortified towns along the coast.
                •	Introduced Patio houses and the Baroque style (late 16th century).
                •	Baroque architecture was elaborate, detailed, and theatrical, using contrasting colors to create a dramatic effect.
                Notable Constructions
                    •	Sé Cathedral, Goa (Completed in 1619 AD).
                        •	Built in Portuguese late-Gothic style.
                        •	Houses the Golden Bell.
                    •	Basilica of Bom Jesus, Goa (Completed in 1604 AD).
                        •	Built in Baroque style.
                        •	A World Heritage Site.
                        •	Houses the body of St. Francis Xavier.
                    •	Castella de Aguada, Mumbai.
                    •	St. Paul’s Church, Diu (Completed in 1610 AD).
                        •	Built in Baroque style.
                    •	Diu Fort (Built in 1535 AD).
                        •	Located on the coast of Diu island.
                        •	Contains a lighthouse and cannons on the walls.
                        •	Has three churches:
                            •	St. Thomas Church
                            •	St. Paul’s Church
                            •	Church of St. Francis of Assisi
                    •	Church of St. Anne, Talaulim (Goa) (Completed in 1695 AD).
                        •	Built in Baroque style.
            - French Influence
                •	Introduced urban city planning.
                •	Used Cartesian grid plans and scientific architectural designs in cities.
                •	Built imposing structures as a symbol of power.
                •	Introduced anonymous architecture (simple facades without ornamentation, similar to modern buildings).
                •	Developed coastal towns:
                    •	Puducherry
                    •	Chandernagore (West Bengal)
                    •	Mahe (Kerala)
                    •	Karaikal (Tamil Nadu)
                    •	Yanam (Andhra Pradesh)    
                Notable Constructions
                    •	Church of Sacred Heart of Jesus, Puducherry.
                    •	Sacred Heart Church, Chandannagar.
            - British Influence
                •	Introduced Gothic architecture, which merged with Indian styles.
                •	Led to the development of Indo-Gothic architecture.
                •	After 1911, the Neo-Roman style emerged.
                Indo-Gothic Style (Victorian Style)
                    •	A blend of Indian, Persian, and Gothic elements.
                    Features
                        •	Large, elaborate constructions.
                        •	Thinner walls compared to Indo-Islamic buildings.
                        •	Pointed arches, unlike the curved arches of Indo-Islamic architecture.
                        •	Large windows, a hallmark of Victorian architecture.
                        •	Churches followed a cruciform (cross-shaped) ground plan.
                        •	Used advanced British structural engineering:
                            •	Steel, iron, and poured concrete became common.
                    Notable Constructions
                        •	Victoria Memorial, Kolkata.
                        •	Gateway of India, Mumbai.
                Neo-Roman (Neo-Classical) Style
                    •	Post-1911, British Raj constructions followed the Neo-Roman (Neo-Classical) style.
                    •	The architecture of New Delhi, designed by Edwin Lutyens and Herbert Baker, is a prime example.
                    •	This phase is often referred to as the “Rome of Hindustan.”
                    Key Features
                        •	Anonymous and unremarkable constructions with minimal artistic expression.
                        •	A confluence of multiple architectural styles, making the designs congested and cramped.
                        •	Simplicity, modernity, and utility were compromised due to excessive hybridization.
                        •	Emphasis on circular buildings.
                        •	Overuse of oriental motifs to fit Western architectural aesthetics.
                        •	Introduction of the upturned dome, seen in structures like:
                            •	Supreme Court of India
                            •	Rashtrapati Bhavan

            Post-Independence Architecture
                After 1947, two architectural schools emerged:
                    •	Revivalist
                    •	Modernist
                However, both retained a colonial influence, leading to a decline in India’s architectural traditions.
                    For instance, the Punjab government hired French architect Le Corbusier to design Chandigarh.
                Notable Architects
                    Laurie Baker
                        •	Known as the “Architect of the Poor” for revolutionizing mass housing in Kerala.
                        •	Nominated for the Pritzker Prize (2006), often called the “Nobel Prize for Architecture”.
                        Key Features of His Architectural Style
                            •	Focused on environment-friendly buildings using locally available materials.
                            •	Introduced filler slab construction to reduce steel and cement consumption.
                            •	Emphasized ventilation and thermal comfort.
                            •	Known as “The Conscience Keeper of Indian Architecture” and “The Gandhi of Indian Architecture”.                
                    Charles Correa
                        •	Renowned for his contributions to urban architecture and spatial planning.
                        •	Adapted modern architectural principles to suit local sensibilities and needs.
                        •	Designed iconic buildings such as:
                            •	Madhya Pradesh Assembly Building
                            •	Mahatma Gandhi Memorial Museum, Ahmedabad
                            •	LIC Building, Connaught Place, New Delhi
                        •	Awarded Padma Vibhushan (2006).
            Conclusion                    
                From pre-historic times, art and architecture have played a vital role in India’s life and culture.
                Influences from Greeks, Arabs, Persians, and Europeans have contributed to the rich and diverse Indian architectural heritage.

- INDIAN PAINTINGS
    History of Painting in India
        India has a long tradition of artistic excellence, with painting being a major medium of expression. 
        The history of Indian painting can be traced through various historical phases:
            - Ancient Period
                - Prehistoric Rock Paintings
                    •	Earliest evidence of artistic expression.
                    •	Found in Bhimbetka, Mirzapur, and Panchmarhi.
                - Indus Valley Civilization
                    •	Painted pottery suggests early artistic expression.
                - Gupta Age – The True Beginning of Indian Painting
                    •	Considered the real beginning of painting as an art form.
            - Medieval Period
                •	The rise of Miniature Painting as a dominant style in Mughal and Rajput courts.
            - Colonial Influence
                •	With the arrival of Europeans, painting and engraving adopted Western techniques.
            - Modern Era
                - Experimentation and Innovation
                    •	Indian painters began experimenting with styles, colors, and designs.
                - Global Recognition
                    •	Many Indian artists gained worldwide recognition, winning prizes and accolades for their vision.

    PRINCIPLES OF PAINTING
        The Shadanga (3rd Century AD) (six limbs of painting) were described by Vatsyayana in his book Kamasutra. 
        These principles form the foundation of traditional Indian painting.
                ---------------------------------------------- Table Start ----------------------------------------------
                Variety of form : Rupabheda
                Proportion of the object or subject : Pramanam
                Creation of luster and gleam with the colours : Bhava
                Immersion of emotions : Lavanya Yojanam
                Portrayal of likeliness of the subject : Sadrisyam
                Mixing of colours to resemble the effects of modelling : Varnikabhanga
                ---------------------------------------------- Table End ----------------------------------------------

        References in Brahmanical and Buddhist Literature
            •	Ancient texts mention different types of paintings:
                •	Lepya Chitra – Representation of myths and lore on textiles.
                •	Lekhya Chitra – Line drawings and sketches.
                •	Other types include Dhuli Chitra and Pata Chitra.
            •	The play Mudrarakshasa by Vishakhadatta references various paintings (patas), helping understand different painting styles and the application of Shadanga.
                Some of the styles (from Mudrakshasa) were:
                ---------------------------------------------- Table Start ----------------------------------------------
                    Styles of Paintings Major features
                    Cauka Pitaka
                    Isolated framed drawings
                    Dighala Pitaka
                    Long scrolls of paintings
                    Yama Pitaka
                    Isolated paintings
                ---------------------------------------------- Table End ----------------------------------------------

    PRE-HISTORIC PAINTINGS (Headings won't include everything)
        Pre-historic paintings were primarily executed on rocks, and these rock engravings were called Petroglyphs. The first set of pre-historic paintings was discovered in Bhimbetka caves, Madhya Pradesh.      
        Phases of Pre-Historic Paintings (three major phases)
            - Upper Paleolithic Period (40,000-10,000 BC)
                •	The rock shelter caves were made of quartzite, and minerals were used as pigments.
                •	One of the most commonly used minerals was ochre (geru) mixed with lime and water.
                •	Different minerals were used to produce colors like red, white, yellow, and green.
                •	Depictions in paintings:
                    •	Large animals like bison, elephants, rhinos, and tigers were depicted using white, dark red, and green.
                    •	Human figurines:
                        •	Red was used for hunters.
                        •	Green was used mostly for dancers.
            - Mesolithic Period (10,000-4000 BC)
                •	This period primarily saw the use of red color.
                •	The paintings became smaller in size compared to the Upper Paleolithic Period.
                •	Common themes:
                    •	Group hunting scenes were frequently depicted.
                    •	Paintings also portrayed grazing activities and riding scenes.
            - Chalcolithic Period (4000-1000 BC)
                •	Paintings from this period show increased use of green and yellow colors.
                •	Common themes:
                    •	Battle scenes were frequently depicted.
                    •	Many paintings show men riding horses and elephants, some carrying bows and arrows, possibly indicating preparedness for skirmishes.
                •	Late Historical Inscriptions:
                    •	Samples of Ashokan and Gupta Brahmi scripts found in these cave sites suggest habitation in later historical periods.
        Regional Pre-Historic Painting Sites        
            - Narsinghgarh, Madhya Pradesh
                •	Depictions:
                •	Skins of spotted deer left for drying, supporting the theory that early humans perfected the art of tanning skins for shelter and clothing.
                •	Musical instruments like the harp.
                •	Complex geometric shapes like spirals, rhomboids, and circles.
            - Jogimara Caves, Chhattisgarh
                •	Located in Ramgarh Hills, Surguja district.
                •	Paintings date to around 1000 BC.
            - Kanker District, Chhattisgarh
                •	Caves include Udkuda, Garagodi, Khairkheda, Gotitola, and Kulgaon.
                •	Depictions:
                •	Human figurines, animals, palm prints, bullock carts – showing a higher, more sedentary lifestyle.
            - Korea District, Chhattisgarh
                •	Ghodsar and Kohabaur rock art sites feature similar paintings.
            - Chitwa Dongri, Durg District, Chhattisgarh
                •	Depictions:
                •	A Chinese figure riding a donkey.
                •	Dragons and agricultural sceneries.
            - Other Notable Sites
                •	Limdariha (Bastar District, Chhattisgarh)
                •	Oogdi & Sitalekhni (Surguja District, Chhattisgarh)
                •	Gudahandi Rock Shelter, Odisha
                •	Yogimatha Rock Shelter, Odisha
        BHIMBETKA ROCK PAINTINGS
            •	Location: South of Bhopal, Madhya Pradesh, in the Vindhyan Ranges.
            •	Recognition: Declared a UNESCO World Heritage Site in 2003.
            •	Age:
                •	The oldest paintings are estimated to be 30,000 years old.
                •	The caves show continuous human habitation from 100,000 BC to 1000 AD.
                •	Many paintings were painted over older ones, showing an evolution of artistic styles.
            •	Artistic Periods Represented:
                •	Upper Paleolithic
                •	Mesolithic (Most dominant)
                •	Chalcolithic
                •	Early Historic
                •	Medieval Period
            - Themes and Subjects in Bhimbetka Paintings
                •	Daily life of pre-historic humans:
                    •	Stick-like human figures.
                    •	Social activities: Dancing, playing music, honey collection, community life.
                    •	Men carrying weapons (bows, arrows, spears, shields, swords).
                •	Animals depicted:
                    •	Elephants, bison, deer, peacocks, snakes.
                •	War scenes:
                    •	Men with weapons, possibly depicting battles or conflicts.
                •	Geometric designs:
                    •	Simple symbols, spirals, and abstract shapes.
            Colors Used in Bhimbetka Paintings
                •	Natural pigments obtained from minerals:
                    •	Red ochre (from haematite ores).
                    •	Purple, brown, white, yellow, green.

    CLASSIFICATION OF INDIAN PAINTINGS
        Mural Paintings in India
            Murals refer to paintings made on walls or solid structures. 
            They have existed in India since ancient times and can be dated between 10th century BC and 10th century AD.
            Murals in India are found at multiple locations, including Ajanta, Armamalai Cave, Ravan Chhaya Rock Shelter, Bagh Caves, Sittanavasal Caves, and the Kailasha Temple at Ellora. 
                Most of these paintings are in natural caves or rock-cut chambers, and they follow Hindu, Buddhist, and Jain themes. 
                However, some murals also depict secular subjects, such as the ancient theatre room murals in Jogimara Caves.
            - Jataka Stories in Indian Murals
                Jataka tales narrate the previous births of Gautam Buddha, where he appears in different forms like a king, outcast, god, or animal, exhibiting virtues to impart moral lessons.
                Famous Jataka Stories Depicted in Murals
                    •	The Ass in the Lion’s Skin (Sihacamma Jataka)
                    •	The Foolish, Timid Rabbit (Daddabha Jataka)
                    •	The Jackal and the Crow (Jambu-Khadaka Jataka)
                    •	King Shibi (Shibi Jataka)
                    •	The Swan with Golden Feathers (Suvannaharnsa Jataka)
                    •	The Turtle Who Couldn’t Stop Talking (Kacchapa Jataka)
            - Famous mural paintings
                - Ajanta Cave Paintings
                    One of the oldest surviving murals in the Indian subcontinent, Ajanta Caves were carved between 2nd century BC and 5th century AD out of volcanic rocks. 
                    The caves are carved in a horseshoe shape and consist of 29 Buddhist caves, known for their exquisite mural paintings.
                    Key Features of Ajanta Murals
                    •	Timeline:
                        •	Cave No. 9 and 10 belong to the Shunga period.
                        •	The rest belong to the Gupta period (Cave No. 1 and 2 being the most recent).
                    •	Techniques Used:
                        •	Both murals and fresco paintings (on wet plaster).
                        •	Tempera style (pigments applied with binding agents).
                    •	Themes Depicted:
                        •	Jataka stories (previous lives of Buddha).
                        •	Life of Gautam Buddha and various Bodhisattvas.
                        •	Elaborate decorative patterns of flora and fauna.
                    •	Distinctive Features:
                        •	Female figures have unique hairstyles.
                        •	Emotions are expressed through hand gestures.
                        •	Even animals and birds are shown with emotions.
                    •	Painting Medium:
                        •	Vegetable and mineral dyes.
                        •	Outlines in red ochre, with contours in brown, black, or deep red.
                    - Important Paintings at Ajanta
                        •	Jataka Stories: Scenes from Buddha’s former lives.
                        •	Bodhisattva Paintings (Cave No. 1)
                            •	Vajrapani – Protector and guide, symbolizing Buddha’s power.
                            •	Manjusri – Manifestation of Buddha’s wisdom.
                            •	Padmapani (Avalokitesvara) – Symbol of Buddha’s compassion.
                        •	The Dying Princess (Cave No. 16).
                        •	Shibi Jataka: King Shibi offers his own flesh to save a pigeon.
                        •	Matri-Poshaka Jataka: An ungrateful man betrays the elephant that saved him.
                - Ellora Cave Paintings
                    Ellora murals are found in five caves, with the most famous being Cave No. 16 (Kailasha Temple).
                    Phases of Painting
                        1.	First Phase – Painted during the carving of caves.
                        2.	Second Phase – Painted centuries later in Gujarati style.
                    Themes Depicted
                        •	Hindu Paintings:
                            •	Vishnu with Lakshmi, carried by Garuda.
                            •	Shiva with his followers.
                        •	Shaiva Procession: Later paintings show Shaiva holy men.
                        •	Religious Diversity: Murals represent Buddhism, Jainism, and Hinduism.
                    Important Paintings at Ellora
                        •	Goddess Lakshmi and Lord Vishnu.
                        •	Graceful Apsaras.
                - Bagh Cave Paintings
                    Located in Madhya Pradesh, Bagh murals are an extension of the Ajanta school.
                    Key Features
                        •	Figures are tightly modeled, with stronger outlines and earthly, human-like expressions.
                        •	Cave No. 4 (Rang Mahal) has murals similar to Ajanta’s Jataka tales.
                        •	Secular Elements: Depicts contemporary lifestyle along with religious themes.
                - Armamalai Cave Paintings
                    Located in Vellore district, Tamil Nadu, these natural caves were converted into a Jain temple (8th century).
                    Key Features
                        •	Unbaked mud structures inside the caves served as resting places for Jain saints.
                        •	Themes: Depicts Astathik Palakas (deities protecting eight directions) and Jainism.
                - Sittanavasal Cave (Arivar Koil) Paintings
                    Located in Tamil Nadu, dating from 1st century BC – 10th century AD.
                    Key Features
                        •	Themes: Jain Samavasarana (Preaching Hall).
                        •	Similarities to Ajanta and Bagh – Paintings on walls, ceilings, and pillars.
                        •	Scholarly Debate: Some attribute the paintings to Pallavas, while others to the Pandyas (7th century).
                        •	Medium: Vegetable and mineral dyes applied on wet lime plaster.
                        •	Prominent Colours: Yellow, green, orange, blue, black, and white.
                    Notable Paintings
                        •	Pond with lotuses, monks, ducks, swans, fishes, and animals.
                        •	Depiction of Samavasarana: The sacred hall where Tirthankaras preach.
                - Ravan Chhaya Rock Shelter
                    Located in Keonjhar district, Odisha.
                    Key Features
                        •	Fresco paintings on a half-open umbrella-shaped rock shelter.
                        •	Royal procession painting (7th century AD).
                        •	Remains of Chola period paintings (11th century AD).
                - Lepakshi Temple Paintings
                    Located in Anantapur, Andhra Pradesh, inside the Veerabhadra temple (16th century).
                    Key Features
                        •	Vijayanagara Period murals, based on Ramayana, Mahabharata, and Vishnu’s incarnations.
                        •	Absence of blue color, showing decline in mural quality.
                        •	Black outlines for costumes and figures.
                - Jogimara Cave Paintings
                    Located in Surguja district, Chhattisgarh, dated 1000–300 BC.
                    Key Features
                        •	Artificially carved cave, believed to be part of an ancient amphitheatre.
                        •	Red-outlined paintings of dancing couples, elephants, and fish.
                        •	Inscriptions in Brahmi script narrating a love story.
                - Murals at Badami Cave Temples, Karnataka
                    While famous for sculptures, Badami caves also feature murals (6th–7th century AD).
                    Key Features
                        •	Earliest surviving Hindu paintings.
                        •	Subjects:
                            •	Chalukyan kings.
                            •	Jain saints renouncing worldly life.
                            •	Shiva, Parvati, and Puranic deities.
                        •	Notable Painting: Cave No. 3 features an ancient mural of four-armed Brahma on a swan.
            Conclusion
                Mural paintings in India are not just decorative but also historical records of religious, cultural, and social life. 
                They exhibit advanced artistic techniques and rich storytelling, making them invaluable to India’s artistic heritage.

        Miniature Paintings in India (This heading is part of content of Medieval era paintings)
            - Basic info
                The word ‘miniature’ comes from the Latin word Minium, meaning red lead paint.
                This paint was used in illuminated manuscripts during the Renaissance period.
                Many people confuse miniature with minimum, assuming it refers to small size.
                However, miniatures are small but highly detailed paintings.
                The Indian subcontinent has a long tradition of miniature paintings.
                Several schools of miniature painting developed, each differing in composition and perspective.
            - Certain conditions must be met for a painting to be called a miniature:
                •	The painting should not be larger than 25 square inches.
                •	The subject should be painted at one-sixth of its actual size.
                    •	For example, if a human head is 9 inches, it should not exceed 1.5 inches in the painting.
            - Common Features of Indian Miniatures
                •	Human Figures
                    •	Usually shown in side profile.
                    •	Have bulging eyes, pointed nose, and a slim waist.
                •	Regional Variations
                    •	Rajasthani miniatures depict people with brown skin.
                    •	Mughal paintings show figures with fair skin.
                    •	Lord Krishna is always depicted with blue skin.
                •	Facial Features
                    •	Women have long black hair.
                    •	Eyes and hair are generally painted in black.
                •	Clothing
                    •	Men wear traditional clothes and turbans.
            - Early Miniatures
                Miniature paintings were small, detailed artworks often created for books or albums.
                They were painted on perishable materials like paper, palm leaves, and cloth.
                This art form developed between the 8th and 12th centuries, emerging as a reaction to large mural paintings.
                Two prominent schools of early miniature painting were:
                    - Pala School of Art (750–1150 AD)
                        •	Flourished in Bihar and Bengal under Pala rulers.
                        •	Found in Buddhist manuscripts, painted on palm leaves or vellum paper.
                        •	Buddhist monks used these paintings, adhering to non-violence by limiting materials to banana or coconut leaves.
                        Characteristics:
                            •	Sinuous lines and subdued background tones.
                            •	Single figures dominate the compositions; group paintings are rare.
                            •	Patronized by rulers promoting Buddhism, especially the Vajrayana School.
                            •	Prominent painters: Dhimman and Vitapala.    
                    - Apabhramsa School of Art (11th–15th Century)
                        •	Originated in Gujarat and Mewar (Rajasthan).
                        •	Flourished as the main Western Indian painting style.
                        •	Initially Jain-themed, later influenced by Vaishnavism, incorporating themes like Gita Govinda and secular love.
                        Development:
                            •	Early phase: Painted on palm leaves.
                            •	Later phase: Shifted to paper as a medium.
                            •	Unlike book illustrations, the style remained similar to mural paintings in a smaller form.
                            •	Colors had symbolic meanings, mainly using red, yellow, and ochre.
                            •	Gold and bright colors were introduced in the later period.
                        Human and Animal Depictions:
                            •	Bulging, fish-shaped eyes, pointed noses, and double chins.
                            •	Early attempts at angular faces in three-quarter and fourth profiles.
                            •	Figures are stiff, with detailed ornamentation.
                            •	Female figures have enlarged hips and breasts.
                            •	Animals and birds resemble toy-like forms.
                        Famous Examples:
                            •	Kalpasutra and Kalakacharya Katha (15th century).
            Transition Period Miniature (14th Century)
                The arrival of Muslims in India led to cultural transformations and an artistic renaissance.
                However, traditional Indian styles survived in Western Indian courts, blending with new influences.
                    •	In Vijayanagara (South India), a distinct Deccan style emerged.
                    •	Colors were applied flatly, with black outlines for human figures.
                    •	Three-quarter face profiles gave subjects a detached appearance.
                    •	Landscapes were filled with trees, rocks, and decorative elements, rather than naturalistic scenery.
            Miniature Art During the Delhi Sultanate
                Miniature paintings from the Delhi Sultanate blended Persian and Indian artistic traditions.
                - Illustrated Manuscripts
                    •	Nimatnama (Book on Cookery) – commissioned by Nasir Shah of Mandu.
                    •	This manuscript shows a fusion of Persian and Indian art styles.
                - Lodi Khuladar Style
                    •	A painting style used in Delhi, Jaunpur, and other Sultanate regions.
                - Evolution into Major Styles
                    The Sultanate period laid the foundation for three dominant miniature painting styles of medieval India:
                        1.	Mughal Miniature Painting
                        2.	Rajput Miniature Painting
                        3.	Deccan Miniature Painting
                    These styles built on Sultanate-era influences while developing their own distinct identity.
            Mughal Era Miniature Paintings
                Mughal paintings had a distinct style, drawing influence from Persian art.
                There were significant changes in color palette, themes, and forms.
                    •	The focus shifted from depicting gods to glorifying rulers and court life.
                    •	Popular themes included hunting scenes, historical events, and courtly depictions.
                    •	The paintings combined Persian naturalistic styles with Mughal grandeur, creating beautifully illustrated folios.
                    •	Brilliant colors and precise line drawings were key characteristics.
                    •	Unlike religious paintings, Mughal art explored diverse themes.
                Innovations
                    •	Introduced foreshortening (a technique where objects appear smaller as they recede).
                    •	Developed a more realistic approach to depiction.
                - Painting under different Mughal Era's
                    - Early Mughal Painters
                        - Babur & Humayun
                            •	Babur had little time for paintings due to military campaigns, but he patronized Persian artist Bihzad.
                            •	Humayun, a great patron of the arts, was exiled to Persia during Sher Shah Suri’s rule.
                            •	In Persia, he met Abdus Samad and Mir Sayyid Ali, two renowned artists.
                            •	After reclaiming the throne, Humayun brought these artists to India, introducing Persian influences to Mughal art.
                            •	The first major Mughal illustrated manuscript: Tutinama (Tales of a Parrot) during Akbar’s reign.
                    - Akbar’s Era (Significant Expansion of Mughal Painting)
                        •	Akbar established a formal painting department called Tasvir Khana.
                        •	Hired artists on salary, allowing them to develop unique styles.
                        •	Viewed painting as both a learning tool and entertainment.
                        •	Believed paintings could reflect a person’s demeanor and rewarded lifelike depictions.
                        •	Encouraged the use of calligraphy in paintings.
                        •	Shift from folk art to court art, focusing more on royal life than common people.
                        Characteristics of Akbar’s Paintings
                            •	Three-dimensional figures.
                            •	Extensive use of foreshortening for realism.
                            •	Fusion of Persian and Indian artistic styles.
                        Famous Artists
                            •	Daswanth, Basawan, Kesu.
                        Important Illustrated Manuscripts
                            •	Tutinama
                            •	Hamzanama
                            •	Anvar-i-Suhaili
                            •	Gulistan of Sadi
                    - Jahangir’s Era (Peak of Mughal Miniature Painting)
                        •	Jahangir was a naturalist, favoring paintings of flora and fauna (birds, animals, trees, flowers).
                        •	Shifted focus from illustrated manuscripts to albums.
                        •	Developed individual portraits with an emphasis on naturalism.
                        •	Introduced decorative margins around paintings, sometimes as elaborate as the artwork itself.
                        Notable Contributions
                            •	Personally interested in painting but no major work by him survives.
                            •	His artists specialized in miniature paintings.
                        Famous Artists
                            •	Ustad Mansoor – known for realistic animal and human depictions.
                        Famous Works
                            •	Naturalistic paintings of a zebra, turkey, and cock.
                            •	Illustrated animal fable: Ayar-i-Danish (Touchstone of Knowledge).
                    - Shah Jahan’s Era (Shift Toward Artificial Elements)
                        •	Moved away from naturalistic depictions to a more stylized approach.
                        •	Introduced artificial stillness in paintings, inspired by European influences.
                        •	Discouraged charcoal sketches, promoting pencil drawing instead.
                        •	Increased use of gold and silver.
                        •	Preferred brighter color palettes than his predecessors.
                        - Changes in Mughal Painting
                            •	The Mughal atelier expanded, but the style and techniques changed significantly.
                    - Aurangzeb’s Era (Decline of Mughal Miniature Painting)
                        •	Aurangzeb did not patronize art.
                        •	Many Mughal court painters migrated to provincial courts in Rajasthan and elsewhere.
                        •	This led to a sharp decline in the Mughal painting tradition.
            Regional Schools of Miniature Painting
                Even though the medieval period was dominated by the Mughal style of painting, sub-imperial schools developed their own distinct styles. 
                They retained their Indian roots and a penchant for colorful paintings, contrasting the more naturalistic Mughal style.
                - Rajasthani School of Painting (Rajput School of Painting)
                    Rajput courts began patronizing paintings, either as an emulation of Mughal court practices or due to the influence of Deccan Sultanates and local artistic traditions. 
                    It has several sub-schools based on princely states:
                        •	Mewar School of Painting
                            •	Patronized even during periods of adversity; flourished during times of peace.
                            •	Dominated by the 17th-century painter Sahibdin, who depicted literary texts such as:
                                •	Ragamala
                                •	Ramayana
                                •	Bhagavata Purana
                            •	Later paintings focused on court life, with unique tamasha paintings showing court ceremonies and city views.
                        •	Amber-Jaipur (Dhundar) School of Painting
                            •	Close association with the Mughals, but identity less well-known due to private collections.
                            •	Earliest evidence from wall paintings at Bairat and Amer Palace.
                            •	Men depicted in Mughal-style attire, but the paintings have a folk-style finish.
                            •	Reached its peak under Sawai Pratap Singh (18th century), who commissioned miniatures illustrating:
                                •	Bhagavata Purana
                                •	Ramayana
                                •	Ragamala
                        •	Marwar School of Painting
                            •	Includes Jodhpur, Bikaner, and Jaisalmer.
                            •	Initially followed Mughal patterns (15th–16th centuries), later incorporated Rajput elements (18th century), such as:
                                •	Linear rhythm and bright colors.
                            •	Flourished under Man Singh (1803-1843), who commissioned paintings of:
                                •	Shiva Purana
                                •	Natacharitra
                                •	Durgacharitra
                                •	Panchatantra
                        •	Kishangarh School of Painting (17th–18th Century)
                            •	Associated with the romantic legend of Sawant Singh and Bani Thani.
                            •	Distinctive Features:
                                •	Female figures with elongated lotus-like eyes, thin lips, and pointed chin.
                                •	Famous for Radha-Krishna depictions in devotional and amorous themes.
                        •	Bundi School of Painting (17th–19th Century)
                            •	Developed in Bundi and Kota (Hadoti region).
                            •	Kings were devotees of Krishna, declaring themselves regents ruling on his behalf.
                            •	Paintings feature:
                                •	Detailed local vegetation.
                                •	Round human faces with pointed noses.
                                •	Sky painted in different colors, often with a red ribbon motif.
                - Pahari School of Painting (17th–19th Century)
                    Developed in sub-Himalayan states under Mughal influence, covering around 22 princely states from Jammu to Almora.
                        •	Basholi School (17th Century)
                            •	Originated in Basholi, Jammu & Kashmir.
                            •	Characterized by:
                                •	Expressive faces with receding hairline and lotus-petal-shaped big eyes.
                                •	Bright primary colors (red, yellow, green).
                            •	Patronized by Raja Kirpal Pal, with notable themes including:
                                •	Rasamanjari
                                •	Gita Govinda
                                •	Ramayana
                        •	Kangra School (Mid-18th Century)
                            •	Emerged as Guler-Kangra School after Mughal decline.
                            •	Flourished under Raja Sansar Chand, known for:
                                •	Sensual and emotive style.
                                •	Popular themes:
                                    •	Gita Govinda
                                    •	Bhagavata Purana
                                    •	Satsai of Biharilal
                                    •	Nal-Damayanti
                                    •	Love scenes of Krishna.
                            •	A famous series from this school: “Twelve Months” – illustrating emotions associated with each month.
                            •	Influenced later Kullu, Chamba, and Mandi paintings.
                - Ragamala Paintings
                    •	A series of illustrative paintings based on musical Ragas (16th–17th centuries).
                    •	Represented in Pahari, Rajasthani, Deccan, and Mughal styles.
                    •	Each Raga personified by:
                        •	A hero and heroine (nayaka-nayika) in a specific mood.
                        •	Depiction of seasons and time of day for performance.
                        •	Hindu deities associated with Ragas, e.g.:
                            •	Bhairava/Bhairavi → Shiva
                            •	Sri → Devi
                    •	Six principal Ragas:
                        •	Bhairava
                        •	Deepak
                        •	Sri
                        •	Malkaush
                        •	Megha
                        •	Hindola
