            South Indian Miniature Paintings
                South India had a distinct painting tradition that included both miniature and larger mural styles. Unlike North Indian miniature schools, South Indian miniature paintings:
                    •	Had a stronger religious focus rather than glorifying rulers.
                    •	Frequently used gold leaf and embellishments, especially in Tanjore and Mysore styles.
                    •	Developed alongside temple mural traditions, which influenced their themes and compositions.
                - Vijayanagara & Nayaka Miniature Paintings (14th–17th Century)
                    Predecessors to later South Indian miniature traditions
                    Developed in manuscripts and temple murals
                    Influence on later Tanjore and Mysore miniature styles
                    •	Patronage: Vijayanagara kings and later the Nayakas of Tamil Nadu & Andhra Pradesh.
                    •	Themes:
                        •	Religious epics: Ramayana, Mahabharata, and Puranas.
                        •	Narrative storytelling, often in manuscript illustrations.
                    •	Features:
                        •	Bright colors, flat compositions, and detailed costumes/jewelry.
                        •	Miniatures in palm-leaf and paper manuscripts.
                        •	Temple murals influenced later South Indian miniatures.
                - Tanjore Miniature Paintings (17th–19th Century) - Gold-Coated Miniatures
                    Developed as a distinct South Indian miniature style
                    Evolved from Vijayanagara traditions
                    Characterized by gold leaf, embossing, and bright colors
                    •	Origin: Thanjavur (Tanjore), Tamil Nadu.
                    •	Developed After:
                        •	The fall of Vijayanagara Empire.
                        •	Patronized by Maratha rulers in the 18th century.
                    •	Unique Features:
                        •	Miniature format but painted on wooden planks (Palagai Padam).
                        •	Brilliant colors and liberal use of gold leaf.
                        •	Embellishments with gemstones & cut glass.
                    •	Themes:
                        •	Primarily Hindu deities, especially Lord Krishna in various forms.
                    •	Patronage:
                        •	Flourished under Maharaja Serfoji II of the Maratha dynasty.
                    •	Current Status:
                        •	Still practiced today with themes expanded to birds, animals, and modern subjects.
                        •	Recognized as a Geographical Indication (GI) by the Government of India.    
                - Mysore Miniature Paintings (18th–19th Century) - Delicate & Detailed Style
                    Evolved from Vijayanagara & Nayaka painting styles
                    More delicate and refined than Tanjore miniatures
                    Used gesso paste technique for a glossy effect
                    •	Patronized by: Rulers of Mysore, continuing into British rule.
                    •	Themes:
                        •	Primarily Hindu gods and goddesses.
                        •	Typically featured two or more figures, with one dominant figure in size and color.
                    •	Unique Technique:
                        •	Used “gesso paste” – a mix of white lead powder, gamboge, and glue.
                        •	Created a shiny background, contrasting with muted figure colors.
                    •	Connection with Tanjore Paintings:
                        •	Both evolved from Vijayanagara & Nayaka traditions.
                        •	Mysore paintings were finer and more intricate, while Tanjore paintings were bold and ornamental.  
                - Palm-Leaf & Manuscript Miniature Paintings
                    Early form of South Indian miniatures
                    Found in religious manuscripts and temple records
                    Used intricate line work and natural pigments
                    •	Medium: Palm leaves and paper.
                    •	Themes: Hindu deities, epics, and regional folklore.
                    •	Technique:
                        •	Fine engraving and inking on palm leaves.
                        •	Natural dyes and vegetable colors for illustrations.
                - Ganjifa Cards (Hand-Painted Miniature Playing Cards - 16th Century Onward)
                    A form of miniature painting with Mughal & South Indian variations
                    Popular in royal courts as a decorative art form
                    •	Origin: Popular in medieval India, especially in Mughal & South Indian courts.
                    •	Features:
                        •	Each suit had different colors and intricate miniature designs.
                        •	Referenced in Baburnama (Mughal Emperor Babur’s autobiography).
                    •	Mysore Ganjifa Cards:
                        •	Recognized as a Geographical Indication (GI) in 2008 by the Government of India.

    Modern Indian Painting
        - Miniature and Hybrid Painting Styles          
            - Company Paintings (Kampani Kalam)
                •	In the colonial period, a hybrid style of painting emerged that combined elements of Rajput, Mughal, and other Indian styles with European influences.
                •	This style evolved when British Company officers employed Indian-trained painters, leading to a fusion of their European tastes with Indian artistic traditions.
                •	Known as ‘Company Paintings,’ they were characterized by:
                    •	The use of watercolors.
                    •	Linear perspective and shading, influenced by European techniques.
                •	This style originated in Kolkata, Chennai, Delhi, Patna, Varanasi, and Thanjavur.
                •	Patrons: Mary Impey and Marquess Wellesley supported numerous painters, who depicted India’s exotic flora and fauna.
                •	Notable artists: Mazhar Ali Khan and Ghulam Ali Khan.
                •	This genre continued to flourish until the 20th century.
            - Bazaar Paintings
                •	Also influenced by European encounters in India.
                •	Unlike Company Paintings, Bazaar Paintings did not blend Indian elements with European techniques. Instead, they adopted Greek and Roman influences.
                •	Key Features:
                    •	Painters were made to copy Greek and Roman statues.
                    •	Common in Bengal and Bihar.
                    •	Depictions of Indian bazaars with a European background.
                    •	Popular themes:
                        •	Indian courtesans dancing before British officials.
                        •	Religious themes, but figures with multiple arms or elephant faces (like Lord Ganesha) were prohibited, as they deviated from European notions of human form.
        - Evolution of Modern Indian Painting
            - Raja Ravi Varma (1848–1906)
                •	Considered the Father of Modern Indian Art.
                •	Contribution: Originated a modern school of painting that combined:
                    •	South Indian painting styles.
                    •	Western techniques of color and shading.
                •	Famous works:
                    •	Ladies in the Moonlight.
                    •	Shakuntala.
                    •	Damayanti and Swan.
                    •	Ravana Kidnapping Sita and Killing Jatayu (from the Ramayana).
                •	A film titled “Rang Rasiya” was made on his life.
            - Bengal School of Art
                •	Emerged as a reaction to previous painting styles.
                •	Used simpler colors with an emphasis on Swadeshi values, reducing Western materialistic influences.
                •	Key Figures:
                •	Abanindranath Tagore: His Arabian Night series gained global recognition.
                •	Nandalal Bose: 
                        •	Developed Modern Indian Art further.
                        •	Known for his white-on-black Gandhi sketch of the Dandi March.
                        •	Illustrated the original Constitution of India.
                •	Rabindranath Tagore:
                    •	Used dominant black lines to make subjects prominent.
                    •	Created small-sized paintings.
                    •	Some historians believe his paintings were linked to his writings.
                •	Other notable painters: 
                    Asit Kumar Haldar, Manishi Dey, Mukul Dey, Sunayani Devi.
            - Cubist Style of Painting in India
                •	Inspired by the European Cubist movement.
                •	Characteristics:
                    •	Objects were broken, analyzed, and reassembled in abstract forms.
                    •	Aimed to achieve balance between line and color.
                •	Prominent Artist: M.F. Husain
                    •	Frequently used the horse motif to depict fluidity of motion.
        - Progressive and Folk Art Movements
            - Progressive Artists Group (Post-1947)
                •	Gained attention for their progressive and bold themes.
                •	Incorporated abstract and modernist elements.
                •	Inspired by European Modernism.
                •	Founding Members:
                    •	F.N. Souza
                    •	S.H. Raza
                    •	M.F. Husain
                    •	K.H. Ara
                    •	H.A. Gade
                    •	S.K. Bakre
            - Additional side information
                - Folk Paintings
                    •	Madhubani Paintings (Mithila Art, Bihar)
                        •	Themes: Hindu religious motifs (Krishna, Rama, Durga, Lakshmi, Shiva).
                        •	Symbolism: Fish (good luck, fertility).
                        •	Traditionally made with rice paste and vegetable colors on cow dung-coated walls.
                        •	No shading; two-dimensional.
                        •	Recognized in 1970 when President of India honored Jagdamba Devi.
                        •	Other famous artists: Baua Devi, Bharti Dayal, Ganga Devi, Mahasundari Devi, Sita Devi.
                        •	Given GI (Geographical Indication) status.
                    •	Tikuli Art (Bihar)
                        •	Name derived from “Bindi”, worn by women.
                        •	Painted on hardboard, cut into shapes, coated with enamel for a polished finish.
                        •	Incorporates Madhubani motifs.
                    •	Pattachitra Painting (Odisha)
                        •	Traditional cloth-based painting.
                        •	Themes: Jagannath, Vaishnava, Shakti, and Shaiva cults.
                        •	Colors from natural sources (burnt coconut shells, hingula, ramaraja).
                        •	No pencil or charcoal used; outlines drawn with a brush.
                        •	Palm leaf variant: Talapattachitra.
                    •	Patua Art (Bengal)
                        •	Originated around 1000 years ago.
                        •	Scroll paintings (Pats) narrating Hindu mythological stories.
                        •	Traditionally painted on cloth; modern versions on paper.
                        •	Popular in Medinipur, Murshidabad, North & South 24 Parganas, Birbhum.
                    •	Paitkar Painting (Jharkhand)
                        •	Tribal scroll paintings.
                        •	Common Theme: What happens to human life after death.
                        •	Associated with Ma Mansa worship.
                    •	Kalamkari Painting (Andhra Pradesh)
                        •	Hand-painted textiles, drawn freehand using bamboo pens.
                        •	Centers: Srikalahasti and Machilipatnam.
                        •	Origin: Vijayanagara Empire.
                        •	Given GI status.
                    •	Warli Painting (Maharashtra-Gujarat border)
                        •	Tribal art resembling Bhimbetka murals.
                        •	Geometric forms (triangles, circles, squares) used to depict humans and animals.
                        •	White pigment made from gum and rice powder on a red ochre base.
                        •	Traditionally painted for harvests and weddings.
                    •	Thangka Painting (Tibetan Buddhist Art)
                        •	Practiced in Sikkim, Himachal Pradesh, Ladakh, Arunachal Pradesh.
                        •	Used as Buddhist devotional art.
                        •	Painted on cotton canvas with vegetable/mineral dyes.
                        •	Colors hold meanings (e.g., red for passion, white for serenity, black for anger).
                    •	Manjusha Painting (Bihar)
                        •	Also called Angika Art.
                        •	Always features snake motifs.
                    •	Phad Painting (Rajasthan)
                        •	Scroll-type religious paintings of deities Pabuji and Devnarayan.
                        •	Painted with vegetable colors on long cloth (Phad).
                    •	Cheriyal Scroll Painting (Telangana)
                        •	Nakashi art form.
                        •	Narrates Hindu epics in comic-like scrolls.
                        •	Granted GI status in 2007.
                    •	Pithora Painting (Madhya Pradesh & Gujarat)
                        •	Tribal religious paintings depicting horses.
                    •	Saura Painting (Odisha)
                        •	Similar to Warli paintings.
                        •	Dedicated to Idital, the Saura deity.
                        •	Made with natural dyes.


- INDIAN HANDICRAFTS
    Introduction
        Handicrafts are an amalgamation of all the things crafted by human hands. 
        Many states in India have their own handicrafts, reflecting the skill and artistic proficiency of their artisans. 
        These crafts can be either decorative or functional and are unique because they are solely handmade without the use of machines.
        In India, handicrafts are not only a means of livelihood but also a form of art preserved for future generations. 
        They hold special significance for tribal and rural communities who depend on them for their sustenance.

    Some of the major handicrafts of India are:
        - Glassware
            The art of glassmaking in India dates back to ancient times.
            - Ancient References and Archaeological Evidence
                •	The first reference to glassmaking appears in the Indian epic Mahabharata.
                •	However, material evidence does not indicate the presence of glass beads in the early Harappan Civilization.
                •	The first physical evidence of glass beads comes from the Painted Grey Ware culture of the Ganges Valley (1000 BC).
                •	The Vedic text Satapatha Brahmana refers to glass as kanch or kaca.
            - Glass Industry in Ancient and Medieval India
                •	Brahmapuri and Kolhapur (Maharashtra) (2nd century BC – 2nd century AD) had a thriving glass industry that specialized in lenticular beads.
                •	The Sanskrit text Vyasayogacharita mentions spectacles, suggesting the early development of optical lenses.
                •	In southern India, archaeological evidence of glass has been found at Maski (Chalcolithic site in the Deccan).
                •	Other sites yielding evidence of glass production include:
                    •	Ahar (Rajasthan)
                    •	Hastinapur and Ahichchhatra (Uttar Pradesh)
                    •	Eran and Ujjain (Madhya Pradesh)
                •	During the medieval period, the Mughals patronized the art of glassware, using it for decoration in monuments such as the Sheesh Mahal.
                •	Mughal-era glass items included:
                    •	Glass hukkas
                    •	Perfume boxes (ittardans)
                    •	Engraved glasses
            - Glassware in Modern India
                •	The glass industry has evolved significantly, with many regional specializations:
                    •	Glass bangles – Hyderabad produces the most exquisite ones, called “Churi ka jodas”.
                    •	Glass chandeliers & decorative pieces – Firozabad, Uttar Pradesh.
                    •	Glass toys (Panchkora) – Saharanpur, Uttar Pradesh.
                    •	Decorative glass beads (Tikuli Art) – Patna, Bihar.
                        •	Tikuli Art has almost disappeared due to industrialization, but it is still worn by the Santhal tribes of Bihar.
                        •	Today, Tikuli Art is also made on glazed hardboards to adapt to modern artistic expressions and preserve the tradition.
            - Cloth Handicrafts
                Cloth handicrafts in India involve various techniques like weaving, printing, and dyeing to create intricate patterns and textures. 
                Artists often use wooden blocks or printed cloths to imprint designs on fabrics. 
                One of the most well-known techniques is Tie and Dye, which produces stunning patterns on textiles.
                - Tie and Dye
                    The Tie and Dye technique, known as Bandhani or Bandhej, is an ancient Indian art form that remains popular today, particularly in Rajasthan and Gujarat. 
                        It is also practiced in Andhra Pradesh and Tamil Nadu.
                    - Tie and Dye Process
                        •	This technique is a resist dyeing process, meaning that the tied portions of the fabric do not absorb the dye.
                        •	The fabric is tied into a series of knots, dipped in dye, and then untied to reveal intricate patterns.
                    - Variations of Tie and Dye
                        •	Laharia:
                            •	Produces wave-like or ripple patterns on fabric.
                            •	Mainly practiced in Jaipur and Jodhpur (Rajasthan).
                        •	Ikat:
                            •	Also known as resist dyeing, but the dyeing is done on the yarn before weaving.
                            •	Major centers: Telangana, Odisha, Gujarat, and Andhra Pradesh.                
                - Other Traditional Fabric Art Forms    
                    - Kalamkari
                        •	A form of hand-painting on fabric using vegetable dyes.
                        •	Characterized by deep colors and intricate designs.
                        •	Commonly practiced in Andhra Pradesh.
                    - Batik Art
                        •	Involves wax-resist dyeing, where fabric is coated with molten wax, then dyed to create multicolored patterns.
                        •	Mainly practiced in Madhya Pradesh and West Bengal.
                        •	The art form originates from Indonesia.
                - Influence of Foreign Techniques
                    Some fabric techniques in India originated from trade routes and foreign influences:
                        •	Tanchoi Silk Weaving:
                            •	Inspired by Chinese silk weaving.
                            •	Likely introduced through trading communities in Surat, Gujarat.
                            •	Today, weavers from Varanasi are experts in crafting Tanchoi silk brocades, which resemble fine miniature paintings.
                        •	Jamdani Weaving:
                            •	An ancient art of muslin weaving practiced in West Bengal.
                            •	Features opaque patterns woven on a transparent background in various styles.

            Embroidery Crafts
                Embroidery is a unique craft used on textiles, involving raised designs created with threads of gold, silver, silk, or cotton. 
                    These designs are stitched onto fabric using needles or applied through wooden blocks. 
                India has a rich tradition of embroidery, with different regions developing distinct styles.
                Some important regional Embroidery Styles:
                    - Appliqué or Pipli Work (Odisha)
                        •	Practiced in Pipli village, Odisha.
                        •	A form of patchwork embroidery where colorful fabric pieces are sewn together to create a single decorative piece.
                        •	Used to make lamps, wall hangings, and decorative items.
                    - Phulkari (Punjab, Haryana, Delhi)
                        •	Phulkari means “flower making”.
                        •	Uses darning techniques to create vibrant floral patterns on fabric.
                    - Bagh (Punjab)
                        •	A medieval embroidery technique similar to Phulkari but with denser floral designs.
                        •	Created using silk threads on green cloth.
                    - Gota and Karchobi Work (Rajasthan)
                        •	Gota Work:
                            •	Uses gold and silver threads to create appliqué-like patterns.
                            •	Major centers: Jaipur and Khandela.
                        •	Karchobi Work:
                            •	A raised embroidery technique using flat stitches over cotton stuffing.
                            •	Resembles metallic Zari embroidery.
                    - Chikankari (Lucknow, Uttar Pradesh)
                        •	A delicate white-thread embroidery.
                        •	Used on fabrics like cotton, mulmul, polyester, and voile.
                        •	Defines the textile heritage of Lucknow.
                    - Kashida (Kashmir)
                        •	Famous for its fine needlework.
                        •	Typically embroidered on Cashmere shawls with floral and nature-inspired motifs.
                    - Banaras Brocade (Varanasi, Uttar Pradesh)
                        •	Known for gold and silver Zari embroidery on silk sarees.
                        •	Features floral motifs.
                        •	Takes two to eight weeks to complete a saree.
                    - Himroo (Aurangabad, Maharashtra)
                        •	Used historically in royal dresses and shawls during the medieval period.
                        •	Even today, Himroo shawls remain popular for their intricate weaving.
                    - Maheshwari Sarees (Madhya Pradesh)
                        •	Made using a blend of cotton and silk yarns.
                        •	Embellished with golden zari work.
                        •	Feature reversible borders, allowing them to be worn on both sides.
                    - Chamba Rumals (Himachal Pradesh)
                        •	Delicately embroidered handkerchiefs with themes from Krishna’s tales.
                        •	Influenced by Chamba Pahari paintings.
                        •	Commonly gifted during marriages.
                        •	Designs include trees, flowers, and nature motifs.
                    - Shisha or Mirror Work (Kutch, Gujarat)
                        •	Uses tiny mirrors sewn onto fabric with herringbone and satin stitches.
                        •	A signature embroidery style of Kutch.
                    - Muga Silk Embroidery (Manipur)
                        •	Features dark-colored muga silk thread embroidery.
                        •	Seen on phaneks (traditional shawls worn by Manipuri women during auspicious occasions like weddings).
                    - Kasuti (Karnataka)
                        •	A folk embroidery style that features geometrical motifs created using cross-stitch.
                        •	Handmade and possibly dates back to the Chalukyan era.

        - Ivory Carving
            - Basic info
                •	Practiced in India since the Vedic period, referred to as ‘danta’ (elephant tusk).
                •	Evidence of ivory exports from Harappan sites to Turkmenistan, Afghanistan, and the Persian Gulf.
                •	2nd-century BC inscription from Sanchi mentions ivory workers from Vidisha.
                •	Ivory artifacts from the Mughal period include combs, dagger handles, and ornaments.
            Traditional Centers
                •	Delhi, Jaipur, Murshidabad – Famous for ivory caskets, palanquins, and Ambari Hathi.
                •	Kerala – Known for painting on ivory.
                •	Jodhpur – Famous for ivory bangles.
                •	Jaipur – Known for ivory jali work used in home décor and small art objects.
            Begram Ivories (Afghanistan, 1st–2nd century AD)
                •	Decorative plaques, small figures, and carvings made of ivory and bone.
                •	Associated with Kushan Art.

        - Silver Crafts
            •	Filigree Work – Delicate silver wire designs used in jewelry and decorative pieces.
            •	Odisha – Famous for:
                •	Silver anklets (Painri and Paijam).
                •	Gunchi – Knitted silver ornaments.
            •	Bidri Work (Karnataka)
                •	Silver inlay work on dark metal backgrounds.
                •	Creates a striking contrast between silver and black.
                •	Recognized with GI (Geographical Indication) status.

        - Clay and Pottery Work
            - Basic info
                •	Called the “Lyric of Handicrafts” due to its artistic appeal.
                •	One of the oldest crafts, practiced since Neolithic times.
            Historical Evidence
                •	Mehrgarh (Pakistan, 6000 BC) – Earliest pottery evidence.
                •	Painted Grey Ware (1000–600 BC) – Common in the Vedic period.
                •	Red and Black Pottery (1500–300 BC) – Found in West Bengal.
                •	Northern Black Polished Ware (700–100 BC) – Associated with the Mauryan period.
                •	Roulette Pottery (200–100 BC) – Found in Arikamedu, near Puducherry.
            Later Developments
                •	Gupta period (4th century AD) – Introduction of embellishment, painting, and molding.
                •	Indo-Islamic period – Glazed pottery emerged.
                •	Present Day – Every region in India specializes in different types of clay crafts.

        - Bronze Crafts
            •	Ancient Usage
                •	Initially, metals were used for spears and arrows rather than art.
                •	Metal casting has been practiced for over 5000 years.
                •	The Dancing Girl of Mohenjo-daro (3500–3000 BC) is an early example of bronze work.
            •	Formation & Literary Evidence
                •	Bronze = Copper + Tin (one of the earliest non-ferrous alloys).
                •	Matsya Purana – Describes different bronze casting methods.
                •	Rasaratnakara – Mentions metal purity and zinc distillation.
            •	Major Bronze Craft Centers
                •	Uttar Pradesh (Etawah, Sitapur, Varanasi, Moradabad)
                    •	Produces decorative items (flower pots, deity statues).
                    •	Specializes in ritual objects (Tamrapatra, Kanchantal, Panchpatra).
                •	Tamil Nadu
                    •	Renowned for bronze statues inspired by the Pallava, Chola, Pandyan, and Nayaka periods.

            ---------------------------------------------- Table End ----------------------------------------------

        - Other Metal Crafts
            •	Techniques Used
                •	Metal casting can be done in iron, copper, bell metal, etc.
                •	Ornamentation techniques include:
                    •	Engraving – Scratching and cutting designs into metal.
                    •	Embossing (Repoussé) – Creating raised relief designs.
                    •	Damascening – Inlaying lighter metal on a darker base.
            •	Notable Metalwork Styles
                •	Marori Work (Rajasthan)
                    •	Etching on base metal with black lac filling.
                •	Tarkashi (Rajasthan)
                    •	Uses fine copper/brass wires to create intricate patterns.
                •	Koftgiri (Jaipur & Alwar, Rajasthan)
                    •	A type of damascening where light metal is inlaid on a dark base.
            •	Major Metal Craft Centers
                •	Marwar, Rajasthan
                    •	Produces Badla pots (semi-circular pots made of zinc).
                •	Moradabad, Uttar Pradesh
                    •	Specializes in Baarik Kaam (delicate metal engraving).
                    •	Uses Nakkashi style (engraving technique) to create pots.

        Leather Products
            •	Historical Background
                •	The art of leather tanning dates back to 3000 BC.
                •	Early tanned skins: Tiger and deer, later camel leather became popular.
                •	Rishis (sages) in ancient times used animal skins as mats.
                •	Leather was used during the Mughal period, but its degenerative nature makes archaeological evidence rare.
            •	Major Leather Craft Centers
                •	Rajasthan
                    •	Camel leather is used to make bags in various shapes and sizes.
                    •	Jaipur & Jodhpur – Famous for Mojaris (traditional leather footwear).
                •	Uttar Pradesh
                    •	Kanpur – A major center for leather and tanned products, sustaining a large part of its economy.
                •	Maharashtra
                    •	Known for Kolhapuri chappals, famous handcrafted leather footwear.
                •	Chennai & Kolkata
                    •	Important hubs for leather bags and footwear.
                •	Punjab
                    •	Punjabi Juttis – Embellished with appliqué work, making them highly decorative.
            •	Notable Leather Art
                •	Manoti Art (Bikaner, Rajasthan)
                    •	Involves decorating articles with camel skin.
                    •	Also called Usta Art, derived from the Persian word Ustad (Master).

        - Wooden Work
            •	Wood as a Resource
                •	India has a large forest cover, making wood a major resource.
                •	Furniture is one of the biggest commodities made from wood.
                •	Commonly used woods: Sheesham, pinewood, walnut, deodar, etc.
            •	Major Wooden Craft Centers
                •	Kashmir
                    •	Wooden craft flourished since the 11th century.
                    •	Uses walnut and deodar wood extensively.
                    •	Kashmiri artisans specialize in making wooden houses and houseboats, suited for the cold and wet climate.
                    •	Famous Kashmiri lattice work styles:
                        •	Acche-dar
                        •	Khatamband
                        •	Azli-pinjra
                •	Gujarat
                    •	Noted for wooden latticework on windows and wooden doors.
                •	Himachal Pradesh
                    •	Hilly areas with abundant wood excel in woodcarving techniques.
                    •	Towns like Brahmour and Chattrahi are known for intricate woodwork in temples.
                    •	Famous carving techniques:
                        •	Naghbel
                        •	Kutheriphool
                        •	Jali
                        •	Dori

        Various Types of Toys
            •	Historical Evidence of Toy Making
                •	Harappan Civilization:
                    •	Toy carts made of copper, bronze, and terracotta found in sites like:
                        •	Alamgirpur, Ambkheri, Kalibangan, Lothal
                •	Early Historic Period:
                    •	Similar cart models found in sites like:
                        •	Atranjikhera, Nagarjunakonda, Sambhar, Sonapur, Ujjain, Brahmpuri
            •	Materials Used in Ancient and Medieval Period
                •	Clay, paper, papier-mâché, painted wood, lacquered wood
                •	Redwood dolls, e.g., Tirupati dolls (Andhra Pradesh)
            •	Major Toy-Making Centers
                •	Rajasthan:
                    •	Famous for dolls and stuffed toys made of colorful cloth
                •	Assam:
                    •	Traditional dolls made of pith (Indian cork)
                •	Southern India:
                    •	Specializes in wooden toys
                •	Karnataka:
                    •	Mysore and Channapatna:
                        •	Known for lacquered wooden toys
                •	Andhra Pradesh:
                    •	Kondapalli (Ponki wood):
                        •	Famous for Ambari Hathi toy

        - Stoneware
            •	Ancient Stone Crafting in India
                •	Stone masonry and carving have been prevalent in India due to its tropical and geographical landscape.
                •	Artisans used various stones such as soft-brittle sandstone, patchy red stone, and hard granite to create intricate monuments.
            •	Examples of Ancient Stone Art
                •	South Indian towns: Renowned for exquisite stone structures.
                •	Mauryan Period:
                    •	Rock-cut caves of Ajanta and Ellora.
                    •	Erotic sculptures of Khajuraho.
                    •	Buddhist carvings of Sanchi and Bharhut.
                •	Hilly Regions:
                    •	Masrur rock-cut temples (Kangra, Himachal Pradesh, 8th century AD) showcase monolithic carving.
            •	Mughal Period: Shift to Marble Work
                •	Introduction of Pietra Dura (inlay work with colorful stones on marble).
                •	Famous marble structures:
                    •	Taj Mahal and Itmad-ud-Daulah’s tomb (white marble).
                    •	Use of sandstone in many Mughal monuments.
                •	Major marble-producing regions:
                    •	Rajasthan: Famous for Makrana marble (Sang-e-Marmar).
                    •	Jhansi, Uttar Pradesh: Produces dark brown stone (Sang-e-Rathak).
        - Floor Designs
            •	General Characteristics
                •	Found across many Indian states, made during religious and auspicious occasions.
                •	Usually drawn freehand, starting from a central dot, expanding into geometric shapes (circles, squares, triangles, straight lines, and curves).
                •	Made with natural materials that do not stain the floor and can be easily erased.
                    •	White color: Dry white chalk, lime powder, powdered marble, or a mix of rice powder and lime.
            •	Regional Variants
                •	Chowk Purna (Punjab, Uttar Pradesh)
                    •	Uses squares, circles, and triangles as motifs.
                    •	Derived from “Chowki” (seat of Lakshmi, goddess of wealth and prosperity).
                •	Aipan (Himachal Pradesh)
                    •	Similar in style to Chowk Purna.
                •	Mandana (Rajasthan, Madhya Pradesh)
                    •	Literally means “decoration”.
                    •	Patterns include squares, hexagons, triangles, and circles.
                    •	Ground is first cleaned with cow dung and sometimes finished with crimson red (reti or red earth).
                •	Santhias (Gujarat)
                    •	Made at the entrance of houses on important occasions.
                •	Rangoli (Maharashtra)
                    •	Features lotus, swastik, and elegant motifs.
                •	Kolam (South India)
                    •	Designs formed by connecting an array of dots in various combinations.
                    •	Drawn with powdered rice or crushed stone powder on wet ground.
                    •	Outlined with red geru.
                    •	Known as Hase (Karnataka), Muggulu (Andhra Pradesh), and Golam (Kerala).
                    •	Mandapa Kolam: Large designs made for weddings, drawn with wet rice paste.
                    •	Graha Kolam: Drawn daily for home sanctification.
                •	Jhonti (Odisha) & Aripana (West Bengal, Assam)
                    •	Highly stylized designs using motifs like conch shells, fish, serpents, flowers, etc.
                    •	Made with chalk powder and filled with colored powder or rice paste.
                    •	Red (Alta/Sindoor), Yellow (Turmeric).
                    •	Customarily, a flower is placed before each Aripana design.

- UNESCO’S LIST OF WORLD HERITAGE SITES IN INDIA
    - Criteria for Selection
        The nominated sites must be of “outstanding universal value” and should meet at least one of the ten criteria listed below.
        - Criteria for Cultural Sites
            - Represents a masterpiece of human creative genius.
            - Exhibits an important interchange of human values over time or within a cultural area, influencing developments in architecture, technology, monumental art, town planning, or landscape design.
            - Bears a unique or exceptional testimony to a cultural tradition or civilization, living or extinct.
            - An outstanding example of a building type, architectural or technological ensemble, or landscape illustrating a significant stage in human history.
            - An outstanding example of a traditional human settlement, land use, or sea use that represents a culture or human interaction with the environment, especially when threatened by irreversible change.
            - Is directly or tangibly associated with events, living traditions, beliefs, or artistic and literary works of outstanding universal significance.
        - Criteria for Natural Sites
            - Contains superlative natural phenomena or areas of exceptional natural beauty and aesthetic importance.
            - Represents major stages of Earth’s history, including the record of life, geological processes in landform development, or significant geomorphic/physiographic features.
            - An outstanding example of ecological and biological processes in the evolution of terrestrial, freshwater, coastal, and marine ecosystems, and plant and animal communities.
            - Contains the most important natural habitats for in-situ conservation of biodiversity, including those with threatened species of outstanding universal value.

    - Legal Status of Designated Sites
        Once a site is declared a World Heritage Site by UNESCO, it is recognized as culturally sensitive and warrants legal protection under international law, including:
            •	The Geneva Convention
            •	The Hague Convention for the Protection of Cultural Property in Armed Conflict
            •	Other international treaties
        - Article 53 of the Geneva Convention
            Protection of Cultural Objects and Places of Worship:
                •	It is prohibited to attack historic monuments, works of art, or places of worship that constitute cultural or spiritual heritage.
                •	These objects cannot be used for military purposes.
                •	They cannot be targeted as reprisals.

    - UNESCO World Heritage Sites in India

    - Benefits of World Heritage Site Status
        •	International recognition for preservation and conservation needs.
        •	Boosts tourism, ensuring economic benefits.
        •	UNESCO funding for restoration, preservation, and training.
        •	Strengthens ties with the United Nations, offering prestige and support.
        •	Provides access to global project management resources.
        •	Facilitates partnerships between government, private sector, and NGOs.
        •	Legal protection under the Geneva Convention against destruction or misuse during wartime.


PERFORMING ARTS

- INDIAN MUSIC
    - Introduction
        •	Music is the soul of any culture, and India has a long tradition of musical ingenuity.
        •	Narada Muni is said to have introduced music to Earth, teaching about Nada Brahma, the sound that pervades the universe.
        •	Musicologists suggest that “Om” is the source of all ragas and notes.
        •	Panini (4th century BC) made the first proper reference to music-making.
        •	Bharata’s Natyashastra (200 BC–200 AD) was the first text discussing musical theory.

	- History and Evolution of Indian Music
        •	Indian music dates back to the Indus Valley Civilization, where a seven-holed flute was found at excavation sites.
        •	The Ravanahatha, originating from Sri Lanka’s Hela Civilization, is one of the world’s oldest instruments.
        •	The first literary references appear 2,000 years ago in the Vedic period.
            •	The seven notes of raga Kharaharapriya appear in descending order in the Sama Veda.
            •	The science of music, Gandharva Veda, is an Upaveda of the Sama Veda.
            •	The Veena is mentioned in Aitareya Aranyaka, and Jaiminiya Brahmana speaks of dance and music together.
        •	Music evolved through devotional performances:
            •	In the later Vedic period, Sangama involved chanting verses to musical patterns.
            •	Epics were narrated with Jatigan music.
        •	Bharata’s Natyashastra identified the octave and its 22 shrutis (keys).
        •	Dattilam confirmed 22 shrutis per octave, suggesting they were the only ones a human could produce.
        •	Sarangadeva (13th century) supported this view in his Sangeet Ratnakara:
            •	Defined 264 ragas from both North Indian and Dravidian traditions.
            •	Classified microtones into different categories.
        •	Other medieval texts focused on specific aspects:
            •	Brihaddeshi (6th–8th century, Matanga) defined raga.
            •	Swaramela-Kalanidhi (16th century, Ramamatya) focused on ragas.
            •	Chaturdandi-Prakashika (17th century, Venkatamakhin) detailed Carnatic musicology.
        •	Persian influence reshaped North Indian music:
            •	Dhruvapad (devotional) transformed into Dhrupad by the 15th century.
            •	Khayal emerged by the 17th century.
            •	Many folk singing styles developed.

	- Gurukul System in Music Education
        •	Also known as the Ashram or hermitage system.
        •	Followed the Guru-Shishya tradition, emphasizing close teacher-student relationships.
        •	Music education in ancient times:
            •	Teachers were sages.
            •	Students lived in the hermitage for 12 years, gaining knowledge by serving the master.
        •	Patronized by kings and wealthy individuals.
        •	Life in the hermitage was rigorous, reflective, and based on direct experience.
        •	All students, regardless of status, were treated equally, with no discrimination.

    Anatomy of Indian Music
        Before one delves into the different types and kinds of Indian music it is necessary to understand the anatomy of Indian classical music. 
        There are three main pillars of Indian classical music:
            Swara
                •	In ancient times, “Swara” was associated with Vedic recitation. Over time, it came to define the note or scale degree in a composition.
                •	Bharata’s Natyashastra divided Swaras into a 22-note scale.
                •	The modern Hindustani notational system consists of: Sa, Re, Ga, Ma, Pa, Dha, Ni, collectively called Saptak or Sargam.
                •	Difference between Swara and Shruti:
                    •	A Shruti is the smallest pitch gradation, representing frequency quality.
                    •	There are 22 Shrutis, of which only 12 are audible (7 Suddha Swaras and 5 Vikrit Swaras).

                ---------------------------------------------- Table Start ----------------------------------------------
                He listed each pitch using the following names:
                    Name of the pitch Function

                    Abbreviation

                    Shadja

                    Tonic

                    Sa

                    Rishabha

                    Supertonic

                    Gandhara

                    Panchama

                    Dhaivata

                    Ni
                ---------------------------------------------- Table End ----------------------------------------------

            Raga
                - Basic info
                    •	The term Raga comes from Sanskrit Ranj, meaning “to delight or satisfy.”
                    •	Raga forms the basis of melody, while Tala governs rhythm.
                    •	Each raga evokes a distinct personality, mood, and emotion.
                Types of Ragas (Jaatis) Based on Notes:
                    •	Audav/Odava Raga 
                        - Five-note (pentatonic) raga.
                    •	Shadava Raga 
                        - Six-note (hexatonic) raga.
                    •	Sampurna Raga 
                        – Seven-note (heptatonic) raga.
                Classification of Ragas (Raga Bhed):
                    •	Shuddha Raag 
                        – Even if extra notes are played, the nature of the raga remains unchanged.
                    •	Chhayalag Raag 
                        – If extra notes are played, the raga’s character changes.
                    •	Sankeerna Raag 
                        – A combination of two or more ragas.
                Key Components of Raga:
                    •	Vaadi (King) 
                        – The principal note.
                    •	Samvaadi (Queen) 
                        – The second-most important note.
                    •	Anuvaadi 
                        – Supporting notes.
                    •	Vivadi 
                        – Notes absent in the composition.
                Movement of Notes:
                    •	Aroha (Ascent): 
                        Notes arranged in increasing pitch (e.g., Sa Re Ga Ma Pa Dha Ni).
                    •	Avaroha (Descent): 
                        Notes arranged in decreasing pitch (e.g., Ni Dha Pa Ma Ga Re Sa).
                Laya (Speed of Raga Performance):
                    1.	Vilambit 
                        – Slow tempo.
                    2.	Madhya 
                        – Medium tempo.
                    3.	Drut 
                        – Fast tempo.

                Although there are 72 melas or parent scales on which ragas are based under carnatic music, there are six main ragas under Hindustani music repertoire and all of these are time and season specific and evoke a particular type of emotion (Rasa):
                ---------------------------------------------- Table Start ----------------------------------------------
                    Raga

                    Time

                    Season

                    Mood (Rasa)

                RASA
                    Ragas evoke emotional responses in performers and audiences. 
                    These emotions are called Rasas or “Aesthetic Delights.” Originally, eight rasas were identified, but later, Shanta Rasa (peace) was added, forming the Navarasas (Nine Rasas).
                    
                    ---------------------------------------------- Table start ----------------------------------------------
                    These are:
                    Type of Rasa Mood it Evokes
                    Shringara

                    Love

                    Hasya

                    Humour/Laughter

                    Karuna

                    Pathos

                    Raudra

                    Anger

                    Bhayanak

                    Horror

                    Veer

                    Bravery

                    Adbhuta

                    Wonder

                    Bibhatsya

                    Disgust

                    Shant

                    Peace or calm
                    ---------------------------------------------- Table end ----------------------------------------------

                    •	After the 15th century, Bhakti (devotion to God) was widely accepted as an additional rasa. Some argue that Bhakti and Shant Rasa are the same.
                Samay (Time Theory of Ragas)
                    Each raga is performed at a specific time to enhance its effect.
                    Time classification:
                        •	12 AM – 12 PM: Poorva Bhaag (Morning to Noon) → Poorva Raga
                        •	12 PM – 12 AM: Uttar Bhaag (Afternoon to Night) → Uttar Raga
                    Saptak Shift by Time Period:
                        •	Poorvang Period (Morning–Noon): Sa to Ma (Sa, Re, Ga, Ma).
                        •	Uttarang Period (Afternoon–Night): Pa to Sa (Pa, Dha, Ni, Sa).
                Other Key Components of Raga Performance
                    - Alap 
                        – A slow exposition of the raga, emphasizing Vaadi and Samvaadi.
                    - Sthayee/Mukhda 
                        – The primary melody section, repeated frequently.
                    - Antara 
                        – The secondary melody section.
                    - Taan 
                        – Fast, intricate patterns of notes.
                        •	Short, fast taans with 3-4 notes are called Murki.
                    - Alankar (Ornamentation) 
                        – Pattern-based embellishment (e.g., Sa Re Ga, Ga Ma Pa).
            Tala (Rhythm in Indian Music)
                Basic info
                    •	Tala (or Taal) is the rhythmic structure that organizes beats into cycles.
                    •	Talas range from 3 to 108 beats.
                    •	Hindustani and Carnatic music follow different time measurement systems.
                    •	Tempo (Laya) of tala determines uniformity:
                        •	Slow (Vilambit)
                        •	Medium (Madhya)
                        •	Fast (Drut)
                Common Talas in Hindustani Music:
                    - Dadra
                    - Kaharba
                    - Rupak
                    - Ektal
                    - Jhaptal
                    - Teen-Taal (most used, 16 beats)
                    - Chautal
                Carnatic Tala System:
                    •	Three components: Laghu, Dhrutam, and Anu Dhrutam.
                    •	Originally 35 talas, each having 5 subtypes, leading to 175 talas.
                    •	Aditala is the most prominent tala in Carnatic music.
            Thaat (Classification of Ragas)
                •	Thaat is a system of organizing ragas into 10 categories, developed by V.N. Bhatkhande.
                •	Unlike Raga, Thaat is not sung—it serves as a structural framework.
                Rules for Thaat:
                    •	Must have 7 notes in ascending order (Aarohi).
                    •	Based on the 12 Swaras (7 Suddha + 5 Vikrit Swaras).
                10 Thaats in Hindustani Music:
                    - Bilawal
                    - Khamaj
                    - Kafi
                    - Asavari
                    - Bhairavi
                    - Bhairav
                    - Kalyan
                    - Marwa
                    - Poorvi
                    - Todi

    CLASSIFICATION OF INDIAN MUSIC
        There are many types of music prevalent in the Indian sub-continent that belongs to different categories. Some are closer to the classical bent and some are experimenting with the global music. Recently, there has been a trend to create a fusion of the classical heritage with newer musical strands like pop, jazz, etc. and this is garnering attention of the masses. The classification of

        Indian music is as follows:
            Classical Music
                Over time, two distinct schools of Indian classical music evolved:
                    Hindustani Music
                        - Basic info
                            •	Originates from Natyashastra.
                            •	Separated from Carnatic music in the 14th century.
                            •	Focuses on structure and improvisation.
                            •	Uses Shudha Swara Saptaka (Octave of Natural Notes).
                        •	Main styles:
                            •	Dhrupad
                                - Basic infor
                                    •	One of the oldest & grandest forms of Hindustani music.
                                    •	Mentioned in Natyashastra (200 BC–200 AD).
                                    •	Derived from Prabhanda and Dhruvapada.
                                    •	Name comes from dhruva (fixed) + pada (verse).
                                    •	Became classical in the 13th century.
                                    •	Reached its peak in Akbar’s court.
                                - Patronage
                                    •	Akbar’s court musicians:
                                        •	Tansen (one of Navratnas)
                                            •	16th-century musician in Akbar’s court.
                                            •	Title: Mian (learned man).
                                            •	Master of Dhrupad.
                                            •	Created several new ragas.
                                            •	Composed music on Hindu Gods, Goddesses, and Emperor Akbar.
                                            •	Tomb located in Gwalior.
                                        •	Baba Gopal Das
                                        •	Swami Haridas
                                    •	Baiju Bawra possibly sang at Akbar’s court.
                                    •	Practiced in Gwalior under Raja Man Singh Tomar.
                                    •	Major form of singing in the medieval period.
                                    •	Declined in the 18th century.
                                - Performance Style
                                    •	Begins with Alap (slow, wordless melody).
                                    •	Alap sets the mood of the raga.
                                    •	Tempo gradually increases.
                                    •	Dhrupad starts after Alap, with Pakhawaj accompaniment.
                                - Dhrupad Characteristics
                                    •	Temple origin.
                                    •	Uses Sanskrit syllables.
                                    •	Compositions have 4-5 stanzas.
                                    •	Performed by two male vocalists.
                                    •	Tanpura and Pakhawaj accompany.
                                - Dhrupad Gharanas
                                    - Dagari Gharana
                                        •	Dagar family sings Dagar Vani.
                                        •	Emphasizes Alap.
                                        •	Traditionally performed in pairs.
                                        •	Mostly Muslim singers but use Hindu texts.
                                        •	Famous duo: Gundecha Brothers.
                                    - Darbhanga Gharana
                                        •	Sings Khandar Vani & Gauhar Vani.
                                        •	Strong raga alap with layakari improvisation.
                                        •	Leading family: Mallik family (Ram Chatur Mallik, Prem Kumar Mallik, Siyaram Tewari).
                                    - Bettiah Gharana
                                        •	Performs Nauhar & Khandar Vani.
                                        •	Unique techniques only taught within families.
                                        •	Leading family: Mishra family.
                                        •	Connected with the Haveli style of Dhrupad.
                                    - Talwandi Gharana
                                        •	Sings Khandar Vani.
                                        •	Based in Pakistan, making it difficult to sustain in Indian music.
                                - Gharana System
                                    •	Gharana = musical lineage & style.
                                    •	From ghar (house/family).
                                    •	Influences performance, teaching, and appreciation.
                                    •	Major gharanas:
                                        •	Agra
                                        •	Gwalior
                                        •	Indore
                                        •	Jaipur
                                        •	Kirana
                                        •	Patiala
                            •	Khayal
                                - Basic info
                                    •	Derived from Persian Khyal = “idea” or “imagination”.
                                    •	Attributed to Amir Khusrau.
                                    •	More popular than Dhrupad due to improvisation flexibility.
                                    •	Short compositions (Bandish) of 2-8 lines.
                                    •	Patronized by Hussain Shah (Jaunpur Sultanate, 15th century).
                                    •	Frequent use of taans, less Alap than Dhrupad.
                                - Types of Khayal
                                    1.	Bada Khayal – slow tempo.
                                        •	Bada Khayal is the main part of a performance.
                                    2.	Chhota Khayal – fast tempo.
                                •	Themes: 
                                    - Love, devotion, praise of kings/Gods.
                                •	Famous compositions dedicated to Lord Krishna.
                                - Khayal Gharanas
                                    - Gwalior Gharana
                                        •	Oldest & most elaborate Khayal Gharana.
                                        •	Balanced approach to melody & rhythm.
                                        •	Prefers simple ragas.
                                        •	Exponents: Nathu Khan, Vishnu Paluskar.
                                    - Kirana Gharana
                                        •	Founded by Nayak Gopal, made popular by Abdul Karim Khan & Abdul Wahid Khan.
                                        •	Famous for slow tempo ragas.
                                        •	Focus on melody, pronunciation, & traditional ragas.
                                        •	Exponents: Bhimsen Joshi, Gangubai Hangal.
                                        - Pandit Bhimsen Joshi
                                            •	Major Khayal exponent of Kirana Gharana.
                                            •	Received Bharat Ratna (2008).
                                            •	Famous ragas: Suddha Kalyan, Miyan ki Todi, Multani, Bhimpalasi.
                                            •	Recognized for Mile Sur Mera Tumhara (1988).
                                    - Agra Gharana
                                        •	Established by Khudabuksh (19th century) or Haji Sujan Khan.
                                        •	Revived by Faiyyaz Khan, renamed Rangila Gharana.
                                        •	Blend of Khayal & Dhrupad-dhamar styles.
                                        •	Exponents: Mohsin Khan Niazi, Vijay Kichlu.
                                    - Patiala Gharana
                                        •	Founded by Bade Fateh Ali Khan & Ali Baksh Khan (19th century).
                                        •	Sponsored by Maharaja of Patiala.
                                        •	Famous for ghazal, thumri, Khayal.
                                        •	Focus on emotion & intricate taans.
                                        •	Exponent: Bade Ghulam Ali Khan (Raga Darbari expert).
                                    - Bhendibazaar Gharana
                                        •	Founded by Chhajju Khan, Nazir Khan, Khadim Hussain Khan (19th century).
                                        •	Unique long-breath singing technique.
                                        •	Incorporates Carnatic ragas.
                            •	Tarana
                                •	Rhythm-centric style.
                                •	Short melody repeated with variations.
                                •	Uses fast-paced syllables.
                                •	Requires advanced rhythmic skills.
                                •	Invented by Amir Khusrau (13th-14th century).
                                •	Used by Guru Gobind Singh.
                                •	Fastest Tarana Singer: Pandit Rattan Mohan Sharma (Mewati Gharana).
                                •	Given the title “Tarana ke Baadshah” at Pandit Motiram Sangeet Samaroh (Hyderabad).

                        Semi-Classical Styles of Hindustani Music
                            - Basic info
                                Semi-classical music is based on swara (notes) but deviates slightly from the strict structure of a raga. 
                                It uses lighter versions of ragas like Bhoopali or Malkaush and employs madhyam or dhrut laya (faster tempo). 
                                Unlike classical music, it focuses more on bhava (expression) and lyrics rather than alap-jor-jhala.
                            - Prominent Semi-Classical Styles                                
                                Thumri
                                    •	Based on mixed ragas and considered semi-classical.
                                    •	Compositions are romantic or devotional, often inspired by the Bhakti movement.
                                    •	Lyrics often depict a girl’s love for Krishna.
                                    •	Language: Braj Bhasha dialect of Hindi.
                                    •	Usually sung in a female voice and characterized by sensuality.
                                    •	Allows for improvisation with flexible raga usage.
                                    •	Linked to the classical dance Kathak.
                                    •	Other lighter forms: Dadra, Hori, Kajari, Saavan, Jhoola, Chaiti.
                                    •	Major gharanas: Varanasi & Lucknow.
                                    •	Famous exponents: Begum Akhtar, Girija Devi (Purab Ang of Benaras Gharana).
                                Tappa
                                    •	Rhythm plays a crucial role.
                                    •	Characterized by fast, subtle, and knotty phrases.
                                    •	Originated from folk songs of camel riders in North-West India.
                                    •	Gained recognition in the Mughal court of Muhammad Shah.
                                    •	Popular among both elites and common classes.
                                    •	Baithaki style evolved in elite assembly halls (baithak khanas, jalsaghar).
                                    •	Now rare, few musicians practice it today.
                                    •	Famous exponents: Laxman Rao Pandit (Gwalior Gharana), Shanno Khurana.
                                Ghazal
                                    •	A poetic form with rhyming couplets and a refrain.
                                    •	Expresses love, pain, and separation.
                                    •	Origin: Iran (10th century AD).
                                    •	Spread to South Asia in the 12th century, flourished in the Mughal period.
                                    •	Amir Khusrau (1253–1325) was one of the earliest exponents.
                                    •	Many ghazal poets were Sufis or influenced by Sufi philosophy.
                                    •	Themes: Love (divine or human), often open to interpretation.
                                    •	Modern ghazals use simplified language for a wider audience.
                                    •	Famous poets: Muhammad Iqbal, Mirza Ghalib, Rumi, Hafez, Kazi Nazrul Islam.

                    Carnatic Music
                        - Basic info
                            Carnatic music is played in the traditional octave and is kriti-based, focusing more on saahitya (lyrics). 
                            The kriti is a highly evolved musical song set to a specific raga and a fixed tala (rhythmic cycle).        
                            Purandara Dasa (1484-1564) is regarded as the Pitamaha (father/grandfather) of Carnatic music.
                        Structure of a Carnatic Composition
                            •	Pallavi:
                                •	The first or second thematic line of the composition.
                                •	Repeated in each stanza.
                                •	Considered the highlight of Carnatic music, especially in Ragam Thanam Pallavi, allowing great improvisation.
                            •	Anu Pallavi:
                                •	Two lines that follow the Pallavi.
                                •	Sung at the beginning and sometimes at the end.
                                •	Not necessarily repeated after every stanza (Charanam).
                            •	Charanam:
                                •	The final and longest verse that concludes the song.
                        Other Components
                            •	Swara-Kalpana: Improvised section performed with the drummer in medium and fast paces.
                            •	Tanam: A melodic improvisation in free rhythm with mridangam.
                            •	Ragam: Similar to Tanam but without mridangam.
                        Early Proponents of Carnatic Music
                            Annamacharya (1408-1503)
                                •	First known composer of Carnatic music.
                                •	Composed sankirtanas in praise of Lord Venkateswara (a form of Lord Vishnu).
                                •	Compositions were mainly in Telugu.
                                •	Recognized as the Grandfather of Telugu Songwriting.
                            Purandara Dasa (1484-1564)
                                •	One of the founding proponents of Carnatic music.
                                •	A devotee of Lord Krishna.
                                •	Known as Pitamaha (father/grandfather) of Carnatic Music.
                                •	Believed to be an avatar of Sage Narada.
                                •	Famous compositions include Dasa Sahithya.
                            Kshetrayya (1600-1680)
                                •	Telugu poet and prominent composer.
                                •	Composed Padams and Keertanas, mainly based on Lord Krishna.
                                •	Traveled extensively.
                                •	His Padams are still performed in Bharatanatyam and Kuchipudi.
                            Bhadrachala Ramadasu (1620-1680)
                                •	His compositions were mainly in praise of Lord Rama.
                                •	Wrote primarily in Telugu.
                                •	One of the famous Vaggeyakaras (composers who write lyrics and set them to music).
                                •	Other famous Telugu Vaggeyakaras: Annamacharya, Tyagaraja, Syama Sastri.
                        Trinity of Carnatic Music (All Born in Tiruvarur)
                            Tyagaraja
                                •	Composed mostly in Telugu, a few in Sanskrit, praising Lord Rama.
                                •	Famous for Pancharatna Kritis.
                                •	A crater on Mercury is named after him.
                                •	Created several new ragas.
                            Muthuswami Dikshitar
                                •	Oldest among the Trinity.
                                •	Compositions were mostly in Sanskrit, centered on Hindu gods and temples.
                                •	Emphasized gamakas (ornamentations).
                                •	Signature mudra (name): Guru-Guha.
                                •	Skilled Veena player.
                            Syama Sastri
                                •	Composed mainly in Telugu, praising Goddess Kamakshi.
                                •	Wrote fewer kritis compared to the other two.
                                •	Signature mudra: Syama Krishna.
                                •	Associated with the Svarajati musical genre.

                    ---------------------------------------------- Table end ----------------------------------------------

            Folk Music in India
                India’s geographical diversity is reflected in its rich folk music traditions, with each state having its own distinct style.
                Characteristics of Indian Folk Music
                    •	Unlike classical music, which follows the Natyashastra and guru-shishya (student-mentor) tradition, folk music is the music of the people with no rigid rules.
                    •	Based on diverse themes, often rhythmic and dance-oriented.
                    •	Each state has its own folk music traditions, reflecting its culture and history.
                Folk Music by Region
                    North India
                        Wanawan (Jammu and Kashmir)
                            •	Sung during wedding ceremonies.
                            •	Considered auspicious.
                        Alha (Madhya Pradesh)
                            •	A heroic ballad with intricate lyrics.
                            •	Sung in Braj, Awadhi, and Bhojpuri.
                            •	Related to Mahabharata, glorifying heroes as reincarnations of the Pandavas.
                            •	The five Pandavas are represented as Alha, Udal, Malkhan, Lakhan, and Deva.
                        Pai Song (Madhya Pradesh)
                            •	Sung during festivals, especially monsoon-related festivals.
                            •	A prayer for good rainfall and harvest, sung by farmer communities.
                            •	Often accompanied by the Saira dance.
                    West India
                        Lavani (Maharashtra)
                            •	A famous folk music and dance form.
                            •	Performed to the beats of Dholki (percussion instrument).
                            •	Known for strong rhythm and energetic performances.
                        Ovi (Maharashtra, Goa)
                            •	Sung by women during household chores and leisure time.
                            •	Short, four-line poetic songs.
                            •	Themes include marriages, pregnancy, and lullabies.
                        Powada (Maharashtra)
                            •	Ballads narrating the heroic deeds of past warriors.
                            •	Often sung in praise of Shivaji.
                        Mando (Goa)
                            •	A blend of Indian and Western musical traditions.
                            •	Uses guitar, violin, and ghumot drum.
                    East India
                        Baul (West Bengal, Assam, Tripura)
                            •	A Bengali religious sect as well as a musical tradition.
                            •	Baul Sangeet is influenced by Hindu Bhakti and Sufi traditions.
                            •	Represents mysticism through songs.
                            •	Notable exponents: Yotin Das, Purno Chandra Das, Lalon Fakir, Naboni Das, Sanatan Das Thakur Baul.
                    South India
                        Kolannalu / Kolattam (Andhra Pradesh)
                            •	A music and dance combination, similar to Dandiya.
                            •	Ancient stick dance involving rhythmic movements in two circles.
                        Bhavageete (Karnataka, Maharashtra)
                            •	Emotional songs, musically similar to Ghazals.
                            •	Sung at a slower pitch.
                            •	Themes include nature, love, and philosophy.
                    Central & North-Eastern India
                        Pandavani (Chhattisgarh)
                            •	Based on the Mahabharata, focusing on Bhima as the hero.
                            •	Includes gayan (singing) and vadan (playing instruments).
                            •	Accompanied by tambura.
                            •	Famous artist: Teejan Bai (Padma Shri 1987, Padma Bhushan 2003, Padma Vibhushan 2019).
                        Khongjom Parba (Manipur)
                            •	A ballad narrating the Battle of Khongjom (1891).
                            •	Describes the struggle between British and Manipuri forces.
                    Western India – Rajasthan & Gujarat
                        Panihari (Rajasthan)
                            •	Thematically related to water.
                            •	Depicts women fetching water and village life.
                            •	Sometimes romantic or about mother-in-law vs. daughter-in-law conflicts.
                        Maand (Rajasthan)
                            •	Developed in royal courts, also recognized in classical circles.
                            •	Lies between Ragas and folk songs.
                            •	Sung by bards praising Rajput rulers.
                            •	Famous song: Kesariya Balam (in Maand style).
                        Dandiya Raas (Gujarat)
                            •	Associated with Holi and Krishna-Radha’s Lila in Vrindavan.
                            •	Main dance of Navratri celebrations.
                            •	In Dandiya Raas, men and women dance in two circles with sticks.
                            •	Related form: Garba (Raas Garba).

            Fusion of Classical and Folk Music
                Basic info
                    Over time, classical and folk music have intermingled, giving rise to new musical forms that incorporate elements from both traditions. 
                    Devotional music has played a major role in this fusion, as it was patronized by both royalty and commoners, leading to a blend of structured classical elements and free-flowing folk traditions.
                Major Forms of Classical-Folk Fusion Music
                    - Bhajan (North India)
                        •	Originated from the Bhakti movement, where saints spread the message of God through hymns.
                        •	Lyrics are set to simple melodies and sung in one or more ragas.
                        •	Themes: Stories from the Mahabharata, Ramayana, and lives of deities.
                        •	Instruments: Chimta, Dholak, Dafli, Manjira.
                        •	Major composers: Mirabai, Tulsidas, Surdas, Kabir.
                        •	Famous singers: Anup Jalota, Anuradha Paudwal.
                    - Shabad (Punjab, Sikh Tradition)
                        •	Devotional songs sung in Gurudwaras, dedicated to Sikh Gurus.
                        •	Developed by: Guru Nanak and his disciple Mardana.
                        •	Types:
                            •	Raga-based Shabad singing.
                            •	Traditional Shabad (from Adi Granth).
                            •	Lighter versions for general audiences.
                        •	Guru Granth Sahib is organized into ragas, each containing multiple Shabads.
                    - Qawwali (Sufi Tradition)
                        •	Devotional music in praise of Allah, Prophet Muhammad, or Sufi saints.
                        •	Sung in Urdu, Punjabi, Hindi, with some Brajbhasha and Awadhi words.
                        •	Performed in Sufi shrines, often with intense build-up leading to a transcendental state.
                        •	Instruments: Tabla, Dholak, Harmonium.
                        •	Notable Qawwals: Sabri Brothers, Nusrat Fateh Ali Khan, Aziz Warsi.
                        •	Possible Originator: Amir Khusrau (disputed claim).
                        •	Amir Khusrau’s Contribution
                            •	Invented the Sehtar, a three-stringed instrument.
                    - Rabindra Sangeet (Bengal)
                        •	Composed by Rabindranath Tagore.
                        •	A blend of classical elements and Bengali folk strains.
                        •	Themes: Worship, devotion to nature, beauty, love, life, and patriotism.
                        •	More than 2,000 compositions are still performed today.
                    - Haveli Sangeet (Rajasthan, Gujarat)
                        •	Originally performed inside temples but later moved to public performances.
                        •	Practiced by Pushtimarg Sampradaya, a community believing in Pushtimarg as a path to salvation.
