    Musical Instruments
        Music is created using various instruments, which can be classified into four major traditional categories based on their characteristics.
            - Avanaddha Vadya (Membranophones)    
                These instruments have an outer membrane that is beaten to produce sound. 
                They are also known as percussion instruments as they require striking to generate music.
                    •	They usually have one or two faces covered with skin.
                    •	The Bhumi Dundubhi (earth drum) is the most ancient instrument in this category.
                    •	Common examples: Tabla, Drum, Dhol, Congo, Mridangam.
                        •	Tabla is a key accompaniment in Hindustani classical music.
                        •	Mridangam is used in Carnatic music performances.
            - Sushira Vadya (Aerophones - Wind Instruments)
                These instruments produce sound through air vibration.
                    •	Common examples: Bansuri (Flute), Shehnai, Pungi, Ninkirns.
                    •	Shehnai is a double-reeded wind instrument.
                        •	Ustad Bismillah Khan is considered the Shehnai King.
                    •	Flute (Bansuri) is widely used and has been revered since the Vedic period.
                        •	Lord Krishna’s flute is an iconic symbol in Hindu culture.
                        •	Pandit Hariprasad Chaurasia is one of the most famous Indian flutists.
            - Ghana Vadya (Idiophones - Percussion Without Membranes)    
                These are non-drum percussion instruments that do not require tuning.
                    •	Examples: Manjira, Jaltarang, Kanch-tarang, Ghungroo, Ghatam, Khartal.
                    •	Manjira (Jhanj) is a small brass cymbal used in religious ceremonies.
                        •	Harappan Civilization excavations show the use of Manjira in ancient India.
                    •	These instruments are primarily used for keeping rhythm and time.
            - Tata Vadya (Chordophones - String Instruments)
                These instruments produce sound through vibrating strings, modified by hand.
                Types of Tata Vadya:
                    - Bowed Instruments: Played using a bow.
                        •	Examples: Sarangi, Esraj/Dilruba, Violin.
                    - Plectral Instruments: Strings are plucked using fingers or a plectrum.
                        •	Examples: Sitar, Veena, Tanpura, Guitar.
                    - Struck Instruments: Played using small hammers or sticks.
                        •	Examples: Gotuvadyam, Swaramandal.
                - Additional information
                    •	Sarod: 
                        The Bhangash family pioneered its 20th-century development.
                    •	Sitar: 
                        Played across various gharanas (Jaipur, Varanasi, Etawah/Imaad Khani).
                    •	Veena: 
                        One of the most ancient instruments, associated with Goddess Saraswati.
                        Santoor
                            •	A 100-string instrument, traditional to Jammu and Kashmir.
                            •	Commonly used in Sufiana Kalam music.
        Folk Musical Instruments
            Apart from classical instruments, India has a rich tradition of folk instruments, categorized into four types:
                - Chordophones (String Instruments)
                    •	Tumbi – Played during Bhangra in Punjab.
                    •	Ektara (Tun Tuna) – A one-stringed instrument played by wandering monks.
                    •	Dotara – A two-stringed instrument used by Bauls of Bengal.
                    •	Chikara – A bowed instrument used in Rajasthan, Uttar Pradesh, and Madhya Pradesh.
                    •	Dilruba/Esraj – Used in Punjab and Rabindra Sangeet (Eastern India).
                    •	Onavillu – A bamboo instrument from Kerala.
                    •	Sarinda – A tribal instrument used by Santhals in Eastern India, Rajasthan, and Assam, similar to the Sarangi.
                - Aerophones (Wind Instruments)
                    •	Pungi (Been) – Used by snake charmers, made from a dried bottle gourd and bamboo.
                    •	Algoza – A double flute, popular in North-West India, especially Punjab.
                    •	Tangmuri – A folk instrument of the Khasi people in Meghalaya.
                    •	Titti – A bagpipe-like instrument made from goat skin, used in Kerala and Andhra Pradesh.
                    •	Mashak – A folk bagpipe used in Garhwal (Uttarakhand), Rajasthan, and Uttar Pradesh.
                    •	Gogona – A bamboo instrument used in the Bihu festival of Assam.
                - Membranophones (Drum-Based Instruments)
                    •	Ghumot – A drum played during Ganesh festival in Goa.
                    •	Idakka – A damru-like instrument from Kerala.
                    •	Udukai – An hourglass-shaped instrument like damru, from Tamil Nadu.
                    •	Sambal – A drum played with sticks in the Konkan region of Maharashtra.
                    •	Tamak – A two-headed drum used by the Santhal tribe.
                    •	Diggi – A folk drum from Ghariya village, Uttar Pradesh.
                - Idiophones (Self-Sounding Percussion Instruments)
                    •	Chimta – Evolved from fire tongs, used in Punjab.
                    •	Gharha – Earthen pots, used in folk music of Punjab.
                    •	Andelu – Used in Burra-Katha, made of hollow metal rings.

    Modern Developments in Music
        - Basic info
            Music has been evolving continuously since its inception in the early historic period. 
            The 21st century has seen significant advancements in the music industry, including the establishment of institutions that teach music academically and provide theoretical knowledge. 
            Additionally, there has been a rise in organizations that showcase artists’ talent to the public through open performances.
        Key Developments
            - Gandharva Mahavidyalaya
                •	Founded by V.D. Paluskar in 1901 to preserve and teach Indian classical music and dance.
                •	Originally established in Lahore, later shifted to Mumbai in 1915.
                •	Focuses on both Hindustani and Carnatic classical music.
            - Prayag Sangeet Samiti
                •	Established in 1926 in Allahabad (now Prayagraj).
                •	Dedicated to imparting education in Hindustani classical music.
            - Sangeet Natak Akademi
                •	Founded in 1952 as India’s first national academy for performing arts.
                •	Established by the Government of India to promote music, drama, and dance.
                •	Serves as the primary body for showcasing performing arts and preserving India’s intangible heritage.
            - Marris College of Music (Bhatkhande Music Institute Deemed University)
                •	Founded in 1926 by legendary musicologist Vishnu Narayan Bhatkhande in Lucknow.
                •	One of India’s premier institutions for classical music.
                •	Focuses on both theory and practical training in singing and playing instruments.
                •	Later renamed Bhatkhande Music Institute Deemed University.
            - SPIC MACAY (Society for Promotion of Indian Classical Music and Culture Amongst Youth)
                •	Founded in 1977 by Kiran Seth as a voluntary youth movement.
                •	Aims to revive interest in Indian classical music, dance, yoga, meditation, and cultural heritage.
                •	Organizes free-entry events to engage young audiences.
                •	Has expanded into a global organization with multiple chapters.
                •	Headquarters: Delhi.
            - Chaturprahar Festival
                •	An annual Indian classical music festival held at the National Centre for Performing Arts (NCPA), Mumbai.
                •	Based on the concept of time association with ragas, where specific ragas are performed according to the time of the day.

- INDIAN DANCE FORMS
    Background
        •	Natya Veda: 
            Created by Lord Brahma as the fifth Veda, combining elements from the four Vedas:
            •	Pathya (words) – from the Rig Veda
            •	Abhinaya (gestures) – from the Yajur Veda
            •	Geet (music) – from the Sam Veda
            •	Rasa (emotions) – from the Atharva Veda
        •	Sacred Importance of Dance:
            •	Shiva’s Tandava symbolizes creation, preservation, and destruction.
            •	Parvati’s response represents grace and harmony.
            •	Dance is deeply rooted in Indian mythology.
        •	Historical Evidence:
            •	Bhimbetka cave paintings depict community dances.
            •	Bronze Dancing Girl (Harappan Civilization) highlights dance as a social activity.
        •	Natya Shastra (200 BCE – 200 CE):
            •	The first formal reference to Indian dance.
            •	A comprehensive treatise detailing:
                •	Postures, techniques, emotions, and expressions.
                •	Ornaments, costumes, stage setup, and audience engagement.
            •	Describes dance as the “complete art”, integrating music, sculpture, poetry, and drama.

    Aspects of Dance
        According to Natya Shastra, Indian classical dance has two fundamental aspects:
            •	Lasya 
                – Represents grace, bhava (expression), rasa (emotion), and abhinaya (acting).
                •	Symbolizes the feminine elements of dance.
            •	Tandava 
                – Focuses on rhythm and movement.
                •	Symbolizes the masculine aspects of dance.
        According to Abhinaya Darpan (5th–4th century BCE) by Nandikeshwara, a dance performance consists of three key elements:
            •	Nritta 
                – Pure rhythmic dance steps with no expression or storytelling.
            •	Natya 
                – Dramatic representation, where a story is conveyed through dance.
            •	Nritya 
                – Expressive dance involving sentiment and emotions through mudras (hand gestures) and facial expressions.
        Nayaka-Nayika Bhav
            •	The deity (Nayaka - hero) is the eternal subject of devotion.
            •	The devotee (Nayika - heroine) expresses love and surrender through dance.
        Nine Rasas (Emotions) Expressed in Dance
            •	Shringara – Love
            •	Roudra – Anger
            •	Bibhatsa – Disgust
            •	Veera – Heroism
            •	Shaant – Peace & Tranquility
            •	Haasya – Laughter & Comedy
            •	Karuna – Tragedy
            •	Bhayanak – Horror
            •	Adbhuta – Wonder
        Mudras (Hand Gestures & Postures)
            •	108 fundamental mudras are used in various combinations to depict specific emotions and expressions in dance.

    Indina classical dance forms
        •	Evolution of Indian Dance Styles:
            •	Different regions of India have developed distinct dance styles, each with unique nuances.
            •	Despite variations, all dance forms follow the fundamental rules of the Natya Shastra.
        •	Guru-Shishya Parampara:
            •	True knowledge transfer occurs only through a guru.
            •	The guru imparts knowledge of different traditions (sampradayas) to the disciple.
            •	This tradition forms the core of Indian classical dance.
        •	Eight Classical Dance Forms (As per Sangeet Natak Akademi):
            •	Bharatanatyam
                - Basic info
                    •	Oldest among all classical dance forms.
                    •	The name derives from Bharata Muni and Natyam (Tamil for dance).
                    •	Some scholars attribute Bharata to:
                        •	Bhava (expression)
                        •	Raga (melody)
                        •	Tala (rhythm)
                •	Origins and Revival:
                    •	Evolved from Sadir, a solo dance performed by Devadasis in Tamil Nadu.
                    •	Previously known as Dashiattam.
                    •	Declined with the end of the Devadasi system.
                    •	Revived by E. Krishna Iyer, a freedom fighter.
                    •	Gained global recognition through Rukmini Devi Arundale.
                    •	Originally performed by solo female dancers, now popular among male and group artists.
                •	Tanjore Quartet and the Bharatnatyam Recital:
                    •	Four dance teachers from Thanjavur (Chinnaiah, Ponniah, Vadivelu, Shivanandam) structured the recital.
                    •	Bharatnatyam is also called Tanjore Natyam in their honor.
                •	Elements of a Bharatnatyam Recital:
                    •	Alarippu: 
                        Invocatory piece with rhythmic syllables, seeking divine blessings.
                    •	Jatiswaram: 
                        Pure dance (Nritta), focusing on poses and movements without expressions.
                    •	Shabdam: 
                        Expressive (Abhinaya) piece, usually in praise of God.
                    •	Varnam: 
                        Combination of dance and emotions (Nritya), most important part of the performance.
                    •	Padam: 
                        Mastery of Abhinaya (expressions) with a spiritual message.
                    •	Jawali: 
                        Short love-lyrics performed at a fast tempo.
                    •	Thillana: 
                        Concluding pure dance (Nritta) with intricate rhythmic variations.
                •	Distinct Features of Bharatnatyam:
                    •	Known as the “fire dance”, movements resemble a dancing flame.
                    •	Balances both Tandava (vigorous) and Lasya (graceful) aspects.
                    •	Emphasizes Mudras (hand gestures), especially Katakamukha Hasta, symbolizing “Om”.
                    •	Characterized by Ekacharya Lasyam, where one dancer plays multiple roles.
                    •	Performed with bent knees and equal weight distribution on both feet.
                •	Famous Proponents:
                    •	Yamini Krishnamurthy, Lakshmi Viswanathan, Padma Subramaniam, Mrinalini Sarabhai, Mallika Sarabhai, etc.
            •	Kuchipudi
                - Origin and Evolution
                    •	Originated as a group performance by Kusselavas, traveling actors.
                    •	Named after the village Kusselavapuri (or Kuchelapuram) in Andhra Pradesh.
                    •	Systematized in the 17th century by Siddhendra Yogi, who authored Bhama Kalapam and other plays.z
                    •	Historical Development:
                        •	Influenced by Vaishnavism, became a male Brahmin monopoly and temple performance.
                        •	Bhagavata Purana became the central theme, and performers were called Bhagavathalus.
                        •	Flourished under Vijayanagara and Golconda rulers but remained confined to villages.
                        •	Revived in the 20th century by Balasaraswati and Ragini Devi.
                        •	Lakshminarayan Sastri introduced solo performances and female participation.
                •	Key Features of Kuchipudi:
                    •	Involves intricate foot movements and is primarily a group performance.
                    •	Recitals are based on Bhagavata Purana but often have secular themes, emphasizing Shringara Rasa.
                    •	Each principal character introduces itself with a Daaru (a short dance-song composition).
                    •	Incorporates all three classical dance elements:
                        •	Nritta (pure dance)
                        •	Natya (dramatic element)
                        •	Nritya (expressive dance)
                    •	Similar to Bharatanatyam but distinct in style.
                •	Elements of Performance:
                    •	Sollakath (Patakshara): Nritta component, focusing on body movements.
                    •	Kavutvams: Nritya component, involving acrobatics; sometimes performed as Nritta.
                    •	Symbolizes the manifestation of earthly elements in the human body.
                    •	Combines dance and drama, with dancers sometimes singing as well.
                    •	Balances both Lasya (graceful) and Tandava (vigorous) elements.
                •	Solo Performance Elements:
                    •	Manduk Shabdam: Story of a frog.
                    •	Tarangam: Dancer performs on the edge of a brass plate while balancing a pot of water or lit lamps.
                    •	Jala Chitra Nrityam: Dancer draws patterns on the floor with their toes while dancing.
                •	Music and Accompaniment:
                    •	Performed with Carnatic music, mainly in Telugu.
                    •	Principal instruments: Violin and Mridangam.
                •	Famous Proponents:
                    •	Radha Reddy, Raja Reddy, Yamini Krishnamurthy, Indrani Rahman, etc.
            •	Kathakali
                •	Origins & Evolution:
                    •	Developed from Ramanattam and Krishnattam, two dance-dramas of Kerala.
                    •	Patronized by feudal lords, narrating episodes from Ramayana and Mahabharata.
                    •	Closely linked to Koodiyattam (Sanskrit drama tradition) and ancient martial arts.
                    •	Combination of music, dance, and drama.
                    •	Declined with the fall of feudalism but revived in the 1930s by V. N. Menon under Mukunda Raja’s patronage.
                •	Key Features:
                    •	Performed by an all-male troupe.
                    •	Minimal props but elaborate facial makeup and headgear:
                        •	Green: Nobility, divinity, virtue.
                        •	Red patches near the nose: Royalty.
                        •	Black: Evil, wickedness.
                        •	Yellow: Saints, women.
                        •	Completely red face: Evil.
                        •	White beard: Higher consciousness, divinity.
                    •	Intertwined dance and drama—not clearly separable.
                    •	Represents the eternal conflict between good and evil, drawing from epics and puranas.
                    •	Known as the Ballad of the East.
                    •	Language: Manipravalam (a mix of Malayalam and Sanskrit).
                    •	Music: Crucial for conveying drama; different compositions add depth.
                    •	Gestures:
                        •	Key feature of Navarasas (nine facial expressions) expressed through eye and eyebrow movements.
                        •	Extensive hand gestures require rigorous training.
                    •	Performance Setting:
                        •	Open-air theatres or temple premises with lush greenery as the backdrop.
                        •	Lighting: Traditional brass lamps.
                    •	Symbolism: Represents the element of sky/ether.
                    •	Performance begins and ends with drumbeats from Chhenda and Maddala.
                    •	Famous Proponents:
                        •	Guru Kunchu Kurup, Gopi Nath, Kottakal Sivaraman, Rita Ganguly, etc.
            •	Mohiniattam
                •	Origins & Evolution:
                    •	Mohini means beautiful woman and Attam means dance.
                    •	Solo female dance form, further developed by Vadivelu in the 19th century.
                    •	Gained prominence under Travancore rulers, especially Swathi Thirunal.
                    •	Revived by V. N. Menon and Kalyani Amma.
                •	Key Features:
                    •	Blends the grace of Bharatanatyam with the vigour of Kathakali.
                    •	Gentle footwork, no forceful stamping.
                    •	Narrates the feminine dance of Vishnu.
                    •	Combines Nritta and Nritya like other classical dances.
                    •	Dominated by Lasya (beauty & grace), making it primarily a female dance form.
                    •	Accompanied by music & songs.
                    •	Costume:
                        •	White & off-white with gold brocade designs.
                        •	No elaborate facial makeup.
                        •	Ankle bells (Ghungroo) attached to a leather strap.
                    •	Symbolism: Represents the element of air.
                    •	Movements:
                        •	Atavakul (Atavus): A collection of 40 basic movements.
                    •	Musical Accompaniment:
                        •	Instruments: Cymbals, Veena, Drums, Flute, etc.
                •	Famous Proponents:
                    •	Sunanda Nair, Kalamandalam Kshemavathy, Madhuri Amma, Jayaprabha Menon, etc.
            •	Odissi
                •	Origins & Evolution:
                    •	Oldest classical dance from Eastern India.
                    •	Earliest evidence found in Udayagiri-Khandagiri caves.
                    •	Mentioned in Natya Shastra as Odra Nritya.
                    •	Practiced by Mahris (temple dancers), patronized by Jain King Kharavela.
                    •	Vaishnavism led to the decline of the Mahari system; replaced by Gotipuas (young boys dressed as females).
                    •	Nartala variant continued in royal courts.
                    •	Gained international acclaim in the mid-20th century due to Charles Fabri and Indrani Rahman.
                •	Key Features:
                    •	Similar to Bharatanatyam in using Mudras & postures for expression.
                    •	Signature Postures:
                        •	Tribhanga: Three-bent posture.
                        •	Chowk: Squared stance, symbolizing masculinity.
                    •	Movements:
                        •	Lower body remains static, while torso moves gracefully.
                        •	Hand gestures convey expressions in Nritya.
                    •	Known as ‘Mobile Sculpture’ due to its intricate body shapes & patterns.
                •	Elements of Odissi:
                    •	Mangalacharan: Begins with an offering of flowers to Mother Earth.
                    •	Batu Nritya: Includes Tribhanga & Chowk postures.
                    •	Pallavi: Expresses the essence of the song.
                    •	Tharijham: A pure dance section before the conclusion.
                    •	Two types of concluding sequences:
                        •	Moksha: Joyful movements signifying liberation.
                        •	Trikhanda Majura: Performer bids farewell to the gods, audience, and stage.
                •	Music & Accompaniment:
                    •	Hindustani Classical Music.
                    •	Instruments: Manjira (Cymbals), Pakhawaj (Drums), Sitar, Flute, etc.
                    •	Lyrics: Gita Govinda by Jayadeva and compositions by local poets.
                •	Symbolism: Represents the element of water.
                •	Costume & Jewelry:
                    •	Elaborate hairstyles, silver jewelry, long necklaces.
                •	Famous Proponents:
                    •	Guru Pankaj Charan Das, Guru Kelu Charan Mohapatra, Sonal Mansingh, Sharon Lowen (USA), Anandini Dasi (Argentina).
            •	Manipuri
                Origins & Evolution
                    •	Mythologically linked to the celestial dance of Shiva and Parvati in the valleys of Manipur, accompanied by the local Gandharvas.
                    •	Gained prominence in the 15th century with the advent of Vaishnavism, making Krishna the central theme.
                    •	Traditionally performed by females.
                    •	18th century: Raja Bhag Chandra attempted to revive the dance.
                    •	Rabindranath Tagore reintroduced it to the national stage by incorporating it into Shantiniketan (West Bengal).
                Key Features
                    •	Emphasizes devotion over sensuality.
                    •	Thin veils cover the dancers’ faces; hand gestures and gentle foot movements hold greater importance than facial expressions.
                    •	Incorporates both Tandava and Lasya, with greater emphasis on Lasya.
                    •	Female dancers wear unique long skirts, focusing on slow, graceful hand and knee movements.
                    •	Nagabandha mudra (body curved in an ‘8’ shape) is a key posture.
                    •	Ras Leela (Radha-Krishna love story) is a recurring theme.
                    •	Musical accompaniment includes Pung (drum), flute, Khartals (wood clappers), dhols, with compositions from Jayadeva and Chandidas.
                    •	Influences can be seen in Thang-Ta and Sankirtana traditions.
                Famous Exponents
                    •	Jhaveri Sisters (Nayana, Suverna, Ranjana, Darshana)
                    •	Guru Bipin Singha
                    •	N. Madhabi Devi
            •	Kathak
                Origins & Evolution
                    •	Originated from the Ras Leela tradition of Brajbhoomi.
                    •	Derives its name from Kathikas (storytellers) who narrated epics through gestures and music.
                    •	Mughal Era:
                        •	Shifted into a court dance with Persian influences.
                        •	Became more lascivious in style.
                    •	20th century: Lady Leela Sokhey led efforts to revive the classical Kathak tradition.
                Key Features
                    •	Developed into different gharanas within the Hindustani musical tradition:
                        •	Lucknow: Flourished under Nawab Wajid Ali Shah, emphasizing expression and grace.
                        •	Jaipur: Founded by Bhanuji, known for fluency, speed, and rhythmic complexity.
                        •	Raigarh: Patronized by Raja Chakradhar Singh, focusing on percussion music.
                        •	Banaras: Developed under Janakiprasad, emphasizing floor movements and symmetry.
                    •	Characterized by intricate footwork and pirouettes.
                Elements of a Kathak Recital
                    •	Ananda 
                        – Introductory sequence, marking the dancer’s stage entry.
                    •	Thaat 
                        – Soft, varied movements.
                    •	Todas & Tukdas 
                        – Short, fast-paced rhythm sections.
                    •	Jugalbandi 
                        – A competitive interplay between dancer and tabla player.
                    •	Padhant 
                        – Dancer recites bols (syllabic rhythms) before demonstrating them.
                    •	Tarana 
                        – A rhythmic movement sequence similar to Thillana.
                    •	Kramalaya 
                        – Concluding intricate footwork segment.
                    •	Gat Bhaav 
                        – Storytelling through silent movements, without music or chanting.
                Music & Accompaniment
                    •	Traditionally accompanied by Dhrupad music.
                    •	Taranas, Thumris, and Ghazals were introduced during the Mughal period.
                Famous Exponents
                    •	Birju Maharaj
                    •	Lacchu Maharaj
                    •	Sitara Devi
                    •	Damayanti Joshi
            •	Sattriya
                Origins & Evolution
                    •	Introduced in 15th century Assam by Vaishnava saint Sankaradeva.
                    •	Originated in Vaishnava monasteries (Sattras), where it was primarily practiced.
                    •	Finds mention in Natya Shastra by Bharat Muni.
                    •	Inspired by the Bhakti Movement.
                Key Features
                    •	Fusion of Assamese dance traditions, mainly Ojapali and Devdasi.
                    •	Devotional in nature, narrating mythological stories of Vishnu.
                    •	Comprises three main components:
                        •	Nritta (pure dance)
                        •	Nritya (expressive dance)
                        •	Natya (dramatic representation)
                    •	Traditionally performed in groups by male monks (Bhokots) as part of daily rituals and festivals.
                    •	Strong emphasis on rhythmic syllables, dance postures, and intricate footwork.
                    •	Combines both Lasya and Tandava elements.
                    •	Strictly codified in terms of hand gestures and footwork.
                Music & Accompaniment
                    •	Instruments used: Khol (drum), Cymbals (Manjira), Flute.
                    •	Lyrics are mainly Borgeets (compositions of Sankaradeva).
                Costume & Jewelry
                    •	Male dancers: Dhoti and Paguri (turban).
                    •	Female dancers: Ghuri and Chador (made of Pat silk), with traditional Assamese jewelry.
                    •	Waist cloth is worn by both genders.
                Modern Evolution
                    •	Developed into two distinct styles:
                        •	Gayan-Bhayanar Nach – Focuses on singing and dancing.
                        •	Kharmanar Nach – A more stylized form.
                    •	Ankia Naat:
                        •	A play or musical-drama using Sattriya techniques.
                        •	Originally written in an Assamese-Maithili hybrid called Brajavali.
                        •	Bhaona is a related form based on Krishna’s stories.
        Recognition
            •	Sangeet Natak Akademi recognizes eight classical dance forms.
            •	Ministry of Culture acknowledges nine, including Chhau.


    Folk Dances of India
        - Basic info
            India’s folk dances reflect its diverse cultural heritage, incorporating local songs, myths, and traditions. 
            These dances are spontaneous and untrained, often specific to a region or community, passed down through generations.
        - Major Folk Dances of India:
        Major Folk Dances of India
            - Eastern India
                •	Chhau (Jharkhand, Odisha, West Bengal)
                    •	Originates from ‘Chhaya’ (shadow).
                    •	A martial mask dance narrating mythological and natural themes.
                    •	Three styles:
                        •	Saraikella Chhau (Jharkhand)
                        •	Mayurbhanj Chhau (Odisha) – No masks.
                        •	Purulia Chhau (West Bengal)
                    •	Recognized by UNESCO (2010) as an Intangible Cultural Heritage.
                    •	Jat-Jatin (Bihar, Mithila region)
                        •	Depicts the love and quarrels of a married couple.
                    •	Biraha / Bidesia (Bihar)
                        •	Expresses women’s sorrow for absent partners.
                        •	Performed only by males, who play female roles.
                    •	Jhumair (Jharkhand, Odisha)
                        •	A harvest dance, with two variations:
                            •	Janani Jhumair (women)
                            •	Mardana Jhumair (men)
                    •	Danda-Jatra (Odisha)
                        •	One of India’s oldest folk arts, blending dance, drama, and music.
                        •	Themes revolve around Shiva, social harmony, and brotherhood.
                    •	Paika (Odisha)
                        •	A martial dance performed with wooden spears and shields.
                        •	The word Paika signifies battle.
            - Western India
                •	Garba (Gujarat)
                    •	Performed during Navratri.
                    •	Revolves around a Garbha Deep (earthen lamp) placed inside a perforated pot.
                •	Dandiya Raas (Gujarat)
                    •	A stick dance, symbolizing Durga’s battle with Mahishasura.
                •	Ghoomar (Rajasthan)
                    •	Performed by Bhil women.
                    •	Characterized by pirouetting movements and vibrant costumes.
                •	Kalbelia (Rajasthan)
                    •	A sensuous dance mimicking serpent movements.
                    •	Uses Been (snake charmer’s flute).
                    •	Recognized by UNESCO (2010) as an Intangible Cultural Heritage.
                •	Tera Tali (Rajasthan)
                    •	Performed by the Kamar tribe.
                    •	Women sit on the ground, playing cymbals (Manjiras) tied to their bodies.
                •	Tippani (Gujarat, Saurashtra region)
                    •	Performed exclusively by women, who beat floors with wooden sticks.
            - Northern India
                •	Bhangra & Giddha (Punjab)
                    •	Bhangra: A high-energy men’s dance, with drum beats.
                    •	Giddha: The female counterpart of Bhangra.
                •	Charba (Himachal Pradesh)
                    •	A Dussehra festival dance.
                •	Jawara (Madhya Pradesh, Bundelkhand region)
                    •	A harvest dance, performed with a basket of jawar (sorghum) on the head.
                •	Matki (Madhya Pradesh, Malwa region)
                    •	Solo dance, performed by women balancing earthen pots.
                    •	Variants: Aada and Khada Nach.
                •	Dumhal (Jammu & Kashmir)
                    •	Performed by the Wattal tribe.
                    •	Features tall conical hats and colorful costumes.
            - Southern India
                •	Kolattam-Parichakali (Kerala, Lakshadweep)
                    •	A martial dance using wooden sticks and shields.
                •	Kaikottikali (Kerala)
                    •	A temple dance, performed during Onam.
                •	Padayani (Kerala)
                    •	A martial dance honoring Goddess Kali.
                    •	Dancers wear large masks (Kolams).
                •	Kummi (Tamil Nadu, Kerala)
                    •	A women’s dance, performed in a circle with rhythmic clapping.
                •	Mayilattam (Tamil Nadu, Kerala)
                    •	Peacock dance, where young girls dress as peacocks.
                •	Butta Bommalu (Andhra Pradesh, West Godavari district)
                    •	Dancers wear mask-like costumes, resembling toy figures.
                •	Pata Kunitha (Karnataka, Mysore region)
                    •	A religious dance, where men carry bamboo poles decorated with ribbons.
                •	Hulivesha (Coastal Karnataka)
                    •	Men dress as tigers, dancing to honor Goddess Durga.
            - Northeastern India
                •	Bagurumba (Assam, Bodo tribe)
                    •	Related to nature and the environment.
                •	Bihu (Assam)
                    •	A lively group dance, performed in colorful attire.
                •	Thang Ta (Manipur)
                    •	A martial arts dance, enacting mock fight sequences.
                •	Rangma (Nagaland)
                    •	A war dance, depicting tribal battles.
                •	Singhi Chham (Sikkim)
                    •	A lion mask dance, symbolizing Kanchenjunga Peak.
                •	Cheraw (Mizoram)
                    •	Bamboo dance, performed by women stepping through rhythmic bamboo movements.
                •	Hojagiri (Tripura)
                    •	A Lakshmi Puja dance, performed by 4-6 women balancing earthen pitchers.
            - Central India
                •	Gaur Muria (Chhattisgarh, Bastar region)
                    •	A bison-horn dance, performed in groups.
                •	Raut Naach (Chhattisgarh)
                    •	Performed by the Yadav community during Diwali.
                •	Karma Naach (Chhattisgarh, Chota Nagpur Plateau)
                    •	A tribal dance, performed in a circle with linked arms.
            - Other Unique Folk Dances
                •	Alkap (Jharkhand, West Bengal – Rajmahal Hills, Murshidabad, Malda)
                    •	A dance-drama performed by troops of 10-12 people.
                    •	Features comedic sketches (Kap).
                    •	Associated with Shiva’s Gajan festival.
                •	Garadi (Puducherry)
                    •	Monkey dance, celebrating Rama’s victory over Ravana.
                    •	Dancers wear iron rings (Anjalis) on their legs.
                •	Dalkhai (Odisha)
                    •	A tribal Dussehra dance, based on stories from Ramayana & Mahabharata.


- INDIAN THEATRE
    •	Ancient Indian Theatre
        •	Oldest Amphitheatres
        •	The ruins at Sitabena and Jogimara caves are believed to be the world’s oldest amphitheatres, showcasing India’s long theatrical tradition.
        •	Natya Shastra
            •	Written by Bharat Muni between 200 BC and 200 AD, it is the first formal treatise on dramaturgy.
            •	Describes Natya Veda, created by Lord Brahma, combining elements from the four Vedas.
            •	Defines ten types of plays, ranging from one-act to ten-act performances.
            •	Covers all aspects of classical Sanskrit literature and performance arts.

    •	Classical Sanskrit Theatre
        •	Origins and Characteristics
            •	Theatre in India began as a narrative art form, blending music, dance, and acting.
            •	Recitation, dance, and music were integral to performances.
            •	The Sanskrit word Nataka comes from Nata, meaning a dancer.
            •	Other terms for drama: Rupaka, Drishyakavya, and Preksakavya.
        •	Types of Plays
            •	Lokadharmi: Realistic depictions of daily life.
            •	Natyadharmi: Stylized performances with overt symbolism.
        •	Prominent Playwrights and Works
            •	Sariputraprakarana by Ashvaghosha (first known Sanskrit drama, a nine-act play).
            •	Bhasa (3rd-4th century AD) – composed 13 plays.
            •	Sudraka – introduced conflict in Mricchakatika, featuring an antagonist.
            •	Kalidasa – famous for Malavikagnimitram, Vikramorvashi, and Shakuntalam, portraying the conflict between desire and duty.
            •	Other notable works:
                •	Uttaramacharita and Mahaviracharita by Bhavabhuti.
                •	Mudrarakshasa by Visakhadatta.
                •	Ratnavali by Harshavardhana.
        •	Categories of Sanskrit Plays
            •	Plays were classified into ten types: Anka, Bhana, Dima, Ihamgra, Nataka, Prahasana, Prakarana, Svakarna, Vithi, and Vyayog.
            •	Natya Shastra describes only two: Nataka and Prakarana.
        •	Conventions of Classical Sanskrit Plays
            •	Typically had four to seven acts.
            •	Always ended happily, with the hero achieving his goal (tragedies were rare).
            •	The protagonist was always male.
            •	Well-structured with a defined opening, progression, development, pause, and conclusion.
        •	Ritualistic Progression of a Play
            •	Pre-play rituals (Purva-raga) performed behind the curtain.
            •	The Sutradhar (stage manager/director) entered, worshipped the deity, and introduced the playwright.
            •	The leading lady was summoned, and the setting was announced.
            •	Theatres could accommodate around 400 people.
            •	Two-storeyed stages:
                •	Upper level represented the celestial sphere.
                •	Lower level represented the terrestrial sphere.
            •	Curtains were used for dramatic effect; masks were not used.
        •	Character Types
            •	Nayaka (Hero): Male lead, classified as Lalita (kind-hearted), Shanta (calm), Uddhata (arrogant), etc. Could also be an antagonist (Pratinayaka) like Ravana or Duryodhana.
            •	Nayika (Heroine): Played by queens, friends, courtesans (Ganika), or divine ladies (Divya).
            •	Vidusaka (Clown): A noble, good-hearted comic character, often the hero’s friend. Used satire to challenge social norms. Spoke in Prakrit while others spoke in Sanskrit.
        •	Decline of Sanskrit Theatre
            •	Shift towards poetry led to a decline in dramatic works.
            •	Strict conventions limited creative freedom, pushing writers towards other forms.
            •	Sanskrit became overly embellished and lost popularity among common people.
            •	Muslim rule reduced patronage for Sanskrit theatre, favoring dance and music instead.
        - Additional side information
            •	Koothiyattam (Koodiyattam)
                •	India’s oldest surviving theatre tradition, dating back to the 10th century AD in Kerala.
                •	Adheres to Natya Shastra rules and performed by the Chakyar and Nambiar castes.
                •	Uses Sanskrit, Prakrit, and Malayalam, with Mizhavu and Edakka as background instruments.
                •	Begins with Nirvahana (recollection of past events), followed by slow unfolding with social, philosophical, and political commentary.
                •	Margi Madhu Chakyar is a leading exponent of this art form.

    Folk theatre
        - Basic info
            • Rich tradition across India, reflecting local lifestyles, beliefs, and customs.
            • Sanskrit theatre was urban and refined, while folk theatre had rural roots.
            • Evolved from devotional themes (15th-16th century AD) to include love ballads and heroic tales.
            • Post-independence, it became a medium for social awareness.
        - Categories of Folk Theatre
            - Ritual Theatre
                • Emerged during the Bhakti movement to express devotion.
                • Important examples:
                    Ankia Naat (Assam)
                        • One-act play started by Sankaradeva & Madhavdev.
                        • Opera-style performance on Lord Krishna’s life.
                        • Features masks, mixed Assamese-Maithili language (Brajavali).
                    Ramlila (Uttar Pradesh)
                        • Theatrical enactment of Ramayana before Dussehra.
                        • Performed by male actors, including the role of Sita.
                    Raslila (Gujarat, North India)
                        • Dance-drama of Krishna and Radha’s love stories.
                    Bhuta Aradhana (Karnataka)
                        • Spirit worship theatre (Bhuta Kola).
                    Ramman (Uttarakhand)
                        • Ritual theatre of Garhwal, dedicated to Bhumiyal Devta.
                        • Recognized by UNESCO.
            - Theatres of Entertainment (North, West & East India)
                - Basic info
                    • More secular, focused on love, valor, and socio-cultural themes.
                    • Primarily for entertainment.
                - Important examples:
                    Bhavai (Rajasthan)
                        • Dance-based theatre with romantic themes.
                        • Performers balance pots on their heads.
                        • Sutradhar (narrator) is called Nayaka.
                    Daskathia (Odisha)
                        • Dual-narrator: Gayaka (lead) & Palia (co-narrator).
                        • Wooden musical instrument Kathia used.
                        • Variant: Chhaiti Ghoda.
                    Garoda (Gujarat)
                        • Storytelling using painted pictures by Garoda Brahmins.
                    Jatra (Eastern India)
                        • Open-air theatre started by Chaitanya Mahaprabhu.
                        • Originally religious, later included secular, historical, patriotic themes.
                        • Variants: Rama Jatra, Shiv Jatra, Chandi Jatra.
                        • Odisha’s street theatre: Sahi Jatra.
                    Kariyila (Himachal Pradesh)
                        • Open-air theatre performed during village fairs.
                    Maach (Madhya Pradesh)
                        • Folk theatre of Malwa.
                        • Mythological & romantic folk tales.
                        • Uses poetic couplets (Rangat Dohas).
                    Nautanki (North India)
                        • Musical theatre mentioned in Ain-i-Akbari.
                        • Nagada drum used.
                        • Kanpur & Lucknow schools.
                    Ojapali (Assam)
                        • Narrative theatre linked to Manasha festival.
                        • Three parts: Baniya Khanda, Bhatiyali Khanda, Deva Khanda.
                        • Lead: Oja, chorus: Palis.
                    Powada (Maharashtra)
                        • Ballads of valor, popular after Shivaji’s victory over Afzal Khan.
                    Swang (Rajasthan & Haryana)
                        • Musical dramas using Ektara, Harmonium, Sarangi, Dholak.
                    Tamasha (Maharashtra)
                        • Humorous theatre featuring Lavani songs.
                        • Unique for female actors playing male roles.
                    Bhand Pather (Jammu & Kashmir)
                        • Social satire & mythical stories.
                        • Performed by Muslim artists but secular in nature.
                    Bhaona (Assam)
                        • Religious theatre promoting moral values.
                        • Features Sutradhar (narrator) & Vaishnavite themes.
                    Dashavatar (Maharashtra & Goa)
                        • Drama honoring the ten incarnations of Vishnu.
                        • Two parts: Poorva-ranga (intro) & Uttar-ranga (main act).
                    Naqal / Bhand (Punjab & Uttar Pradesh)
                        • Mimicry-based theatre with social & political satire.
                        • Performed by the Muslim Bhand Community.
            - Theatres of South India
                - Basic info
                    • Gained popularity after Sanskrit theatre declined in North India (~8th century AD).
                    • Dance-focused traditions.
                - Important examples:
                    Villu Paatu (Kerala & Tamil Nadu)
                        • Bow-song musical theatre.
                        • Narrates Ramayana using bow-shaped instruments.
                    Yakshagana (Karnataka & Kerala)
                        • Originated in Vijayanagara empire.
                        • Descriptive dance-drama influenced by Vaishnava Bhakti movement.
                    Burra Katha (Andhra Pradesh)
                        • Storytelling using Burra (percussion instrument).
                        • Lead narrator with two co-artists for rhythm & chorus.
                    Pagati Veshaalu (Telangana & Andhra Pradesh)
                        • Role-playing theatre with Vesham (disguise) as the main character.
                    Bayalata (Karnataka)
                        • Open-air theatre performed during deity worship.
                        • Stories of Radha-Krishna.
                    Tal-Maddale (Karnataka & Kerala)
                        • Predecessor of Yakshagana.
                        • Performed sitting, without costumes, dance, or acting.
                        • Narrator: Bhagavata, aided by Arthadharis.
                    Theyyam (Kerala)
                        • Similar to Bhuta Kola (Karnataka).
                        • Open-air performance in front of temples.
                        • Actors wear elaborate headgear & costumes.
                    Krishnanattam (Kerala)
                        • Dance-drama from the 17th century.
                        • Based on Krishna Geethi, narrating Krishna’s life over 8 nights.
                    Kuruvanji (Tamil Nadu)
                        • Originated 300 years ago.
                        • Dance-ballet form based on Bharatanatyam.
                        • Features a fortune-teller predicting a heroine’s fate.

    Modern Indian Theatre
        Colonial Influence & Emergence of Proscenium Theatre
            • Modern Indian theatre developed during the colonial era, influenced by both Sanskrit classics and Western literature.
            • Adaptations of works by Gotthold Ephraim Lessing and Shakespeare became common.
            • The rise of cities like Calcutta (Kolkata) and Madras (Chennai) created a demand for new entertainment forms.
            • This led to the emergence of the Western Proscenium Style of Theatre in the 18th and 19th centuries.
            • Many theatres were built in Calcutta in the 19th century, such as:
                •	Belgachia Natyashala
                •	Shobhabazar Natyashala
        Indigenous Adaptations & Commercialization
            • Indians developed a unique hybrid theatrical style, blending Western and Indian traditions.
            • Unlike ancient theatre, which was open to all, modern theatre became commercialized with ticketed audiences.
            • Common themes included:
                •	Social evils (dowry, caste system, religious hypocrisy)
                •	Political affairs
            • Due to its growing influence, the British Government imposed the Dramatic Performances Act (1876) to curb political awareness.

    Major Theatrical Movements & Personalities
        - Parsi Theatre (1850s-1920s)
            • Flourished in Western India, especially in Gujarati and Marathi.
            • Known for:
                •	Colorful backdrops
                •	Musical elements
                •	Themes: Romance, humor, melodrama
            • Declined with the advent of cinema in the 1930s, as many Parsi producers moved into filmmaking.
        - Bengali Theatre
            • Rabindranath Tagore 
                – A celebrated playwright who wrote his first play, Valmiki Pratibha, at age 20.
            • Famous works:
                •	Raktakarabi (Red Oleanders)
                •	Chitrangada
                •	Post-Office
            • Themes: Nationalism, spirituality, socio-political issues.
            • Other prominent Bengali playwrights:
                •	Prasanna Kumar Thakur
                •	Girishchandra Ghosh
                •	Dinabandhu Mitra (Nildarpan, a play exposing British exploitation of indigo farmers).
        - Indian People’s Theatre Association (IPTA) – 1943
            • Established as the cultural wing of the Communist Party.
            • Although disbanded in 1947, it played a crucial role in theatre development.
            • Focused on social issues like the Bengal Famine.
            • Notable members:
                •	Balraj Sahni
                •	Prithviraj Kapoor
                •	Bijon Bhattacharya
                •	Ritwik Ghatak
                •	Utpal Dutt
            • IPTA still operates in Chhattisgarh, Punjab, and West Bengal.
        - Prithvi Theatre – 1944
            • Founded by Prithviraj Kapoor as a traveling theatre group with 150 artists.
            • Performed over 2000 plays across India.
            • A permanent theatre was later established in Mumbai in 1978, which still exists today.
        - Institutional Support & Growth
            • Sangeet Natak Akademi (1952) 
                – Established to promote performing arts, including theatre.
            • National School of Drama (NSD) 
                – Played a key role in developing modern theatre talent.
        - Regional Theatre & Institutions
            • Kalakshetra Manipur 
                – Founded by Heisnam Kanhailal in 1969 to preserve traditional Manipuri theatre.
            • Chorus Repertory Theatre (1976) 
                – Established by Ratan Thiyam.

- INDIAN PUPPETRY
    Introduction
        • Puppetry is one of the oldest forms of entertainment.
        • It captivates audiences due to the element of control by a puppeteer.
        • It is cost-effective, making it popular among freelance artists.
        • Artists enjoy unrestricted creative freedom in design, color, and movement, making puppetry a highly ingenious art form.

    Indian Origins of Puppetry
        • Puppetry in India has served both entertainment and educational purposes.
        • Excavations at Harappa and Mohenjo-Daro suggest the existence of puppetry as early as the Indus Valley Civilization.
        • References in Ancient Texts:
            •	500 BC – Early mentions of marionette theatre.
            •	Tamil classic Silappadikaram (1st–2nd century BC) mentions puppetry.
            •	Mahabharata also refers to puppetry.
        • Philosophical Importance:
            •	In Bhagavad Gita, God is described as a puppeteer, controlling the universe with three strings: Satta, Raja, and Tama.
            •	In Indian theatre, the narrator is called Sutradhar (meaning “holder of strings”).
        • Development across India
            • Various forms of regional puppetry developed across India.
            • Themes: Mythology, folklore, and local legends.
            • Puppetry combines painting, sculpture, music, dance, and drama, making it a unique form of artistic expression.
        Decline in Modern Times
            • Challenges:
                •	Lack of devoted audience.
                •	Financial struggles have led to a decline in this art form.

    Types of Puppetry in India
        - String Puppets (Marionettes)
            • Prominent in Indian cultural traditions.
            • Key Features:
                •	Made of wood, typically 8–9 inches in size.
                •	Painted using oil paint, with detailed facial features.
                •	Wooden limbs are covered in colorful miniature costumes.
                •	Jewelry and accessories give them a lifelike feel.
                •	Strings attached to hands, head, and back for manipulation.
            Notable String Puppetry Forms
                - Kathputli (Rajasthan)
                    •	Name derived from ‘kath’ (wood) + ‘putli’ (doll).
                    •	Brightly dressed Rajasthani-style puppets.
                    •	No legs – controlled by strings attached to the puppeteer’s fingers.
                    •	Accompanied by dramatic folk music.
                - Kundhei (Odisha)
                    •	Made of light wood and dressed in long skirts.
                    •	More joints provide better flexibility.
                    •	Strings attached to a triangular prop.
                    •	Influence of Odissi dance in performances.
                - Gombeyatta (Karnataka)
                    •	Inspired by Yakshagana Theatre.
                    •	Requires multiple puppeteers to manipulate a single puppet.
                - Bommalattam (Tamil Nadu)
                    •	Blends rod and string puppetry.
                    •	Strings attached to an iron ring worn on the puppeteer’s head.
                    •	Largest & heaviest marionettes in India (up to 4.5 ft, 10 kg).
                    •	Has four distinct performance stages: Vinayak Puja, Komali, Amanattam, Pusenkanattam.
        - Shadow Puppets
            • A rich tradition in India, still surviving today.
            • Key Features:
                •	Flat figures made of leather.
                •	Painted identically on both sides.
                •	Placed against a white screen, with light from behind creating shadows.
                •	Puppeteers manipulate the figures to create storytelling imagery.
            Notable Shadow Puppetry Forms
                - Togalu Gombeyatta (Karnataka)
                    •	Size varies based on social status of the character:
                    •	Larger puppets for kings/religious figures.
                    •	Smaller puppets for common people/servants.
                - Ravanchhaya (Odisha)
                    •	Known for its theatrical style and bold, dramatic postures.
                    •	Puppets made of deer skin, no joints, making movement complex.
                    •	Includes non-human figures (trees, animals).
                    •	Artists are highly trained, creating lyrical narrations.
                - Tholu Bommalata (Andhra Pradesh)
                    •	Accompanied by classical musical background.
                    •	Themes: Mythology, Epics, and Puranas.
                    •	Larger puppets painted on both sides.
        - Glove Puppets (Hand Puppets)
            • Also called sleeve, hand, or palm puppets.
            • Key Features:
                •	Small figures with head & arms wearing flowing costumes.
                •	Made of cloth or wood.
                •	Puppeteer wears the puppet like a glove, using fingers to control movements.
                •	Usually performed with dholak (drum) beats.
            Notable Glove Puppetry Forms
                - Pavakoothu (Kerala)
                    •	Originated in 18th century AD.
                    •	Influenced by Kathakali dance, seen in its headgear, feathers, and face paint.
                    •	Themes: Narratives from Ramayana & Mahabharata.
        - Rod Puppets
            • Larger than glove puppets, controlled by rods from behind a screen.
            • Popular in Eastern India.
            Notable Rod Puppetry Forms
                - Yampuri (Bihar)
                    •	Made of wood, with no joints.
                    •	Carved from a single piece of wood.
                    •	Painted & dressed in bright colors.
                - Putul Nachh (Bengal-Odisha-Assam Region)
                    •	Figures are 3–4 ft tall.
                    •	Costumes resemble characters from Jatra (folk theatre).
                    •	Have three joints: neck and shoulders.
                    •	Controlled by a rod attached to the puppeteer’s waist.
                    •	Puppeteers move behind a curtain, mimicking puppet movements.
                    •	Accompanied by a musical troupe (harmonium, cymbals, tabla).


- INDIAN CIRCUS
    - Introduction
        •	India has a long tradition of street performers and traveling artists.
        •	However, the circus industry is relatively new, with the first Indian circus emerging around 1880 (as noted by Philip Astley, an English circus master).

    The Great Indian Circus
        •	Founder: Prof. Vishnupant Chatre, an equestrian and singer.
        •	Patron: Raja of Kurduvadi (Chatre worked as a stable master).
        •	First Performance: 1880, followed by extensive tours in India, Ceylon, and Southeast Asia.
        •	Challenges: Failed to compete with North American circuses due to size and grandeur.
        •	Legacy: Chatre is recognized as the Father of Indian Circus.

    Major Circus Companies in India
        Three Ring Circus
            •	Founder: K. Damodaran (1930s).
            •	Evolution: Grew from a two-pole setup to Asia’s only six-pole three-ring circus.
        Great Royal Circus
            •	Founded: 1909, originally Madhuskar’s Circus.
            •	Later owned by: N.R. Walawalkar, renamed Great Royal Circus.
            •	Tours: Performed in Africa, the Middle East, and Southeast Asia.
        Great Bombay Circus
            •	Founded: 1920 as Grand Bombay Circus (by Baburao Kadam).
            •	Merger (1947): K. M. Kunhikannan (Keeleri Kunhikannan’s nephew) merged his circus, renaming it Great Bombay Circus.
            •	Size: One of India’s largest, with 300 performers and 60 animals.
        Gemini Circus
            •	Founded: 1951, in Bilimora, Gujarat by M. V. Shankaran (ex-army man, master aerialist).
            •	First Indian Circus at International Festival (1964):
            •	Performed in Moscow, Sochi, Yalta (USSR).
            •	Cultural Impact: Featured in Indian films, including Raj Kapoor’s ‘Mera Naam Joker’.
        Jumbo Circus
            •	Founded: 1977 in Bihar, later acquired by the Shankaran family.
            •	Largest modern Indian circus, featuring Russian acrobats & performers.

    Notable Personality: Damoo Dhotre
        •	Born: Pune, 1902 (poor family background).
        •	Career:
            •	Joined Isako’s Russian Circus as a proprietor.
            •	1939: Moved to France with Bertram Mills Circus.
            •	Performed with Ringling Brothers and Barnum & Bailey Circus (USA), known as “The Greatest Show on Earth.”
            •	Served in the U.S. Army (1943-1946).
        •	Title: “Wild Animal Man.”
        •	Legacy:
            •	Gained U.S. citizenship (1960) but returned to Pune, passing away in 1973 after 40 years in the circus industry.
        
    Circus: A Marginal Industry
        •	Decline Since 1990s:
        •	1990: 300 active Indian circuses.
        •	2017: Only 30 remain.
        - Reasons for Decline
            •	Secrecy & Limited Management:
                •	Circus companies are family-run, preventing outsiders from entering and modernizing management.
            •	Child Labor Ban (2011):
                •	Circus acrobatics require early training.
                •	Supreme Court banned hiring children under 14, shrinking the talent pool.
            •	Animal Ban (1997):
                •	Government of India prohibited the use of wild animals for entertainment, removing a key attraction.
            •	Perceived as Dangerous:
                •	Circus is seen as risky, discouraging new generations from joining.
            •	Competition from Modern Entertainment:
                •	Young audiences now prefer gymnastics, digital media, and global entertainment options.
        Possible Remedies for Revival
            •	Safety Regulations:
                •	Strengthening safety laws would improve public perception of circus as a profession.
            •	Cultural Integration:
                •	Circus can preserve dying arts and increase audience engagement.
            •	Government Support:
                •	Security and retirement benefits for circus artists (most retire by 40, later working as manual laborers).
            •	Policy Shift:
                •	Currently under Department of Sports & Youth Affairs.
                •	Moving it to Ministry of Culture could help recognize circus as an art form and create better revival strategies.

- MARTIAL ARTS IN INDIA
    Introduction
        India, a land of diverse cultures and ethnicities, has a rich tradition of martial arts that date back to ancient times. 
        Initially used for warfare, these art forms are now practiced for demonstration, rituals, physical fitness, and self-defense. The term “martial arts” literally means “arts associated with waging war.”
        Many Indian martial arts have deep connections with dance, yoga, and performing arts. 
        Some, such as Kalaripayattu and Silambam, were banned during British rule but resurfaced and gained popularity post-independence.
        
    Some of the popular martial arts in India are discussed below:
        - Kalaripayattu (Kerala, 3rd Century BC)
            One of India’s oldest martial arts, Kalaripayattu originated in Kerala and is practiced across Southern India.
                •	Kalari (Malayalam) refers to the training hall/gymnasium.
                •	Includes mock duels, physical exercises, and unarmed combat.
                •	No drumming or songs accompany the training; footwork, kicks, strikes, and weapons are key.
                •	Women also practice this art.
            Key Techniques in Kalaripayattu
                •	Uzhichil – Gingli oil massage.
                •	Otta – Fighting with an S-shaped stick.
                •	Maipayattu – Body exercises.
                •	Puliyankam – Sword fight.
                •	Verumkai – Bare-handed combat.
                •	Angathari – Use of metal weapons and wooden sticks (Kolthari).
        - Silambam (Tamil Nadu, 2nd Century AD)
            A staff-fencing martial art, Silambam was patronized by the Pandyas, Cholas, and Cheras.
                •	Mentioned in Silappadikaram (2nd Century AD).
                •	Traded with Rome, Greece, and Egypt for its unique bamboo staves.
                •	Spread to Malaysia, where it is a popular sport and self-defense technique.
            Key Techniques in Silambam
                •	Swift footwork.
                •	Using both hands to wield the staff.
                •	Thrust, cut, chop, and sweep moves.
                •	Specific strokes (Snake, Monkey, Hawk) for controlling mobs.
                •	Defensive techniques for deflecting thrown objects.
        - Thang-ta and Sarit Sarak (Manipur, 17th Century)
            Developed by the Meitei people of Manipur.
                •	Thang-ta – Armed combat using swords (“Thang”) and spears (“Ta”).
                •	Sarit Sarak – Unarmed hand-to-hand combat.
                •	Used by Manipuri kings to fight the British.
                •	Banned during British rule, revived post-independence.
            Together, these form “Huyen Langlon.”
        - Cheibi Gad-ga (Manipur, Ancient)
            A traditional sword and shield combat.
                •	Now uses a stick covered in soft leather instead of a sword.
                •	Shield: About 1 meter in diameter.
                •	Battlefield: A 7-meter diameter circle with two inner lines (2 meters apart).
                •	Victory: Based on points scored for skill and force.
        - Pari-khanda (Bihar, Rajput Tradition)
            A Rajput martial art using swords and shields.
                •	Still practiced in Bihar.
                •	Influence on Chhau dance, using similar steps and techniques.
                •	Name: “Pari” (shield) + “Khanda” (sword).
        - Thoda (Himachal Pradesh, Mahabharata Era)
            A mix of martial arts, sport, and cultural ritual, performed during Baisakhi (April).
                •	Believed to originate from Mahabharata-era archery.
                •	Two teams: Pashis and Saathis (descendants of Pandavas & Kauravas).
                •	Objective: Archers aim for the opponent’s leg (below the knee) to score points.
                •	Negative points for striking other body parts.
        - Gatka (Punjab, Sikh Martial Art)
            A weapon-based martial art performed by Sikhs.
                •	Weapons used: Sticks, Kirpan (dagger), Talwar (sword), and Kataar (punch dagger).
                •	Footwork and attack/defense vary by weapon type.
                •	Displayed at fairs and Sikh celebrations.
        - Mardani Khel (Maharashtra, Shivaji Era)
            A traditional Maharashtrian armed combat form, developed in Kolhapur.
                •	Focused on weaponry skills, swift movements, and low stances.
                •	Uses:
                    •	Indian Patta (sword).
                    •	Vita (corded lance).
                •	Famous practitioners: Shivaji.
        - Lathi (Punjab & Bengal, Ancient)
            A stick-fighting martial art using 6-8 ft cane sticks, sometimes metal-tipped.
                •	One of the world’s oldest martial arts weapons.
                •	Still used by Indian police for crowd control.
                •	Popular in village sports.
        - Inbuan Wrestling (Mizoram, 1750 AD)
            A strictly rule-based Mizo wrestling style.
                •	No stepping out of the circle.
                •	No kicking or knee-bending.
                •	Victory: Opponent must be lifted off the ground.
                •	Grip: Wrestlers hold a belt tied around their waist.
        - Kuttu Varisai (Tamil Nadu, 1st-2nd Century BC)
            An unarmed martial art known as “Empty-Hand Silambam.”
                •	Mentioned in Sangam literature.
                •	Practiced in Tamil Nadu, Sri Lanka, and Malaysia.
                •	Techniques:
                    •	Grappling, striking, and locking.
                    •	Yoga, gymnastics, and breathing exercises.
                    •	Animal-based movements (Snake, Eagle, Tiger, Elephant, Monkey).
                •	Considered the unarmed component of Silambam.
        - Musti Yuddha (Varanasi, Ancient, Resembling Boxing)
            A bare-handed combat martial art, resembling boxing.
                •	Focuses on: Punches, kicks, knee and elbow strikes.
                •	Popular in the 1960s, now rarely seen.
            Four Fighting Styles (Named After Hindu Gods)
                1.	Jambuvanti – Locking and submission techniques.
                2.	Hanumanti – Technical superiority in combat.
                3.	Bhimaseni – Power and sheer strength.
                4.	Jarasandhi – Breaking limbs and joints.
