- UNESCO’S LIST OF INTANGIBLE CULTURAL HERITAGE
    Introduction
        Cultural heritage is not limited to monuments and collections of objects. 
        It also includes living traditions and expressions passed down from ancestors to future generations. These include:
            •	Oral traditions
            •	Performing arts
            •	Social practices, rituals, and festive events
            •	Knowledge and practices related to nature and the universe
            •	Traditional craftsmanship and associated skills
        To safeguard and promote awareness of such heritage, UNESCO established its Lists of Intangible Cultural Heritage. 
            This initiative aims to protect important intangible cultural heritage worldwide and highlight its significance.
            What is Intangible Cultural Heritage?
                Intangible Cultural Heritage refers to:
                    •	Practices, representations, and expressions
                    •	Knowledge and skills
                    •	Instruments, objects, and artifacts
                These are recognized by communities, groups, or individuals as part of their cultural heritage.

    UNESCO’s Intangible Cultural Heritage Lists
        UNESCO has compiled two key lists to categorize and safeguard intangible heritage:
            - Representative List of the Intangible Cultural Heritage of Humanity
                •	Showcases cultural practices and expressions that illustrate heritage diversity.
                •	Aims to raise awareness about their importance.
            - List of Intangible Cultural Heritage in Need of Urgent Safeguarding
                •	Includes cultural elements that are vulnerable and at risk of disappearing.
                •	Highlights traditions that require immediate preservation efforts.
        Note: The Representative List is particularly important for exam preparation.

    Representative List of the Intangible Cultural Heritage of Humanity (India)
        India has 13 items included in UNESCO’s Representative List of the Intangible Cultural Heritage of Humanity:
            - Koodiyattam (Sanskrit Theatre) – 2008
                •	A classical dance-drama from Kerala performed by the Chakyars (male roles) and Ambalavasi Nambiar women (female roles).
                •	Takes place inside temples and lasts 6 to 20 days.
                •	Based on Hindu mythology, with Sanskrit dialogues and a Malayalam narration by the “Vidushaka” character.
                •	Major musical instrument: Mizhavu.
            - Ramlila – 2008
                •	A folk theatre tradition of Uttar Pradesh, enacting the Ramayana through songs, dances, and dialogues.
                •	Performed during Sharad Navaratras, leading up to Dussehra.
                •	Traditionally performed by males, including the role of Sita.
                •	Unique variation: In Bakshi Ka Talab (Lucknow), Muslim youths have played key roles since 1972.
                •	Adapted into a radio play, Us Gaon Ki Ramlila, to promote communal harmony.
            - The Tradition of Vedic Chanting – 2008
                •	The oral tradition of the Vedas, featuring ancient recitation techniques.
                •	Considered one of the oldest unbroken oral traditions from the early Iron Age.
                •	Recognized by UNESCO as a Masterpiece of the Oral and Intangible Heritage of Humanity.
            - Ramman – 2009
                •	A religious festival and ritual theatre of the Garhwal region in Uttarakhand.
                •	Celebrated by Hindu villagers in Saloor-Dungra, offering prayers to Bhumiyal Devta.
                •	Unique to this village, featuring Jagar, a musical narration of local legends.
            - Mudiyettu – 2010
                •	A ritual theatre dance from Kerala, depicting the mythological battle between Goddess Kali and the demon Darika.
                •	Performed in Bhagavati Kavus (village temples) after the harvest season (February–May).
                •	Features elaborate makeup, costumes, and supernatural-themed facial paintings.
                •	Floor designs (Kalam) of Goddess Kali are drawn and worshipped before the performance.
            - Kalbelia – 2010
                •	A folk dance of the Kalbelia tribe in Rajasthan, with serpent-like movements.
                •	Involves spontaneous composition of lyrics and improvisation.
                •	Musical instruments used: Poongi, Chang, Cymbals.
            - Chhau – 2010
                •	A tribal martial art dance from Odisha, Jharkhand, and West Bengal.
                •	Three subgenres:
                    •	Purulia Chhau (West Bengal)
                    •	Seraikella Chhau (Jharkhand)
                    •	Mayurbhanj Chhau (Odisha)
                •	Performed at night during spring festivals using mock combat techniques.
                •	Themes based on Hindu mythology, with masks worn except in Mayurbhanj Chhau.
            - Buddhist Chanting of Ladakh – 2012
                •	Sacred Buddhist recitations from Ladakh, Jammu & Kashmir.
                •	Associated with Mahayana and Vajrayana traditions.
                •	Performed indoors and in monastery courtyards.
            - Sankirtana – 2013
                •	A ritual art form from Manipur, involving singing, drumming, and dancing.
                •	Originated in 15th century AD, performed mainly in Vaishnavite temples.
                •	Narrates the life and deeds of Lord Krishna through music and dance.
                •	Features two drummers and 10 singer-dancers in a courtyard setting.
                •	Main instruments used: Cymbals, Drums.
            - Traditional Brass and Copper Craft of Utensil Making (Thathera Community, Punjab) – 2014
                •	A metalworking craft tradition passed down through generations.
                •	Involves shaping brass, copper, and Kansa (an alloy of zinc, tin, and copper) into utensils.
                •	Used for both functional and ritualistic purposes, recommended in Ayurvedic texts.
                •	Patronized by Maharaja Ranjit Singh in the 19th century.
                •	Crafted utensils are commonly used in households and Sikh Gurudwara langars.
            - Nowrouz – 2016
                •	Marks the New Year for Parsis and Spring Festival for the Kashmiri Community.
                •	Associated with Zoroastrian environmental respect.
                •	Traditional Nowrouz table includes:
                    •	A copy of the Gathas (sacred text)
                    •	Lit lamp or candle
                    •	Sprouted wheat/beans in a ceramic plate
                    •	Silver coin in a bowl
                    •	Flowers, painted eggs, sweets, and a goldfish bowl
                •	Symbolizes prosperity, wealth, color, and happiness.
            - Yoga – 2016
                •	A practice involving poses, meditation, controlled breathing, word chanting, and self-discipline techniques.
                •	Traditionally passed down through the Guru-Shishya parampara.
            - Kumbh Mela – 2017
                •	A mass Hindu pilgrimage where devotees bathe in sacred rivers.
                •	Held at four locations:
                    •	Prayagraj
                    •	Haridwar
                    •	Nashik (Simhastha Kumbh Mela)
                    •	Ujjain (Simhastha Kumbh Mela)
                •	Occurrence:
                    •	Every 12 years at each location.
                    •	Ardha Kumbh Mela (half-Kumbh) held every 6 years at Prayagraj and Haridwar.
                    •	Prayagraj Kumbh Mela occurs 3 years after Haridwar and 3 years before Nashik/Ujjain.
                    •	Nashik and Ujjain Kumbh Melas are often held in the same year or one year apart.


CULTURE OF INDIA

- Languages in India
    Introduction
        •	Throughout history, writings have reflected culture, lifestyle, society, and polity.
        •	Each culture evolved its own language, creating a rich literary base.
        •	This literary base provides insight into the evolution of languages and cultures over centuries.

    Key Concepts
        •	Language: A system of communication through speech, using sounds with shared meanings.
        •	Language Family: A group of related languages that originated from a common ancestor before recorded history.
        •	Dialect: A regional variant of a language; multiple dialects can emerge from one language.

    Languages in India
        •	India has diverse language families, with most belonging to the Indo-Aryan Group.
        •	The Indo-Aryan Group is a branch of the Indo-European Family.
        •	Some languages in India are indigenous and do not belong to the Indo-European Family.

    Classification of Indian Languages
        - Indo-Aryan Group of Languages
            •	A branch of the Indo-European Family, introduced to India with the Aryans.
            •	It is India’s largest language group, spoken by 74% of the population.
            •	Divided into three subgroups based on their historical development:
                - Old Indo-Aryan Group (1500 BC – 300 BC)
                    •	Sanskrit emerged from this group, forming the basis of Vedic texts (Vedas, Upanishads, Puranas, Dharmasutras).
                    •	Sanskrit is the mother of many Indian languages and one of the 22 scheduled languages in the Constitution.
                    Classical Sanskrit as a Vehicle of Indian Culture
                        •	Panini (400 BC) developed Sanskrit grammar in Ashtadhyayi.
                        •	Buddhist literature, such as Mahavastu (Hinayana) and Lalitavistara (Mahayana), were written in Sanskrit.
                        •	Sanskrit transcended regional barriers, influencing all parts of India.
                        •	The refined form of Sanskrit appeared between 300–200 BC, with the first Sanskrit inscriptions by Rudradaman (Junagarh, Gujarat).
                        •	The Gupta period saw extensive Sanskrit literature, including Mahakavyas (epics), Khandakavyas (semi-epics), and dramas.
                        •	Plays used Sanskrit for upper varnas and Prakrit for women and Shudras.
                - Middle Indo-Aryan Group (600 BC – 1000 AD)
                    •	Characterized by the development of Prakrit, a more natural and unstructured language.
                    •	Prakrit languages were spoken by common people, while Sanskrit was used by elites.
                    •	Jain ‘Agamas’ were written in Prakrit.
                    •	Transition between languages was gradual, without strict chronological shifts.
                    - Major Prakrit Languages
                        •	Pali (5th–1st century BC)
                            •	Spoken in Magadha, related to Sanskrit.
                            •	Used in Buddhist Tripitaka, written in Brahmi script.
                            •	The lingua franca of Theravada Buddhism.
                        •	Magadhi Prakrit (Ardha-Magadhi)
                            •	Most important Prakrit language, spoken by Buddha and Mahavira.
                            •	Mauryan dynasty’s court language.
                            •	Ashoka’s Rock Edicts and Jain texts were written in Ardha-Magadhi.
                            •	Evolved into Bengali, Assamese, Odia, Maithili, Bhojpuri, etc.
                        •	Shauraseni
                            •	Used in medieval dramas, also called Dramatic Prakrit.
                            •	Predecessor of Northern Indian languages.
                            •	Jain monks wrote extensively in Shauraseni (Shatkhandgama).
                        •	Maharashtri Prakrit
                            •	Spoken until 9th century AD, precursor of Marathi and Konkani.
                            •	Official language of Satavahana Dynasty.
                            •	Used in dramas like Gaha Kosha and Gaudavaho.
                        •	Elu
                            •	Ancient form of modern Sinhala (Sri Lanka), similar to Pali.
                        •	Paishachi (Bhuta-Bhasa)
                            •	Considered a dead language.
                            •	Brihatkatha by Gunadhya was written in Paishachi.
                    Apabhramsa (6th–7th Century AD Onwards)
                        •	Represents the transition from Middle to Modern Indo-Aryan languages.
                        •	Bhamaha (6th–7th century AD) classified Sanskrit, Prakrit, and Apabhramsa separately.
                        •	Dandin called Apabhramsa the dialect of the common folk.
                        •	Jain monks contributed significantly to its literature.
                        •	Major texts: Mahapurana (Pushpadanta), Bhavisayattakaha (Dhanapala).
                - Modern Indo-Aryan Group (Post-1000 AD)
                    •	Languages include Hindi, Assamese, Bengali, Gujarati, Marathi, Punjabi, Rajasthani, Sindhi, Odia, Urdu, etc.
                    •	Predominantly spoken in northern, western, and eastern India.
        - Dravidian Group of Languages
            •	Spoken in southern India, covering 25% of the population.
            •	Proto-Dravidian evolved into 21 Dravidian languages.
            •	Divided into three groups:
                - Northern Group
                    •	Includes Brahui (Baluchistan), Malto (Bengal, Odisha), Kurukh (Bengal, Odisha, Bihar, Madhya Pradesh).
                - Central Group
                    •	Includes Gondi, Khond, Kui, Manda, Parji, Gadaba, Kolami, Pengo, Naiki, Kuvi, Telugu.
                    •	Only Telugu developed into a civilized language (Andhra Pradesh, Telangana).
                - Southern Group
                    •	Includes Kannada, Tamil, Malayalam, Tulu, Kodagu, Toda, Kota.
                    •	Tamil is the oldest Dravidian language.
                    •	The four major Dravidian languages are:
                        •	Telugu (largest)
                        •	Tamil (oldest, purest)
                        •	Kannada
                        •	Malayalam (youngest, smallest)
            - Difference between Indo-Aryan Group and the Dravidian Group of Languages:
                - The root words in the two language families are different.
                - There is a different grammatical structure in the two groups.
                    - Grammatical structure of Dravidian Family is agglutinative, i.e. the combinations in which root words are united results in little or no change of form or loss of words.
                    - The grammatical structure of Indo-Aryan Group is inflected, i.e. a word’s ending or its spelling changes according to its grammatical function in a sentence.
        - Sino-Tibetan Group
            •	Belongs to the Mongoloid family, spoken in Himalayan regions, North Bihar, North Bengal, Assam, and Northeast India.
            •	Considered older than Indo-Aryan languages, referred to as Kiratas in Sanskrit texts.
            •	Spoken by 0.6% of the Indian population.
                - Tibeto-Burman (Four Subgroups)
                    - Tibetan: Sikkimese, Bhutia, Balti, Sherpa, Lahuli, Ladakhi
                    - Himalayan: Kinnauri, Limbu
                    - North Assam: Abor, Miri, Aka, Dafla, Mishmi
                    - Kuki-Chin-Naga: Kuki, Garo, Bodo, Mikir, Naga, Meitei (spoken in Manipur)
                - Siamese-Chinese
                    •	Ahom belonged to this group but is now extinct in India.
        - Austric (Austro-Asiatic) Group
            •	Includes Munda and Mon-Khmer languages, spoken in Central, Eastern, and Northeastern India.
            •	Existed before Aryan migration, referred to as Nisadas in Sanskrit literature.
            •	Santhali (Jharkhand, Bihar, Bengal) is the most prominent language.
            •	Except for Santhali and Khasi, most Austro-Asiatic languages are endangered.
        - Other Languages
            •	Includes tribal Dravidian languages like Gondi, Oraon, Praji, which do not fit into the major groups.

    Official languages of India
        Constitutional Provisions
            •	Article 343(1) of the Indian Constitution states that “The official language of the Union Government shall be Hindi in Devanagari Script.”
            •	Unless Parliament decided otherwise, English was to cease as an official language 15 years after the Constitution came into effect (i.e., by 26 January 1965).
            •	This implied that over a period of 15 years, Hindi would replace English as the official language, though Parliament had the power to allow English to continue as an official language.
        Protests and the Official Languages Act, 1963
            •	The decision to replace English with Hindi led to nationwide protests, especially by non-Hindi-speaking communities.
            •	In response, the Official Languages Act, 1963 was enacted, which:
                •	Declared Hindi in Devanagari script as the official language of the Union.
                •	Gave English the status of a subsidiary official language.
            •	The Constitution also allows each Indian state to choose its own official language for state-level communication.
        Languages in the Eighth Schedule
            •	The Eighth Schedule of the Constitution lists languages that may be used by states for official purposes.
            •	Initially, 14 languages were included:
                •	Assamese, Hindi, Malayalam, Punjabi, Telugu, Bengali, Kannada, Marathi, Sanskrit, Urdu, Gujarati, Kashmiri, Odia, Tamil.
            •	Later additions:
                •	Sindhi (15th language) – 21st Amendment Act, 1967.
                •	Konkani, Manipuri, Nepali – 71st Amendment Act, 1992.
                •	Bodo, Maithili, Dogri, Santhali – 92nd Amendment Act, 2003.
            •	Total languages in the Eighth Schedule today: 22.
        Additional Information on National and Official Languages
            •	India does not have a national language. Hindi is not the national language, as neither the Constitution nor any Act defines the term ‘national language’.
            •	States can choose their own official language for conducting official functions.
            •	The language adopted by a state does not necessarily have to be from the Eighth Schedule.
            •	Examples of states with non-scheduled official languages:
                •	Tripura – Kokborok (Sino-Tibetan family).
                •	Puducherry – French.
                •	Mizoram – Mizo.
            •	English is not in the list of 22 scheduled languages under the Eighth Schedule.
            •	Arunachal Pradesh and Nagaland are the only two states where English is the sole official language.

    Status of classical language in India
        Introduction
            In 2004, the Government of India introduced the concept of Classical Language status for languages that meet specific criteria.
        Criteria for Classical Language Status
            A language must fulfill the following conditions to be recognized as a Classical Language:
                - High antiquity of early texts or recorded history spanning over 1,500–2,000 years.
                - A significant body of ancient literature or texts considered a valuable heritage by generations of speakers.
                - A unique literary tradition that is not borrowed from another speech community.
                - The classical language and literature must be distinct from modern forms, and there may be a discontinuity between the classical language and its later developments or offshoots.
        Languages Recognized as Classical
            The following languages have been accorded Classical Language status:
                •	Tamil (2004)
                •	Sanskrit (2005)
                •	Kannada (2008)
                •	Telugu (2008)
                •	Malayalam (2013)
                •	Odia (2014)
            Criticism:
                •	The government has been criticized for not including Pali as a Classical Language, despite arguments that it meets all the specified criteria.
            
        Benefits of Classical Language Status
            Once a language is declared Classical, the Government of India provides the following benefits:
                •	Two major international awards annually for scholars of eminence in Classical Indian Languages.
                •	Establishment of a Centre of Excellence for studies in that language.
                •	The University Grants Commission (UGC) is requested to create Professional Chairs for Classical Languages in Central Universities to promote scholarly research.

    National Translation Mission (NTM)
        The National Translation Mission (NTM) is a Government of India initiative aimed at making higher education knowledge texts accessible in Indian languages through translation.
        Objectives of NTM
            •	Disseminate knowledge in all 22 languages listed in the Eighth Schedule of the Constitution through translation.
            •	Train and orient translators to enhance the quality of translated works.
            •	Encourage publishers to publish translations.
            •	Maintain databases of published translations from, into, and between Indian languages.
            •	Act as a clearing house of information on translation to help establish translation as an industry in India.
            •	Facilitate the modernization of languages by developing new terminologies and discourse styles.
            •	Currently, NTM focuses on translating higher education materials into 22 Indian languages.
        Lingua Franca
            A lingua franca (also known as a bridge language, common language, trade language, or vehicular language) is a language systematically used for communication between people who do not share a native language.
            Characteristics and Importance
                •	Lingua franca languages have emerged throughout history for trade, diplomacy, religion, culture, and scientific exchange.
                •	Often, they are distinct from the native languages of the speakers using them.
                •	English is the most widely recognized modern example of a global lingua franca.

    Linguistic Diversity Index (LDI)
        The Linguistic Diversity Index (LDI) measures the probability that two randomly selected people from a given population have different mother tongues.
            •	It ranges from 0 (if everyone has the same mother tongue) to 1 (if every individual has a different mother tongue).
            •	The Index of Linguistic Diversity (ILD) tracks changes in linguistic diversity over time.
                •	A global ILD of 0.8 indicates a 20% loss of linguistic diversity since 1970.
                •	In some regions, ILD ratios above 1 have been recorded.

    Ancient scripts of India
        Introduction
            A script, also known as a writing system or orthography, is a standardized method of representing spoken language through specific marks on a medium (e.g., paper, rocks, birchbark).
        Ancient scripts
            Indus Script
                •	The Indus Script consists of symbols produced by the Indus Valley Civilization.
                •	Most inscriptions are very short, and it remains uncertain whether these symbols form a complete script representing a language.
            Brahmi Script
                •	The oldest known writing system used in the Indian subcontinent and Central Asia, from the final centuries BC to the early centuries AD.
                •	Thought to have been derived from the Semitic Script or the Indus Script.
                •	All surviving Indic scripts in South and Southeast Asia trace their origin to Brahmi.
                •	Famous Brahmi inscriptions include the rock-cut edicts of Emperor Ashoka (250–232 BC).
                •	Deciphered in 1837 by James Prinsep.
                •	Written left to right and classified as an abugida (each unit is based on a consonant with secondary vowel notation).
                Descendant Scripts of Brahmi:
                    - Gupta Script
                        •	Originated during the Gupta Empire for writing Sanskrit.
                        •	Evolved into Nagari, Sarada, and Siddham Scripts, which later led to major modern Indian scripts like Devanagari, Gurmukhi, Assamese, Bengali, and Tibetan.
                    - Vatteluttu Script
                        •	Developed from Tamil-Brahmi.
                        •	One of the three major Tamil writing systems, used for Tamil Script.
                    - Kadamba Script
                        •	The first script dedicated to writing Kannada.
                        •	Developed during the Kadamba Dynasty (4th–6th century AD).
                        •	Evolved into the Kannada-Telugu Script.
                    - Grantha Script
                        •	Used between the 6th and 20th centuries AD in Tamil Nadu and Kerala.
                        •	Used to write Sanskrit and the classical language Manipravalam.
                        •	Malayalam Script evolved from Grantha, along with Tigalari and Sinhala alphabets.
                    - Sarada Script
                        •	Originated around the 8th century AD.
                        •	Used for writing Sanskrit and Kashmiri.
                        •	Now rarely used except for ceremonial purposes by the Kashmiri Pandit community.
                    - Gurmukhi Script
                        •	Derived from Sarada Script and standardized by Guru Angad in the 16th century AD.
                        •	The Guru Granth Sahib (Sikh holy text) is written in Gurmukhi.
                        •	Used mainly for Punjabi language.
                    - Devanagari Script
                        •	A widely used abugida writing system of India and Nepal.
                        •	Written left to right.
                        •	Used for over 120 languages, including Hindi, Marathi, Nepali, Pali, Konkani, Bodo, Sindhi, Maithili, and Sanskrit.
                        •	One of the most widely used scripts in the world.
                    - Modi Script
                        •	Used for writing Marathi until the 20th century, before being replaced by Devanagari.
                        •	Primarily used for official records but also adapted for Urdu, Kannada, Gujarati, Hindi, and Tamil.
            Kharosthi Script
                •	Used between the 3rd century BC – 3rd century AD in Gandhara (present-day Afghanistan and Pakistan).
                •	Used for Gandhari Prakrit and Sanskrit.
                •	A sister script of Brahmi, deciphered by James Prinsep.
                •	Also an abugida, but written right to left (some inscriptions show left-to-right variations).
                •	Contains numerals similar to Roman numerals (e.g., I, X, etc.).
            Urdu Script
                •	Derived from the Persian alphabet, which in turn originates from Arabic script.
                •	Developed around the 13th century AD.
                •	Written right to left.
                •	The extended form of the Urdu script is called Shahmukhi, used for Punjabi and Saraiki in Pakistan.

    Impact of Ancient Indian Scripts
        •	Indian scripts evolved over time, influenced by Buddhism, trade, and foreign invasions.
        •	The spread of Buddhism led to the adoption of Indian scripts in Sri Lanka, Tibet, and Southeast Asia.
        •	The arrival of Islam introduced Perso-Arabic scripts (e.g., Urdu).
        •	Today, globalization and the dominance of English as a lingua franca are influencing the survival and evolution of Indian scripts.


- RELIGION IN INDIA
    Introduction
        •	The Indian subcontinent has a diverse range of religions that shape the morality and ethics of its followers.
        •	Multiple religious communities coexist, contributing to India’s religious diversity.
        •	Swami Vivekananda (1893, World Parliament of Religions, Chicago):
            “I am proud to belong to a religion which has taught the world tolerance and universal acceptance. We believe not only in universal toleration, but we accept all religions as true.”
        •	Spirituality in each religion is reflected in sacred texts and places of worship.
        •	Religion is a powerful tool that can be used to strengthen or divide communal ties.
        •	India has historically experienced more religious harmony than communal tensions.

    Major religions practised in India are:
        Hinduism
            •	Hinduism is one of the largest religions in India, encompassing various sects and traditions.
            •	The term “Hindu” originates from the geographical reference to people living around the Indus River.
            •	Its foundational principles stem from pre-Vedic and Vedic philosophies.
            •	According to Hindu traditions, Kama (pleasure) and Artha (prosperity) are important goals, but ultimately, one must strive for Dharma (righteousness).
            •	The Upanishads describe four life stages:
                •	Brahmachari (celibate student) → Grihasta (householder) → Vanaprastha (hermit) → Sanyasi (ascetic).
                •	The final goal is Moksha (salvation).
            Major Sects of Hinduism
                - Vaishnavism
                    •	Followers worship Vishnu as the Supreme Lord.
                    •	Origins trace back to the 1st millennium BCE, evolving as Bhagavatism or Krishnaism.
                    •	Several Sampradayas (sub-schools) exist within Vaishnavism:
                        •	Varkari Sampradaya
                            •	Devotees of Vithoba (a form of Vishnu).
                            •	Centered around Pandharpur Temple (Maharashtra).
                            •	Notable saints: Jnaneshwar (1275-1296), Namdev (1270-1350), Eknath (1533-1599), Tukaram (1598-1650).
                            •	Rituals include Vari pilgrimage, Ringan, and Dhava.
                        •	Ramanandi Sampradaya
                            •	Founded by Ramananda.
                            •	Worship Rama (incarnation of Vishnu).
                            •	Largest monastic group in Hinduism.
                            •	Two sub-groups: Tyagi and Naga.
                        •	Brahma Sampradaya
                            •	Founded by Madhvacharya.
                            •	Associated with Gaudiya Vaishnavism (Chaitanya Mahaprabhu).
                            •	ISKCON (International Society for Krishna Consciousness) belongs to this tradition.
                        •	Pushti Marg Sampradaya
                            •	Founded by Vallabhacharya (1500 AD).
                            •	Focuses on pure devotion to Krishna.
                        •	Nimbarka Sampradaya
                            •	Also called Hamsa Sampradaya or Kumara Sampradaya.
                            •	Worship Radha-Krishna.
                - Shaivism
                    •	Worships Shiva as the Supreme Lord.
                    •	Traces its origins to the 2nd millennium BCE as Rudra in Vedic texts.
                    •	Major Shaivite traditions:
                        •	Nathpanthi
                            •	Founded by Gorakhnath and Matsyendranath.
                            •	Use Hatha Yoga for spiritual awakening.
                            •	Ascetics wander and cover themselves with ash.
                        •	Lingayatism (Veerashaivism)
                            •	Founded by Basava (12th century AD).
                            •	Rejects Vedas and caste system.
                            •	Worship Shiva as Linga.
                        •	Dashanami Sanyasis
                            •	Followers of Adi Shankaracharya.
                            •	Divided into ten monastic orders.
                        •	Aghoris
                            •	Worship Shiva as Bhairava.
                            •	Perform extreme rituals in cremation grounds.
                            •	Aim to transcend worldly attachments.
                        •	Siddhars
                            •	Tamil mystics practicing alchemy, medicine, and meditation.
                            •	Developed the Varmam martial art.
                            •	Believed to possess eight supernatural powers.
                - Shaktism
                    •	Worships the Goddess (Devi) as Supreme.
                    •	Associated with Tantric practices.
                - Smartism
                    •	Based on Puranic teachings.
                    •	Worships five deities: Shiva, Shakti, Vishnu, Ganesh, and Surya.
                    •	Recognizes both Saguna Brahman (with attributes) and Nirguna Brahman (without attributes).
            - Other Hindu Traditions
                - Shrautism
                    •	Practiced by Nambudiri Brahmins of Kerala.
                    •	Follows Purva-Mimamsa philosophy.
                    •	Focuses on Vedic sacrifices (Yajna).
                - Bhakti Movement (Medieval Period)
                    •	Translated Sanskrit texts into vernacular languages.
                    •	North India: Saints spread Bhakti (devotion).
                    •	South India:
                        •	Vaishnavite Alvars → Devotees of Vishnu.
                        •	Shaivite Nayanars → Devotees of Shiva.
                - Reform Movements (Modern Period)
                    •	Addressed social evils (caste system, Sati, child marriage, etc.).
                    •	Inspired by Western ideals of equality.
                    •	Major movements: Brahmo Samaj, Arya Samaj.
            - Shramana Schools
                •	Parallel to Vedic religion, emphasizing asceticism.
                •	Belong to Nastika (Heterodox) philosophy.
                Major Shramana Traditions:
                    1.	Jainism (discussed in Chapter 14)
                    2.	Buddhism (discussed in Chapter 14)
                    3.	Ajivikas
                    4.	Ajnanas
                    5.	Charvakas (discussed in Chapter 16)
                Ajivikas
                    •	Founded by Makkhali Gosala (5th century BCE).
                    •	Believed in Niyati (Fate) → No free will, everything is preordained.
                    •	Atomistic philosophy: Everything is composed of atoms.
                    •	Rejection of Karma doctrine (unlike Jainism & Buddhism).
                    •	Atheistic but believed in materialistic souls.
                    •	Bindusara (4th century BCE) was a follower.
                    •	Savathi (Uttar Pradesh) was the center of Ajivikas.
                    •	Ashoka’s edicts mention Ajivikas, but the sect is now extinct.
                Ajnanas
                    •	Advocated radical skepticism.
                    •	Believed that knowledge of nature is impossible and useless for salvation.
                    •	Opposed Jainism and Buddhism.
                    •	Considered ignorance as the best approach.
        - Islam
            •	Origins
                •	Founded in the 7th century AD in the Arabian Peninsula.
                •	The word “Islam” means submission to God.
                •	Prophet Muhammad is the last messenger of God.
                •	Islam shares Abraham as a common ancestor with Christianity and Judaism.
            •	Life of Prophet Muhammad
                •	Received revelations from an angel on a mountain.
                •	Initially faced opposition in Mecca.
                •	Migrated to Medina (Hijrah) due to persecution.
                •	Returned to Mecca after gaining followers.
                •	This journey back to Mecca became the basis for Hajj (pilgrimage).
            •	Sacred Texts
                •	Quran – Holy book, revealed to Prophet Muhammad, compiled before his death.
                •	Hadith – Collection of sayings and teachings of the Prophet.
                •	Sunnah – Actions and practices of the Prophet, forming the basis of Sharia (Islamic law).
            •	Five Pillars of Islam
                    1.	Shahada (Faith) – Belief in one God (Allah) and Muhammad as His messenger.
                    2.	Salah (Prayer) – Five daily prayers, with Friday congregational prayer (Juma Namaz).
                    3.	Zakat (Charity) – Giving a portion of one’s earnings to the needy.
                    4.	Sawm (Fasting) – Fasting from sunrise to sunset during Ramzan (Ramadan), ending with Eid.
                    5.	Hajj (Pilgrimage) – Mandatory pilgrimage to Mecca, once in a lifetime.
            •	Major Schools of Islamic Law in India
                •	Hanafi
                •	Shafei
                •	Maliki
                •	Hambali
            •	Major Sects of Islam
                •	Sunni Islam
                    •	Majority sect, follows Sunnah (practices) of the Prophet.
                    •	Believes Prophet’s successor should be chosen from his closest followers (Abu Bakr).
                •	Shia Islam
                    •	Believes leadership should remain within Prophet Muhammad’s bloodline.
                    •	Supports Ali (Prophet’s son-in-law) as the rightful successor.
                    •	Prominent during Muharram, commemorating the martyrdom of Imam Hussain.
            •	Islamic Movements in India
                •	Various movements influenced the evolution of Islam in the Indian subcontinent.
                •	These movements shaped religious and cultural practices over time.
                
            Some of the major movements are:

        - Christianity
            •	Origins & Spread
                •	Founded by Jesus Christ in Jerusalem.
                •	After his crucifixion and resurrection (on the third day), his followers increased.
                •	Became the state religion of the Roman Empire and spread rapidly.
                •	Vatican City became the center of Roman Catholic Christianity.
                •	Reform movements led to the rise of sects like Protestants, Methodists, etc.
            •	Core Beliefs
                •	Belief in one God, the creator of the Universe.
                •	Jesus Christ was a messenger and savior of humanity.
            •	Holy Trinity:
                - God the Father (Creator)
                - God the Son (Jesus Christ)
                - God the Holy Spirit (God’s presence on Earth)
            •	Sacred Text
                •	Bible – Holy book of Christians, divided into:
                        - Old Testament – Derived from Jewish scriptures.
                        - New Testament – Defined by the Roman Catholic Church, headed by the Pope.
            •	Religious Practices & Celebrations
                •	Christmas – Celebrates the birth of Jesus Christ.
                •	Church – Holy place of worship.
                •	Baptism – Ritual marking entry into the Church.
                •	Eucharist – Breaking bread and wine to symbolize unity with God.
            •	Christianity in India
                •	Medieval Period (1st Century AD)
                    •	Some historians believe St. Thomas, an apostle of Jesus, reached India in 52 AD.
                    •	Preached in Kerala and Tamil Nadu, leading to conversions.
                    •	Believed to be buried in Mylapore, Chennai (Cathedral of St. Thomas).
                    •	Portuguese missionaries arrived with their explorers.
                    •	Emperor Akbar and Jahangir allowed Christian missionaries to preach.
                •	18th Century Missionary Work
                    •	British-era missionaries focused on spreading Christianity.
                    •	Promoted modern (English) education and provided medical aid.
                    •	Organizations like YMCA (Young Men’s Christian Association) and YWCA continue missionary work.
                    •	Focus on tribal communities, offering education and healthcare.
            •	Christian Denominations in India
                •	Syrian Christians of Kerala.
                •	Protestant groups.
                •	Several smaller Christian communities actively practicing their faith.

        - Sikhism
            •	Origins & Teachings of Guru Nanak (1469–1539)
                •	Criticized the religious and social order of his time.
                •	Introduced:
                    •	Congregational worship (Sangat) in Dharamsalas.
                    •	Community dining (Langar) to break caste barriers.
                •	Rejected:
                    •	Idol worship and priestly mediation.
                    •	Renunciation as a path to salvation.
                •	Emphasized:
                    •	Salvation through right belief, right worship, and right conduct.
                    •	Honest labor as a spiritual duty (“They who eat the fruit of their labor, Nanak, recognize the right way.”).
            •	Sikh Worship & Practices
                •	Worship places: Gurudwara / Dharamsala.
                •	Kirtan: Singing hymns in praise of God.
                •	Balance between spiritual life and household duties.
                •	No asceticism—an ideal Sikh should live a family life.
            •	Rise of Sikh Militancy
                •	Early Mughal-Sikh Relations
                    •	Initially cordial but worsened after Guru Arjan Dev’s execution (Jahangir’s orders).
                    •	Marked as the “First Martyrdom of the Sikhs” (Khushwant Singh).
                •	Guru Hargobind (1595–1644)
                    •	Organized the first Sikh army at Ramdaspur.
                    •	Introduced the Two Swords Doctrine:
                        •	Piri (Spiritual authority).
                        •	Miri (Temporal power).
                •	Established:
                    •	Akal Takht – Seat of Sikh temporal authority.
                    •	Lohagarh Fort – For Sikh defense.
            •	Later Sikh Gurus & Mughal Conflict
                •	Guru Har Rai & Guru Har Krishan: Detained by Aurangzeb.
                •	Guru Tegh Bahadur:
                    •	Opposed Aurangzeb’s policies.
                    •	Executed in Delhi (1675).
            •	Guru Gobind Singh & Foundation of Khalsa (1699)
                •	Last physical Guru—ended the system of personal Guruship.
                •	Transferred authority to:
                    •	Guru Granth Sahib (Adi Granth) – Sikh holy scripture.
                    •	Guru Panth – The collective Sikh community.
                •	Founded the Khalsa warrior community.
                •	Initiated the first five Sikhs (“Panj Pyare”) into Khalsa.
                •	Declared:
                    •	All baptized Sikh men to take the name “Singh”.
                    •	All baptized Sikh women to take the name “Kaur”.
            •	Khalsa & Sikh Identity
                •	Introduced distinct Sikh identity through dress and symbols.
                •	Five Ks of Khalsa (Panj Kakar):
                    1.	Kesh – Uncut hair.
                    2.	Kangha – Wooden comb.
                    3.	Kara – Iron bracelet.
                    4.	Kirpan – Dagger/sword.
                    5.	Kachcha – Cotton undergarment.
                •	Distinction between:
                    •	Khalsa Sikhs – Baptized warriors of Guru Gobind Singh.
                    •	Non-Khalsa Sikhs (Sahajdhari Sikhs, Nanak-Panthis, Bhallas, Udasis) – Followed Sikh principles but not Khalsa initiation.
        - Zoroastrianism
                •	Origins & Beliefs
                    •	Founded by Prophet Zarathushtra in 7th century BC in Persia.
                    •	Monotheistic religion – Worships Ahura Mazda, the epitome of justice and goodness.
                    •	Eternal struggle between:
                        •	Ahura Mazda (good).
                        •	Angra Mainyu (evil).
                    •	End of times belief: One day, good will triumph over evil.
                •	Zoroastrians in India
                    •	Arrived in India between 8th–10th century AD, fleeing Islamic invasions in Iran.
                    •	Two major communities:
                        •	Parsis – Larger group, settled in Mumbai, Goa, Ahmedabad.
                        •	Iranis – A smaller, more recent group in India.
                    •	One of the smallest and rapidly shrinking communities in India.
                •	Sacred Texts: Zend Avesta
                    •	Written in Old Avestan.
                    •	Consists of 17 sacred songs (Gathas) and Athuna Vairyo (sacred chant).
                    •	Translations & glossaries of these texts are called Zend.
                    •	Divided into five parts:
                        - Yasna – Worship with ceremony and offerings.
                        - Vendidad – Laws against demons and evil spirits.
                        - Yashts – Worship through praise.
                        - Khordeh Avesta – Common prayer book.
                        - Gathas – A part of Yasna, further divided into:
                            •	Ahunavaiti
                            •	Ushtavaiti
                            •	Spenta-Mainyu
                            •	Vohu-Khshathra
                            •	Vahishto-Ishti
                •	Worship & Rituals
                    •	Fire Worship: Fire is sacred, along with earth.
                    •	Temple Worship:
                        •	Temples called Atash Bahram.
                        •	Only eight exist in India (details in Chapter 1).
                    •	Death Rituals:
                        •	Dead bodies are considered corrupting.
                        •	Traditionally placed in Dakhma (Towers of Silence) to be consumed by vultures (Dakhma Nashini).
                        •	Only active Towers of Silence in Mumbai.
                        •	Due to the Indian vulture crisis, some have started burying their dead.                
                - Qissa-i Sanjan
                    It is an account of the migration of Zoroastrians (Parsis) and their settlement in the Indian Sub-continent. 
                    Its first chapter ends with establishment of fire temple at Sanjan (Gujarat).

                There are three major calendar forms among the Parsis:
                ----------------------------------------------Table Start ----------------------------------------------
                    Shahenshahi
                    (majority)

                    Calculate their calendar from the Last Sassanian king,
                    Yazdegerd III.

                    Qadimi

                    Claim to have the oldest calendar

                    Fasli

                    Claimed to be most accurate religious calendar and the
                    most recent one.
                ----------------------------------------------Table End ----------------------------------------------

        •	Judaism
            •	Origins & Beliefs
                •	One of the oldest religions but has faced significant persecution.
                •	Monotheistic – Worships Yahweh, the one true God instituted by Abraham.
                •	Predates Christianity and Islam, which have borrowed from Judaic philosophies.
                •	Day of Judgment belief:
                    •	A Messiah will come.
                    •	The pious will go to heaven.
                    •	The evil will be relegated to hell.
            •	Sacred Texts
                •	Torah – The primary religious text, also the first five books of Tanakh.
                •	Talmud – A compilation of legal, ethical, and historical Jewish writings.
            •	Major Figures in Judaism
                •	Abraham – Ancestor of all Jews, blessed by God for following His injunctions.
                •	Isaac & Jacob (Israel) – Sons of Abraham, continued the covenant with God.
                •	Moses – Received the Ten Commandments (Sefer Torah) at Mount Sinai, guiding how Israelites (descendants of Jacob/Israel) should live.
                •	Jacob’s 12 Sons – Ancestors of the 12 Tribes of Israel.
            •	Religious Practices
                •	Place of Worship: Synagogues.
                •	Religious Services: Eliyahu-hanavi (Thanksgiving to Elijah the Prophet).
                •	Prayer Rituals:
                    •	Male Jews wear a tsisith (prayer shawl) during prayers.
            •	Jewish Communities in India
                •	First Jewish settlers arrived on the Western coast of India.
                •	Though numerically small, India has five major Jewish communities:
                    - Cochin Jews – Malayalam-speaking Jews in Kerala.
                    - Bene Israel – Marathi-speaking Jews (Largest Jewish community in India).
                    - Baghdadi Jews – Traders from West Asia, settled in Surat, now mainly in Mumbai & Kolkata.
                    - Bnei Menashe – Claim descent from the lost Menasseh Tribe:
                        •	Live in Manipur & Mizoram (India-Myanmar border).
                        •	Descendants of Mizo, Kuki, and Chin tribes.
                        •	Believe they were enslaved by Assyrians, escaped to China, and then migrated to India.
                        •	Call themselves “Children of Menasseh.”
                    - Bene Ephraim – Telugu-speaking Jews, converted to Judaism in the 1990s.

        Major religions practised in India
            - Sanamahism
                •	Practiced mainly by the Meitei people of Manipur.
                •	Involves shamanistic worship of:
                    •	Ancestors
                    •	Almighty God
                    •	Sky
                    •	Elements
                    •	Forests
                •	Sacred Text: Puya
                •	Major Festival: Lai Haraoba (worship of traditional deities and ancestors).
                •	Sanamahi: A household deity, generally kept at the southwestern corner of homes.
                •	Note: This religion is not considered a part of Hinduism.
            - Ayyavazhi
                •	Considered a part of Hinduism and followed in Tamil Nadu and Kerala.
                •	Centered on the life and teachings of Ayya Vaikundar.
                •	Sacred Texts:
                    •	Akilathirattu Ammanai
                    •	Arul Nool
                •	Important Observance:
                    •	Ayya Vaikunda Avataram (incarnation day of Vaikundar) is a state holiday in Tamil Nadu and Kerala.
                •	Unique Worship Practice:
                    •	Instead of a deity, worship centers place:
                        •	Flame-shaped copper structure
                        •	Saffron cloth
                        •	Garlands of rudraksha and flowers
                    •	This setup represents Elunetru, signifying a formless God.
            - Sarnaism (Adi Dharam)
                •	Indigenous tribal religion followed by:
                    •	Munda, Ho, Bhumij, Santal, Baiga, and Khuruk communities.
                •	Found in Jharkhand, Odisha, West Bengal, Bihar, Madhya Pradesh, Maharashtra, and Chhattisgarh.
                •	Nature Worship:
                    •	Centered on the worship of trees, especially the Sal Tree.
                •	Place of Worship: Jaher Than (Sarna temples).
            - Bahai Faith
                •	Originated in Persia (19th century).
                •	Founder: Bahaullah, who claimed to be a prophet sent by God.
                •	Core Beliefs:
                    •	Monotheism – Belief in a single, all-powerful God.
                    •	Goal of a unified world order ensuring prosperity for all races, creeds, and classes.
                    •	Three Central Principles:
                        - Unity of God
                        - Unity of Religion
                        - Unity of Humanity
                •	Major Worship Site in India: Lotus Temple (Delhi).
                •	Unique Calendar:
                    •	Bahai Year consists of 19 months, each with 19 days.
                •	Global Presence: Considered one of the fastest-growing religions in the world.
        

- Busshism and Jainism
    Buddhism
        •	Origins:
            •	One of the major world religions, originating in the Indian subcontinent.
            •	Has spread widely across South-East Asia.
            •	Founded on the teachings, traditions, and beliefs of Siddhartha Gautama, who came to be known as Buddha.
        •	Global Presence:
            •	Fourth-largest religion in the world after Christianity, Islam, and Hinduism.
            •	Followed by approximately 7% of the world’s population.
        •	Buddhism in India:
            •	0.7% of India’s population (~8.4 million individuals) identify as Buddhists.
            •	Maharashtra has the largest Buddhist population in India.

        Basics About Buddha
            •	Birth and Early Life:
                •	Born as Siddhartha Gautama in 563 BC at Lumbini (present-day Nepal).
                •	Parents: King Suddhodana (Sakyan Kingdom, Kshatriya Clan) and Queen Maya.
                •	Born on the auspicious day of Vaishakhi Purnima.
                •	Married Princess Yashodhara and had a son named Rahul.
            •	The Great Renunciation:
                •	At 29 years old, he left his palace on his horse Kanthaka, accompanied by Charioteer Channa, to seek the truth of life.
                •	Wandered as an ascetic for six years.
            •	Enlightenment (Nirvana):
                •	At 35 years old, on the same day as his birth, he attained enlightenment under a pipal tree in Bodh Gaya (Bihar).
                •	Became Buddha, the Enlightened One.
            •	First Sermon & Establishment of Sangha:
                •	After attaining Nirvana, he gave his first sermon at Deer Park, Sarnath (near Varanasi).
                •	This event is known as Dharma-Chakra-Pravartana (Turning the Wheel of Law).
                •	His first five disciples (companions at Sarnath):
                    1.	Kaudinya
                    2.	Bhadrika
                    3.	Vashpa
                    4.	Mahanaman
                    5.	Ashvajit
                •	Together, they formed the Sangha (monastic order).
            •	The Three Jewels (Triratna) of Buddhism:
                1.	Buddha – The Enlightened One.
                2.	Dhamma – The Teachings of Buddha (doctrine).
                3.	Sangha – The Monastic Order (established during the first sermon).
            •	Final Years & Mahaparinirvana:
                •	Attained Mahaparinirvana at Kushinagar (Malla Mahajanapada, Uttar Pradesh) at the age of 80 (c. 483 BC).
                •	Contemporary of King Bimbisara (for most of his life) and Ajatashatru (Haryanka Dynasty, last few years).
            •	Titles & Successors:
                •	Also known as Tathagata and Sakyamuni in Buddhist texts.
                •	Predecessor: Kassapa Buddha.
                •	Successor: Maitreya (Future Buddha).
        Early Buddhist Schools
            After the passing of Buddha, the original Sangha is believed to have split into two major early schools between 383 BC and 250 BC:
                - Sthavira Nikaya
                    •	Sub-sects:
                        •	Mahisasaka
                        •	Sarvastivada
                        •	Sankrantika
                        •	Sautrantika
                        •	Dharmaguptaka
                        •	Vatsiputriya
                        •	Dharmottariya
                        •	Bhadrayaniya
                        •	Sannagarika
                        •	Sammitiya
                - Mahasamghika
                    •	Sub-sects:
                        •	Gokulika
                        •	Prajnaptivada
                        •	Bahusrutiya
                        •	Ekavyaharikas
                        •	Caitika
            Other Early Buddhist Sub-sects
                Some sub-sects that did not fall under the two main schools:
                    •	Hemavatika
                    •	Rajagiriya
                    •	Siddhatthaka
                    •	Pubbaseliya
                    •	Aparaseliya
                    •	Apararajagirika
            Later Buddhist Schools
                •	Theravada
                •	Mahayana
                •	Vajrayana

        Concepts and Philosophy in Buddhism
            Buddhism is based on fundamental principles that explain the nature of suffering and the path to liberation.
            The Four Noble Truths
                1.	Dukkha (The Truth of Suffering) – Life is full of suffering.
                2.	Samudaya (The Truth of the Origin of Suffering) – Suffering arises from desires and attachments.
                3.	Nirodha (The Truth of the Cessation of Suffering) – By eliminating desires, suffering can be ended.
                4.	Marga (The Truth of the Path to the Cessation of Suffering) – Liberation can be attained by following the Noble Eightfold Path.
            The Noble Eightfold Path
                1.	Right Speech – Speak truthfully and kindly.
                2.	Right Action – Act honestly and peacefully.
                3.	Right Livelihood – Engage in a profession that does not cause harm.
                4.	Right Effort – Develop self-control and discipline.
                5.	Right Mindfulness – Be aware of thoughts and actions.
                6.	Right Meditation – Concentrate on the true meaning of life.
                7.	Right Thought – Cultivate sincerity and intelligence.
                8.	Right Understanding – Avoid superstition and seek wisdom.
            Middle Path (Madhyam Marg)
                Buddha emphasized the Middle Way, a balanced approach between extreme indulgence and severe asceticism. It serves as the foundation of the Noble Eightfold Path and leads to liberation.
            Buddhism and Other Beliefs
                •	Rejects the Vedas as authoritative scriptures.
                •	Denies the existence of a permanent soul (atman), unlike Jainism.
            Buddhist Councils and Tripitaka
                After Buddha attained Mahaparinirvana at Kushinagar in 483 BC, efforts were made to preserve his teachings. 
                Over 500 years, four Buddhist Councils were held, leading to the compilation of scriptures into three major texts, collectively called the Tripitaka, all written in Pali:
                    - Vinaya Pitaka – Rules for monastic discipline.
                    - Sutta Pitaka – Buddha’s discourses.
                    - Abhidhamma Pitaka – Philosophical and doctrinal analysis.
            Major Schools of Buddhism
                During the Fourth Buddhist Council under King Kanishka, Buddhism split into two major sects:
                    1.	Hinayana Buddhism – Focused on self-discipline and personal enlightenment.
                    2.	Mahayana Buddhism – Emphasized compassion and the worship of Bodhisattvas.
                Later, two additional schools emerged:
                    3. Theravada Buddhism – The oldest surviving form, focusing on strict adherence to Buddha’s original teachings and attaining Nirvana by practicing the Noble Eightfold Path.
                    4. Vajrayana Buddhism – Incorporates esoteric rituals and meditation techniques.
            Practices in Buddhism
                Buddhist practice includes:
                    •	Taking refuge in the Buddha, Dhamma, and Sangha.
                    •	Studying scriptures and following moral precepts.
                    •	Renouncing craving and attachment.
                    •	Practicing meditation and cultivating wisdom, loving-kindness, and compassion.
                    •	Bodhicitta (Mahayana) – The aspiration to attain Buddhahood for the benefit of all beings.
                    •	Generation & Completion Stages (Vajrayana) – Advanced meditation practices.
                Goals of Different Buddhist Schools
                    •	Theravada Buddhism 
                        – Seeks Nirvana, the cessation of suffering and rebirth.
                    •	Mahayana Buddhism 
                        – Aspires for Buddhahood through the Bodhisattva Path, where enlightened beings remain in the cycle of rebirth to help others attain awakening.
